# Changelog

All notable changes to the **Blockingmachine** monorepo are documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.0.0/),
and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html).

---

## [Unreleased]

### Fixed

- **Mobile QR pairing**: the "Scan pairing QR" card could silently do nothing —
  `requestPermission()` was awaited before entering QR mode, so a rejection (or a
  permanently-denied permission, which resolves without a dialog) left the picker
  screen dead with no prompt and no way back. The flow now enters QR mode first,
  surfaces request failures as an error card, and offers an **Open Settings**
  path when Android reports `canAskAgain: false`. `app.json` also declares
  `NSCameraUsageDescription` (iOS) and `CAMERA` (Android) explicitly rather than
  relying on the expo-camera plugin defaults, and drops `RECORD_AUDIO` /
  `NSMicrophoneUsageDescription`, which the scanner never needed.
- **Mobile floating tab bar**: icon+label sat pinned to the pill's top edge —
  the bar reserved `insets.bottom` as internal padding *and* floated the pill
  with margin, double-counting the safe area. The pill now lifts via
  `marginBottom: insets.bottom + spacing` alone, drops the internal inset
  padding, and centers items vertically.
- The regenerated APK (same `1.0.0-rc.10`) replaces the `mobile-v1.0.0-rc.10`
  release asset and the Forgejo `generic/blockingmachine-mobile/1.0.0-rc.10`
  package — reinstall it to pick up the fix.

## [1.0.0-rc.10] - 2026-10-08

### Added

- **macOS builds now notarize and staple as part of `forge make` (flag 54).** The App Store Connect API-key path wired in `forge.config.cjs` (`osxNotarize`) is live — the three `APPLE_API_*` vars sit in the gitignored `.env`, pointing at the App Store Connect key the developer account already uses for store uploads. Fresh artifacts verify `stapler validate` / `spctl -a -vv` → `source=Notarized Developer ID`, so a downloaded copy passes Gatekeeper on first launch with no right-click Open or quarantine strip. The same three secrets are configured on the repo, and `publish.yml` writes the `.p8` contents to a temp path on macOS before `npm run make`, so tag-push builds notarize as well.

### Security

- **`npm audit` reports zero — the three advisories with no patched release are fixed by vendored upstream copies under `vendor/` (flag 52).** `braces` gets a 256-level brace-nesting depth guard (`GHSA-vfj7-8cjw-p6xm`, stack-exhaustion DoS — no upstream fix exists); `node-forge` gets upstream's unmerged PR #1152 nested-`DigestAlgorithm` element-count check (`GHSA-86w9-cpqp-85rv`, RSA PKCS#1 v1.5 signature-forgery bypass of the 1.4.0 fix); `decode-uri-component` becomes a CJS port of upstream 0.5.0's linear-scan decoder (`GHSA-vcc3-ghjq-m6fr` — upstream's fixed release is ESM-only, uncallable from `query-string@7`'s `require()`). The vendor dirs are root `devDependencies` with `file:` specs so npm dedupes all satisfying consumers onto them (`braces@3.0.4`, `node-forge@1.4.1`), plus one nested override rewriting `query-string`'s `decode-uri-component` edge to the vendored `0.5.0`. Fixes verified against the real PoCs: the `{`-×4000 stack-exhaustion pattern now throws a catchable `NestingDepthError`, and the PR #1152 forged e=3 signature is rejected. When upstream ships real patched releases, the vendor dirs and overrides come back out.

### Changed

- **The shipped hot set is now derived from the live-browser URL trace, and the trace parser lives once in core (flag 15).** `hotlist.txt` was regenerated deliberately from `packages/cli/src/__tests__/fixtures/browsing-request-trace.txt` — the re-captured request-URL trace promoted from scratch to a tracked fixture, since the derivation the header names must be replayable on any checkout. The set now ships 91 rules (71 hostname + 10 path-scoped + 10 exceptions) covering all 413 measured blocks of its own session. `parseRequestTrace`/`hostOf` moved to `packages/core/src/ruleReplay.ts` and are re-exported by `CoverageCommand`, so the coverage report, `build-hot-list.mjs`, and the tests read a trace identically — the builder's own parse previously dropped `<TAB>count` multiplicities (under-counting measured blocks as 217) and glued the count onto the URL. The hotlist suites now replay the `Derivation:` header's named input rather than assuming the hostname fixture, and the "every shipped rule fired" check sums the three winner buckets (hostname + URL-decided + scoped).
- **The flag-72 CodeQL hardening merged in from `main` — quadratic regex scans became linear index walks.** A new `packages/core/src/utils/textScan.ts` holds `firstIndexOfAny`/`stripTrailingChars`/`leadingCountSplit`/`trailingCountSplit`, replacing the nineteen `js/polynomial-redos` regex sites in shipped code (`coverage.ts`, `ledgerAggregate.ts`, `tierPlanInput.ts`, `ruleHost.ts`, `ai/domainEvaluator.ts`, the HA add-on's Bearer parse and protection body, and friends); test trees moved out of CodeQL analysis scope via `codeql-config.yml`; three design-intent alerts were dismissed through the API.
- **`release.mjs` pushes the branch HEAD is on, not a hardcoded `main`** — single-branch release lines can ship from the working branch, and a detached HEAD now refuses the tag outright rather than pushing nothing.

## [1.0.0-rc.9] - 2026-10-04

### Added

- **The Home Assistant integration actually connects and controls the hub (`custom_components/blockingmachine`)** — the config flow, coordinator, switch and binary sensor were wired against an API shape the hub never served, so the integration authenticated against nothing and toggled nothing. It now drives the feed server's real endpoints (protection state, compile status, statistics), handles the bearer-token auth the feed server enforces, and the 13-test integration suite exercises the connect-and-control path end to end.
- **The DNS daemon ships inside the packaged app and the tray can start it (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`)** — "Start Local Daemon" resolved `system-daemon/dist` paths that only exist in the monorepo, so the control could never work outside dev and the tray reported "Not Running" for the one state that needed it. The daemon now ships as `systemDaemon.cjs` (webpack entry, asar-unpacked), a Start DNS Protection tray action drives it, `start()` pipes stderr to the app log, and a daemon that never answers its control API reports the port conflict instead of claiming success — a stopped daemon's header says "Daemon not running", not "Paused".
- **"Download extension package" pulls the real release zips (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`)** — the button shelled out to webpack against `../browser-extension` sources that exist only in the monorepo; in the packaged app the only reachable branch was the error. The handler now resolves the release honestly — the tag matching `app.getVersion()` first, the newest asset-carrying release otherwise — downloads the Chrome and Firefox MV3 zips, verifies them against `SHA256SUMS.txt` when published, and unpacks them through staged directories with zip-slip entries refused. Extraction runs through `jszip` (promoted to a real dependency — the aliased extract-zip is a NAPI package that cannot webpack-bundle), and the pane names the folders and the release it saved.
- **Orphaned daemons reload the persisted compiled lists, not the baseline (`@blockingmachine/system-daemon`, `@blockingmachine/electron-app`, flag 71)** — a daemon spawned `detached` outlives the app, but its only reload upstream was the app's feed server, so a quit stranded it on whatever it booted with. The loader now falls back to `FEED_FILE`/`THREATS_FILE` when every HTTP candidate is unreachable — the hub persists `threats.txt` beside the compiled `dns.txt` (one `renderThreatsFeed` shared with the route, refreshed on every quarantine write), both `daemonManager.start()` call sites pass the paths, and the launchd/systemd install scripts carry them, since a service-installed daemon never has the app running. The feed's fallback candidates now derive from the configured feed's origin instead of hardcoding `127.0.0.1:9191` — the same list for the default hub, the right list for a LAN feed.

### Fixed

- **A hardening pass across the hub's lifecycle, feed server, secrets boundary and the DNS daemon (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`, `@blockingmachine/system-daemon`, `@blockingmachine/cli`)**:
  - *Window lifecycle:* `mainWindow` is nulled on `closed` (identity-guarded so an old window's event cannot clear a newer reference), every menu/tray/feed command routes through a `sendToWindow` helper that queues messages while the renderer is absent, recreates the window, and flushes on `renderer-ready` — commands at a closed window no longer crash or drop.
  - *The compile trigger was dead:* menu Cmd+R, tray "Compile & Update" and `/v1/compile` all navigated to the process view without compiling — `autoTriggerCompile` was never set. A consumption-tracked nonce now actually runs the compile, and a remounted view cannot re-fire a stale request.
  - *Rule evaluation cached:* `/v1/check`, `inspect-domain` and the CLI `ServeCommand` recompiled the full ~361k-rule set per request; a `WeakMap` keyed on the rules-array identity now reuses the compiled evaluator until the rules change.
  - *Secrets are write-only across IPC:* `get-sinkhole-config` and `get-feed-token` return presence flags instead of plaintext, all seven secret fields gained Clear/Undo affordances with honest placeholders, blank saves preserve the stored value, and `feedToken` seals through `safeStorage`. The Unbound recipe emits `$FEED_TOKEN` for shell expansion rather than embedding the plaintext in copied commands.
  - *Daemon answers failure honestly:* the DoH forwarder has a 5s timeout and a 128 in-flight cap, a dead upstream answers SERVFAIL rather than silence, blocked non-A/AAAA queries get NXDOMAIN instead of a type-confused sinkhole record, `DnsServer.start()` rejects on bind error instead of hanging, and the control server handles `error` events.
  - *Feed writes are atomic:* every served artifact writes tmp-then-rename, and a stale `hotlist.txt` is removed when its measurement is absent.
  - *Validation:* the feed-server port rejects malformed and privileged values instead of defaulting to 9191; quarantine domains reject rule-syntax smuggling, loopback/reserved names and malformed labels with accepted/rejected counts.
  - *Renderer CSP:* `index.html` carries a meta policy with loopback-pinned `connect-src` — packaged `file://` responses never saw the runtime header, so production had none.
  - *Pi-hole query log speaks v5 and v6:* one shared `fetchPiholeQueryLog` (v5 `?auth=`, v6 session sid) backs the watchdog, live radar and AI scan; the scan limit clamps to 500 and transport errors propagate instead of reading as an empty log.
  - *Workers terminate on settle* — a finished compile/classify no longer leaves threads parked.
- **The compile pipeline no longer freezes the packaged app (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`)** — a cold compile ran ~650k-rule fetches, a 360k-rule dedup and a ~137s classify pass as uninterrupted synchronous main-thread work: the event loop starved, the feed server died, and the app looked force-quit dead. The classify pass now runs in `classifyWorker.cjs` (third webpack entry, asar-unpacked) with progress ticks and an inline fallback; the generation pass moved into `outputWorker.cjs`, which re-parses the joined rules off-thread; dedup/extract loops yield on a cadence; sources parse through chunked `parseFilterListStream`; and the bundled `./filters/...` sources resolve app-first so they work under launchd's cwd — `filters/` now ships via `extraResource`. `autoSchedule`'s "triggering compilation" log line now calls the real pipeline instead of returning. Verified on the packaged build: a cold compile classified 192,284 hosts in ~130s with the feed answering 200 on every probe; a warm compile ends in ~19s.
- **Compile progress stays honest through the post-95% stages (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`)** — every stage after the main save ran under the "Saving to disk... (95%)" label: segregated writes, sort+hash attribution and the whole classify pass. `sendProgress` now names each stage (96–99%, with host counts on classify), the attribution loop yields, and `lastProcessTime` stamps at completion — the tray's "updated" means a compile finished, not that one is in flight.
- **Tray menu labels escape ampersands** — Electron's menu `&` accelerator parsing rendered "Compile  Update Rules Now"; labels are escaped at the menu boundary, pinned by `trayMenu.test.ts`.
- **Publish failures go red (`scripts/publish-*.mjs`, flag 53's codeable half)** — both scripts swallowed every npm failure behind catch-and-exit-0, so a dead token read as a green step. Output is now captured (token scrubbed), already-published is tolerated as idempotent, and a real failure exits 1 — the Forgejo workflow step has no `continue-on-error`, so it fails loudly.
- **`npm run build` builds the daemon's `dist` before the workspace loop** — the packaged app's daemon entry resolves compiled output, which the loop's own ordering did not guarantee.

### Changed

- **The daemon's tests are type-checked like the other workspaces (`@blockingmachine/system-daemon`)** — the package's tsconfig excluded `src/__tests__` and it had no `type-check` script, so the suite driving real sockets had no compile coverage; a `tsconfig.test.json` on the cli/core pattern fixes IDE resolution and puts it in the root `type-check` sweep.
- **The macOS notarize config takes the API-key path too** — `osxNotarize` accepts `APPLE_API_KEY`/`APPLE_API_KEY_ID`/`APPLE_API_KEY_ISSUER` beside the Apple-ID path so CI notarization needs no app-specific password (flag 54 still tracks the credentials themselves).

## [1.0.0-rc.8] - 2026-10-04

### Added

- **The Deploy Hub's recipes are rehearsed on live daemons, not just documentation (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - **Pi-hole actually speaks v6 now — it used to report "triggered" while nothing ran.** The pane advertised "v5 & v6" while the sync and test paths emitted only the v5 `?auth=` query shape; on a v6 daemon those calls land on the admin SPA and return HTTP 200 HTML — a false positive the doc audit could not see and the live rehearsal proved. `piholeApi.ts` detects the flavor from the configured URL: `api.php` keeps the v5 contract (with an honest error when the answer is the SPA instead of JSON), everything else drives v6 — `POST /api/auth` for a session sid, `X-FTL-SID` on the call, `POST /api/action/gravity` for the trigger, and `DELETE /api/auth` afterwards so a sync cannot leak a session into FTL's cap. The version probe uses `GET /api/info/version` so "Connected" means a session worked end to end.
  - **`sinkholeFetch` is hardened for embedded-daemon HTTP.** Every request gets a fresh single-use socket (`agent: false` + `Connection: close`) — FTL's CivetWeb is observed writing body bytes *after* its chunk terminator, which poisons the next request on a pooled connection — plus `insecureHTTPParser` so LAN daemons' framing quirks cannot corrupt responses, `respondOnHeaders` for endpoints like gravity that stream a long-lived log (the trigger no longer holds the sync open for the whole rebuild), and `x-ftl-sid` added to the credential headers a cross-origin redirect must not carry.
  - **Live suites verify the observable effect on the daemon itself.** `privoxyDeployLive.test.ts` (real Privoxy 4.2.0): a project-emitted action file loads via local `actionsfile`, the blocked name returns the block page, an exempted child escapes through last-match-wins ordering to a DNS-failure page, `show-status` lists the file — and a URL in `actionsfile` is proven *fatal to startup*, making the recipe's copy-a-file-first shape load-bearing. `adguardHomeLive.test.ts` (AGH v0.107.71, docker): subscribe → the app's exact `POST /control/filtering/refresh {"whitelist":false}` → the filter live with its rule count → a listed name answers `0.0.0.0` through AGH's own DNS while a clean name resolves; `validateFilterURL` verified live — AGH resolves feed URLs through *its own* upstream DNS, so LAN-named feeds are refused at subscribe time. `technitiumDeployLive.test.ts` (v15.6, docker): `blockListUrls` + `forceUpdateBlockLists` pull the project-formatted hosts feed and a listed name answers NXDOMAIN under its configured blocking mode. `dnsmasqDeployLive.test.ts` (2.93) proves the addn-hosts-vs-confdir SIGHUP asymmetry on one daemon, and `unbound-reachability-live.sh --native` runs the whole verdict matrix on Unbound 1.26.1. Docker-gated suites inspect for the image and skip — never pull — so CI stays green without a daemon host.
- **BIND null-zone exports now delegate allowed subdomains for real (`@blockingmachine/core`, flag 20)**:
  - `exportFormat` groups `subdomainExceptions` by parent, resolves the child's authoritative NS records (injectable `resolveNs`), emits `db.bm.null.<parent>` zone files carrying `child IN NS` delegations, and marks the line `EXCEPTION DELEGATED` — an allowed subdomain under a sinkholed zone now escapes via a genuine DNS referral instead of staying unhonoured. Authority that will not resolve fails closed to `NOT HONOURED` rather than emitting a delegation to nothing; the synchronous `generateFilterList` path stays honest about not delegating. Proven on live `named`: a delegated child returns a real referral while siblings stay NXDOMAIN.
- **The hub serves the measured hot set (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - `hotlist.txt` was allowlisted for serving but nothing ever wrote it — every consumer got a silent 404. The compile handler now derives it from the picked ledger through the same core pipeline the CLI uses (`parseHitLedgerText` → `selectHotList` → `formatHotList`), and deletes a stale file when the ledger is absent, unreadable or empty — an old file pretending a measurement exists is worse than none.
- **The corpus gate refuses first-party brand tokens (`@blockingmachine/core`, flag 41)**:
  - A bare-label brand (`tiktok`, `walmart`, `westpac`…) could pass admission whenever its apex was listed — the residual the attestation could not see. `FIRST_PARTY_BRAND_LABELS` refuse them with a `brandRefusals` provenance counter; measured on the compiled-tier dry-run, accepted tokens fell 8,107 → 4,157 and every flag-named brand appears in `rejections` while the corpus gate stays 0/122.
- **Forgejo Actions gained a mirrored weekly ledger cut (`.forgejo/workflows/weekly-tier-compile.yml`)** — same Monday 04:17 UTC cron plus `workflow_dispatch`, PR'ing through the pulls API on the injected `GITHUB_TOKEN` (no new actions or secrets).

### Fixed

- **`registrableDomainOf` no longer installs a shared-hosting apex (`@blockingmachine/browser-extension`, flag 51)** — it delegates to the classifier's own `registrableZone` decomposition, so "block this domain" on `evil.blogspot.com`, `user.github.io` or `app.pythonanywhere.com` scopes to the served site, never the platform apex.
- **Forgejo CI runs the build before the suites** — the same ordering bug GitHub's workflow had; it proved itself immediately when run 310 caught a webpack resolution failure `tsc --noEmit` cannot see: `.js`-suffixed imports now resolve through `extensionAlias` in both main and renderer configs. Run 312 is the first green Forgejo CI on the corrected ordering.
- **The Unbound live rig compiles its own dependencies** — `deployRefresh` was never in the harness module list and `argv.mjs` was never copied beside `harness.mjs`; the rig now runs its 10/10 live suite on a real resolver, with the refresh row's honest `off` tolerated where no cron has reported yet.
- **GitHub's weekly workflow names its PR step** — `id: pull-request` was missing, so the no-changes report read an output that could never resolve and would have fired unconditionally.
- **The rc.7 release notes carry the published checksums** — the notes hashed a local build, but `--clobber` had replaced most assets with CI builds; every published asset is now verified by download and hash.

### Changed

- **Electron Forge 7.11.2 → 8.0.1 (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`)** — the deliberate major the `braces` flag named: `npm audit` findings dropped 9 → 6. The upgrade absorbed Forge 8's real breaking surface: `main` moved to `.webpack/main/index.cjs`, `afterPrune` uses the single-object async hook signature, the main bundle emits `[name].cjs`, the runtime preload fallback is `preload.cjs`, and the stale root `junk@^3` override is gone (`@electron/packager@20` is ESM and requires `junk@^4`). The remaining findings are one unpatched advisory (`GHSA-vfj7-8cjw-p6xm`, `first_patched_version: null`) reached only through `webpack-dev-server` — dev-toolchain exposure, nothing shipped.
- Packaging verified end-to-end on the new major: `forge package` stages `index.cjs`/`preload.cjs`/vendor chunks into the asar correctly, `forge make` produces the darwin DMG and zip, and the packaged app boots with its feed answering 200.

## [1.0.0-rc.7] - 2026-10-03

### Added
- **The Deploy Hub Gains A Browser Tab: Build, Download And Load The Extension From The App (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - **"Install the extension" had no surface anywhere in the app.** The Deploy Hub offered every downstream consumer — resolvers, sinkholes, subscriptions — but the extension itself, the install that lives inside the page, could only be built by hand and loaded from memory. A new first tab in the registry carries a **Download extension package…** button: the main process rebuilds `packages/browser-extension` from source on every click (a copy of whatever `dist/` last held would silently ship old blocking code), asks where to save through a directory dialog, and writes a clean `blockingmachine-extension/` folder there — a clean rewrite rather than a merge, so removed files cannot linger beside the new rules.
  - **The browser keeps the install click, and the pane says so.** `Load unpacked` and `about:debugging` are buttons no app may press for the user, so the pane lists them: Chromium's `chrome://extensions` developer-mode path, Firefox's temporary add-on path with its restart caveat, and the feed URL + token the popup needs pointed at this hub. The saved path is copyable once the write lands; a cancelled dialog, a missing extension tree and a failed build each report their own state rather than the same generic error.
  - **The pane doubles as the Hub's index.** "Everywhere else it installs" renders the sibling targets as links into the tab switcher — Home Assistant, Pi-hole, the daemon and the rest — narrowed by the Hub (the pane cannot import the registry that imports it) and routed through the tab-selection handler, now guarded by `isDeployTargetId` since it accepts a plain string.
  - Click specs cover both button sets in `deployPaneClicks.test.ts` (download, copy-path, and the sibling links' `onSelectTarget` payloads), the recipe-directive checker declares the pane's only code spans — the `chrome:`/`about:`/`http:` schemes it prints — and the registry suite pins the new tab order with `browser-extension` first, as the landing tab.
- **The Hot-Set Derivation Accepts A Deployment's Own Session Exports (`scripts`, `@blockingmachine/core`)**:
  - **The per-deployment trace was the one input the derivation could not read.** `build-hot-list.mjs` took a merged `<count> <rule>` ledger (`--hits`) or a request trace (`--trace`), but what a hub's own users can hand it is the popup's `blockingmachine-hit-ledger-*.json` exports — producing them meant a second script and a scratch file before the build could start. `--sessions <path>` now accepts the exports directly: a file, or a directory whose `*.json` expand in sorted order, merged inline through the same `readBrowserLedgerSessions`/`mergeBrowserLedger` core functions `ledger:merge` drives — not a second reading of the format.
  - **A `--sessions` build is `node scripts/build-hot-list.mjs --list <the served list> --sessions <export dir> --out <serve dir>/hotlist.txt`** — one command from "my users exported their ledgers" to a hotlist the hub serves beside its list, derived from its own users rather than the repository's scripted sessions. The `Derivation:` header records the sessions paths, so `--check` replays *those* inputs — a deployment's file never pretends the repo ledger produced it.
  - **Ambiguous measurements are refused, not ranked.** Naming two of `--hits`/`--trace`/`--sessions` exits 2 — `--hits` used to win silently over `--trace`, which would have told a deployment its exports were measured while a different file did the measuring. Repeating `--hits` or `--trace` reads as "merge these" and is refused with the merge script's name; only `--sessions` is legitimately plural.
  - Tests: an end-to-end case derives a hot list from a directory of session exports, proves the inline merge selects the same rules the `ledger:merge` file produces, and replays the recorded `--sessions` provenance under `--check`; refusals for mixed inputs, a repeated ledger flag, a missing path, and a non-JSON file; `parseHotListDerivation` round-trips plural `--sessions` values.
- **The Popup Names Which List The Browser Is Running — Full Export Or Measured Hot Set (`@blockingmachine/browser-extension`)**:
  - **A rule count cannot answer the question.** The hot set is what essentially every browser installs (the export overflows every MV3 budget), and until now the only surface that said so was the worker's log: a user on 17 measured rules and a user on a 30,000-rule pruned export both saw a number that fit the quota. `updateDynamicRules` now reports the `RuleSourcePlan` it just applied through an `onRuleSource` callback, after the browser's `updateDynamicRules` resolves — so a rejected batch can never leave the popup claiming a source that was never installed.
  - **The record survives the worker.** `AppliedRuleSource` — which source, when, how many installed, and how many the full export would have pruned — is persisted under `bm_rule_source` on every apply and read back inside `GET_MV3_STATUS`, so a service-worker restart does not turn "measured hot set" into "unknown". Nothing outside the extension can change its dynamic rules, so the record stays true until the next apply replaces it.
  - **The same check now catches a stale hot set.** Whenever a hot set is supplied, `chooseRuleSource` also measures it against the served export — its own budget standing and the count of rules the export no longer carries verbatim (the builder only ships rules the source list contains, so absent rules mean the pair is out of step). Staleness warns whichever source won — a full-export answer today does not fix the set that will be asked for when the list outgrows the budget — and the row shows `· N stale`.
  - **The quota card carries the answer as a `Rule source` row**: `full export` or `measured hot set` (warn-toned), with the hot set's hover text quoting the pruned count it stands in for — the number that says how much narrower measured protection is than the complete list. Fresh install or no application yet renders no row rather than guessing "full".
  - 18 new tests: the report fires on hot and full applies with the plan's own numbers, stays silent on a rejected apply, on a global pause (clearing the rules installs no source) and on a preserving apply (the kept rules still belong to the source that installed them); the standing is measured on the happy path too; staleness warns even when the full export won and stays quiet on a carried set; the row renders both labels, the stale count, and the absence of a false stale claim; and the wiring is pinned — the apply path persists through the callback and `GET_MV3_STATUS` serves the stored record beside the browser's live quota.
- **A Full Export With No Hot Set Is Trimmed By Measured Tier Benefit, Not A Prefix (`@blockingmachine/browser-extension`)**:
  - **The fallback used to keep whatever came first.** A list that overflows with no measured set to hand was cut in merge order — a statement about how the file was built, not about anyone's traffic. `planListRules` now takes a `TierBenefitRank` ordering — a host's rank is its position inside the shipped tier files, weighted across tiers by the deployment's own `bm_tier_hits` tally (`TierBenefitIndex`, built once per worker from the packaged `rules/tier_*.json`) — and `chooseRuleSource` applies it to the full export whenever pruning has to happen: no hot set served, or a hot set that overflows too.
  - **A deployment with no measurement still beats a prefix.** With no local tally the rank is the compile's own order — curated seeds first, then evidence rank — because the tier files are themselves a measured artifact the package already carries. A ranking that places none of the list's hosts is reported honestly: `tierTrimmed` stays false and the reason says `pruned`, so the log never claims a measurement that had nothing to say.
  - **The trim orders the install, not just the decision.** The same rank feeds the `ownedFilters` plan and the installed plan — a cut decided one way and installed another would retire rules the ranking wanted kept — and the overflow warning, the persisted `AppliedRuleSource` (`tierTrimmed`), and the `Rule source` row's hover text all name it.
  - 15 new tests: the index walks ancestors and reorders tiers by hits; the planner keeps a last-in-file tiered host a prefix would drop, reports a no-answer ranking as a plain prune, and never marks a fitting export or a winning hot set as trimmed; the apply path installs the ranked cut, records the flag, and names the ordering in the warning; the wiring is pinned.
- **The Shipped List's Path-Rule Decidability Is Now Gated At 80% (`@blockingmachine/core`, `scripts`, CI)**:
  - **The path bucket is only *potentially* URL-decidable.** `blockingRuleScope` counts `||host/path$script` as path-scoped because its pattern needs the URL, but its `$script` modifier names a request type no URL carries — so a list update can flood the bucket with rules a URL trace cannot settle while every replay test stays green. `pathRuleDecidability` (core) measures the share directly: `isUrlDecidableRule` over the path-scoped population, nothing else counted.
  - **`npm run check:path-decidability` fails below 80%.** The shipped list measures 6,230 of 7,628 — 81.7%, the figure the module note already quoted. The 1,398-rule residue is measured, not assumed: mostly request-typed paths (`$script`, `$image`, `$popup`…), `$replace=`/`$redirect=` rewrites, and a few shapes no host anchor survives. A stricter floor is `--min`, and a list with zero path rules fails rather than passing vacuously on a share of nothing.
  - **The floor exists because the residue has a growth direction.** Flag 18's separator fix will move this number, and every weekly list pull moves it accidentally — the gate is the instrument that re-measurement reports through. CI runs it in the generated-artifacts step after the build; the refusal contract applies to its flags.
- **Ad And Tracker Are No Longer Unmeasurable In Holdout: Two Contested Cases Contest One Class Each (`@blockingmachine/core`)**:
  - **Open flag 10 was the hole in the measurement, not the model.** The fitted head's held-out win was real but lived entirely in `Content`: Ad and Tracker were 1.0 for *both* heads, so 26 of the 75 held-out cases were evidence of nothing — a fit worse in those classes would have scored exactly the same. The fix was cases that sit between classes the way the third-party embeds do, placed in the holdout by the sorted-label stride rather than by hand.
  - **`hidden-third-party-frame`** is a `display:none` 0×0 cross-origin iframe — the shape the harvest queue surfaced from a real page — labelled `Tracker`: the hand-tuned `thirdPartyFrame` weight reads it Tracker and the fitted head's weakened one reads Content. **`offsite-creative-img`** is a banner-sized image on an ad-delivery host with no ad word anywhere — what image-based ad delivery looks like without vendor markup — labelled `Ad` on the same split. Each class now has exactly one contested held-out case, and both contests go the hand-tuned table's way: the comparison records them as named regressions rather than an anonymous count.
  - **The misses are pinned by name, not absorbed into bounds.** `TRACKER_BLIND_SPOTS` grew to seven (the frame case is an evidence gap, not flag 9's first-party-measurement family, and flag 9's verify now says so), `AD_READ_AS_CONTENT` opened at one, `SOFT_AD_MISSES` names the five permitted Ad→Content readings, and Ad recall's bound is derived from named lists exactly like Tracker's. Held-out misses went 3→6 for *both* heads — the views still agree on where the model is blind. Closes open flag 10.
- **The Harvest Queue Gains A `promote` Step: A Reviewed Candidate Becomes A Corpus Case Without Hand-Editing (`@blockingmachine/core`, `scripts`)**:
  - **Promotion was the last hand-edited step in the harvest pipeline.** `npm run harvest:elements -- --promote <candidate-id>` now renders the corpus case a reviewed candidate promotes to, and `--write` appends it under a `// ─── Harvested promotions` marker at the tail of `ELEMENT_EVAL_CORPUS` — the only place a generated case lands. The renderer (`renderElementEvalCaseSource`) emits the corpus file's own `snap()`/`attr()` literal style, and the inserter (`insertPromotedCaseSource`) finds the marker only as a comment line, so the constant that names it cannot fool the search into writing outside the array.
  - **Every refusal is a question the reviewer did not answer.** `planHarvestPromotion` — pure, so the whole contract is pinned in the unit suite — refuses a candidate with no human decision, requires an explicit `element`/`shape` scope (recorded on the decision or passed as `--scope`, and a flag that disagrees with the record refuses rather than rewriting it), makes a `hide` decision name its class since the proposal's expected set is a placeholder, refuses `--class` on a `keep` whose decision already fixed Content, and refuses labels that exist or are not corpus slugs.
  - **The scope asserted at promotion is written back onto the harvest record**, so the JSONL states what the promote claimed and the queue's proposals stay consistent with the case it produced. A second `--promote` of the same label refuses on the corpus file's own text — the compiled-corpus coverage check cannot see a case appended since the last build.
  - 12 new tests: every refusal path, scope resolution both directions, class narrowing, render escaping/omission, marker insertion position, and the corpus-purity invariant restated as what it now is — a `family: 'harvest'` case must carry `harvestScope`, the recorded-scope provenance the planner insists on before it renders anything.
- **The Popup Reports Whether The Browser Is Enforcing Your Site Choices, Not Just What You Saved (`@blockingmachine/browser-extension`)**:
  - **The shield status could describe a pause that was not pausing.** `deriveShieldStatus` reads decisions out of storage, so a paused site whose `updateDynamicRules` call died — a quota rejection, a torn-down worker, a browser mid-update — still rendered *Paused*. The tiers already had this covered by `RulesetDrift`; the dynamic rules, where the user's own decisions actually live, had only a silent repair on worker start. `SiteControlView` now carries a `drift` field composed from `dnr.installedFamilies()` on every response the popup renders — `GET_SITE_CONTROL` and every toggle answer alike — so the browser's own state is what the panel reports.
  - **The drift names each direction by kind** (`computeSiteControlDrift` in `dnrManager`): pauses and allowances with no rule installed (enforcement the user thinks they have), pauses and allowances installed that no saved decision asks for (enforcement they did not make), saved custom rules with no filter installed, foreign user-family rules, and — while paused everywhere — the count of blocklist rules a failed pause left blocking, which is the one promise "pause everywhere" cannot break silently. Custom rules are compared by filter, the same semantics `customRulesAreInstalled` keeps, so a rule the planner merged is not falsely reported missing.
  - **The notice mirrors the tier one**: `SiteControlDriftNotice` renders beside the shield status under the same show-don't-auto-repair rule — reconciling on read would erase the disagreement before anyone could see it. Its button sends `RECONCILE_SITE_CONTROL`, which repairs through `reconcileDynamicRules` and answers with a view read *after* the repair, so a repair that did not take keeps the notice up. An unreadable browser renders *unknown*, never *in sync*; before the first view arrives the notice renders nothing.
  - 24 new tests: the comparison (including the vacuous cases — a refused line and a paused custom rule), each render direction, and the wiring pins that `siteControlView` composes the browser read and the reconcile reads the view after the repair. Closes open flag 1.
- **CI Now Rehearses A Release Against Fixtures And Opens The Shipped Archives (`scripts`, CI)**:
  - **Packaging breaks used to be release-time discoveries.** The CI job built, tested and checked generated artifacts, but nothing ran the two steps a release actually runs — the tier compile and `package-extension.mjs` — and nothing that ran them looked inside the archives they produced. `npm run rehearse:release` (`scripts/release-rehearsal.mjs`) now does all of it: a committed fixture blocklist (`scripts/fixtures/release-rehearsal-blocklist.txt`) is compiled into the real tier files, packaging runs the full release path — internal compile, webpack bundle, MV3 gate, both zips — and then the archives are opened and asserted.
  - **The fixture's placements are assertions, not statistics.** Every zone is `*.rehearsal.example` — a name no real blocklist carries — so finding `||ads.rehearsal.example^` inside the shipped `tier_ads.json` proves the fixture reached the artifact. One host lands per vocabulary tier plus the compiled-only `tier_unclassified`, and every refused line shape (exception, cosmetic rule, loopback, bare IP, single label, public suffix, wildcard, DNS rewrite) is asserted absent from every tier file.
  - **The archive assertions check what an install would read.** Both zips must contain `manifest.json` parsing to MV3, every ruleset the manifest's `rule_resources` declares present and parseable, curated tiers never empty, the service worker and popup page aboard, no `.DS_Store`/`__MACOSX` debris, and the Firefox archive stamped with the gecko extension id. Compiled-only tiers may ship empty — `tier_security` and `tier_unclassified` are rebuilt from inputs at packaging, not re-seeded — so their fixture hosts are pinned at the compile step rather than in the archive, which is what a real release does.
  - **The rehearsal leaves the tree as it found it.** Tier files and `tierCounts.generated.ts` are snapshotted byte-for-byte before the compile and restored in a `finally`, pass or fail — a rehearsal can never commit a compile by accident. CI runs it as a step after the artifact checks, and 9 new tests pin the wiring: the `rehearse:release` script name, the no-`--skip-tiers` real path, snapshot-before-compile and finally-restore ordering, fixture/assertion agreement, the unzip-based archive checks, and argument refusal before any filesystem touch.
- **The Hub's Malware-Verdict Pass Is Incremental: A Recompile Re-Classifies Only What Changed (`@blockingmachine/core`, `@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - **The slowest part of a compilation was also its most repetitive.** The pass that turns the deduplicated blocklist into `tier_security`'s `malware.txt` runs `classify()` on every candidate host — ~162s on the real ~190k list — and a weekly pull changes thousands of hosts, not the whole file. Verdicts are now persisted to `userData/mini-ai-verdicts.json` as a `host → category` record, and a host the *same* classifier already answered for is served from the record; the log reports the split (`N served from the current verdict cache, M classified in Ts`).
  - **A cached verdict is only reused when the classifier that would answer today is the one that answered then.** The record's fingerprint hashes the three things that can change an answer between compilations: the model weights, the live reputation vocabulary — so a DB hot patch invalidates without any code shipping — and the user's feedback tunings, so a whitelisted host never keeps serving the verdict that made the user correct it. A file that fails to parse, predates the format, or names a category that does not exist is a cache miss, never a wrong answer.
  - **The new core module is dependency-free and deterministic** (`ai/verdictCache.ts`, hashed with the same pure-TS `sha256Hex` the learned-model loader uses, so it runs anywhere core does): lists are canonicalised before hashing, output keys are sorted, and write-back is pruned to the current candidate set so hosts that fell off the list cannot accumulate. What the fingerprint cannot see — the feature-extraction *code* — is the stated manual half: `VERDICT_CACHE_FORMAT` bumps it (open flag 39).
  - 11 new tests: fingerprint determinism and sensitivity to vocabulary, feedback, and list reordering; serialisation determinism across record/Map construction order; refusal of foreign formats and malformed files; per-entry category validation; prototype-pollution safety in `verdictCache.test.ts`; and the call-site ordering — read, gate, classify misses only, write back after `malware.txt` — pinned in `verdictCacheWiring.test.ts` by reading `index.ts`, the codebase's convention for the un-importable main process.
- **The Domain Classifier Gains A `Consent/Annoyance` Category, And `tier_annoyances` Is Measured Against It (`@blockingmachine/core`, `@blockingmachine/browser-extension`, `@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - **The annoyance tier had no word the model could say.** Twenty-one of its 22 pinned hosts are consent-management platforms, notification-prompt networks and popup builders — a real family of nuisance infrastructure that previously had to be filed as `Telemetry/Analytics` or `Advertising` because those were the only blocking families that existed. `Consent/Annoyance` is now a first-class `ThreatCategory` with its own feature (`consentKeywordWeight`), model weight row, evidence vocabulary (`suspiciousConsentTokens`, hand-written plus tier-derived), normalisation, endpoint-guard veto, corroboration path, and `AiDetectorService` verdict mapping (`suspicious`, the middle rung — an annoyance is neither clean nor an ad server).
  - **The derivation grew a third axis.** `tier_annoyances`' contract is now `['Consent/Annoyance']` — consent candidates are tried for its seeds and only consent candidates, so a tracker sitting in the tier records a disagreement rather than being laundered into a consent filing. The independent corpus gate is unchanged: 20 consent vendor tokens earned their place (`cookiebot`, `onetrust`, `iubenda`, `termly`, `wisepops`, …), moving corpus accuracy to 99.1% and macro-F1 to 0.901 with zero clean-domain false positives and two honest rejections — `privy.com`, whose only label is an ordinary English word no derivation may mint, and `onesignal.com`, which stays `Telemetry/Analytics` because the hand-written tracker claim on `onesignal` predates and outranks the consent axis.
  - **The agreement suite's rationale for shipping the tier disabled is rewritten as what it is: a product decision.** `tier_annoyances` carries consent prompts and notification flows that sites legitimately need to show some users; default-off was never evidence the model lacked the word, and now the suite says so — 20 of 22 hosts pinned to `Consent/Annoyance`, the two exceptions named and their mechanisms documented. The suite also gained the missing piece of its pin semantics: a *documented re-filing* (a pinned disagreement whose verdict is a stricter family, verified family-for-family against the derivation's own record) is distinct from an unexplained contradiction, which still fails the suite.
  - **`rules/tier_unclassified.json` ships as `[]`, matching `tier_security`.** The manifest declared the ruleset but the file was never committed, so a clean checkout pointed at nothing and a compiled leftover poisoned the suite's measurement; the empty file is the honest curated state the tier's own documentation already claimed.
  - 8 new corpus cases carry the consent family (CMPs, push-prompt vendors, an `iubenda` delivery endpoint); the eval harness scores `Consent/Annoyance` as a class rather than a note in the confusion matrix; and 18 new tests cover the consent-axis derivation ordering, the three-list vocabulary merge, re-filed disagreement pinning, and the rewritten agreement rationale.
- **The Popup Reports Tier Redundancy Against The Live Dynamic Rules (`@blockingmachine/core`, `@blockingmachine/browser-extension`)**:
  - **The in-browser surface could not answer the question the hub and CLI already print.** `tier plan --synced` and the hub's tier-plan card diff the shipped tiers against the synced list's *source text*; the popup had per-tier usage and a capacity plan but no way to say "these static rules are already covered". A new `GET_TIER_REDUNDANCY` message answers the same question against the artifact one step further along — the dynamic rules the browser is actually holding — so rules scoped, dropped or superseded between compile and install are counted by what is installed, not by what was sent.
  - **`readDynamicRuleHosts` is `readSyncedHosts` for installed rules rather than list text**, added to `@blockingmachine/core` behind a `./tier-plan` export so all three surfaces share the one diff. The semantics are identical — block rules fill the block set, allows fill the exception set, and a rule scoped below a whole domain is skipped rather than credited — but the refusals had to be re-expressed for the DNR shape: a `urlFilter` is a substring match, so only the exact `||host`/`||host^` a host block compiles to names a host (a `||a.com/path` filter or a bare `a.com` substring blocks strictly less); `initiatorDomains`, tab, request-method, domain-type and response-header conditions refuse the way `$domain=`/`$script` modifiers refuse; `allowAllRequests` is skipped outright because it names the page whose subrequests are freed, not a request target; and `requestDomains` standing alone is read as the multi-host spelling of the same claim.
  - **The resource-type baseline is passed explicitly.** The compiler stamps `BLOCK_RESOURCE_TYPES` on every plain block it installs, so that set's presence is the list's spelling of "unconditional" rather than a scope — but a rule holding *fewer* of those types is the scoped one, and is refused exactly as `$script` is on the text side. With no baseline supplied the reader treats any `resourceTypes` as narrowing, matching the text reader's refusal of every `$` modifier.
  - **The states stay distinct in the popup.** "The browser would not list its rules" (`synced: null`) renders as unknown, never as a zero finding; an installed set with no whole-domain blocks says every tier stands alone; a finding names the tiers and carries the caveat in the same breath — duplicated coverage is not absent coverage, and the line sits beside a *Turn off* button that would otherwise read as endorsed. Per row, a tier shows `N of M rules already blocked dynamically`, or `Adds nothing beyond the live dynamic rules` when the tier is entirely covered. The whole path is read-only: opening the popup asks a question and reconciles nothing.
  - 17 new tests: the structured reader's block/allow split, strict host-shape gate, per-condition refusals, the resource-type baseline, multi-host `requestDomains`, and the end-to-end ancestor/exception agreement with `findTierRedundancy` in `tierPlanInput.test.ts`; the report's complete/partial verdicts, exception handling, read-failure and nothing-installed states, and serialisation determinism in `tierRedundancy.test.ts`; and the notice's four renderings in `tierRedundancyNotice.test.ts`.
- **The Hub's Attribution Manifest Now Records Which Sources Produced The Compilation (`@blockingmachine/core`, `@blockingmachine/electron-app`, `scripts`)**:
  - **The manifest could say what a compilation produced but not what it was built from.** `categories/manifest.json` named the categories, their host counts and the contested claims — and nothing about the lists that fed it, because `buildCategoryAttribution` was handed category buckets and the source identity had already been discarded into them. The same list configured differently in two hubs would produce byte-identical manifests, which is the wrong determinism for a provenance record.
  - **`AttributedSource` now carries the source's identity — `name`, `url`, and `error` — and the category resolution moved into the builder with it.** A rule's own `metadata.sourceInfo.category` still wins over the list's declared category, exactly as the call site already resolved it; what changed is that the resolution happens where the record is kept, so each source's record carries the categories its rules *actually* resolved to rather than the filing the config claimed.
  - **A fetch that failed is recorded as attempted, not dropped.** The entry carries its `error`, zero hosts and no categories — the difference between "nine sources produced this" and "nine were configured" is exactly the sentence a thin category needs next to it, and a dropped failure would leave a reader blaming the lists for a hole the network made.
  - **The compiler reads the record and the bundle carries it.** `report.attribution.sources` passes the entries through to `--json`, the human output names what it was built from (`built from EasyList, EasyPrivacy · 1 attempted and failed: …`), and `GENERATED_TIER_SOURCE` gains a `sources` array so a packaged build can answer the question without the hub's `categories/` directory beside it. Manifests written before the field existed read as `[]` — unrecorded, not empty — and a consumer that only wants the category geometry ignores the field the way it did before.
  - 10 new tests: the record's shape, per-rule filing winning over the list's, failed and anonymous sources, deduplication of a source listed twice, and manifest determinism in `ruleHost.test.ts`; the report round-trip, the pre-field manifest reading `[]`, and the generated provenance file in `tierCompiler.test.ts`; and the hub's call site pinned against regressing to per-category buckets in `compilationAttribution.test.ts`, which reads `index.ts` the way `exportFormatDeployCoverage` does because the file starts Electron when imported.
- **The Hit Ledger Export Carries The Per-Tier Tally, So A Measured Plan Can Be Reproduced And Reviewed Outside The Popup (`@blockingmachine/core`, `@blockingmachine/browser-extension`, `scripts`)**:
  - **The number a plan is weighted by was the one thing the export did not carry.** Sessions recorded `<count> <rule>` and nothing else, so the per-tier split — the figure `planTierBenefits` weighs a tier by — existed only inside the popup that measured it, and a plan computed from an export could not be checked against the evidence that produced it. Export format **version 2** adds a `tiers` array and a `tierUnattributed` count to each session. A version 1 file remains valid input — it is simply a session with no split — which is what makes a missing split a visible hole rather than a silent zero.
  - **It is a second axis, not a derivation of the first.** A tier is credited whenever the browser names the winning ruleset — *even when the rule file could not be read to name the filter* — and one filter shipped in two tiers is one entry in `hits` and two in `tiers`. Blocks that named no tier (the synced list is not a ruleset) are counted as `tierUnattributed`, so the two axes add up to the session's blocks and a reader can check the split instead of trusting it. Two refusals are pinned by tests: an `@@` rule credits no tier because it *allowed* a request, and a tier id the catalogue does not know lands in the unattributed count rather than becoming a row that looks weighed.
  - **The popup shows the merged split on the export card** — per tier, highest first, plus the blocks in no tier — computed by the same `mergeSessionTiers` that builds the file, so the card and the download cannot disagree. A file where only some sessions carry the axis says so on the card (`3 of 5 sessions measured`) instead of presenting a quarter of the evidence as all of it, and the one-line summary states the coverage the same way (`4 tiers (1 of 4 sessions)`).
  - **The merge gives tiers the same durability semantics as rules** — sessions and distinct UTC days per tier, not just a total — and writes the tally into the ledger's *header* (`# Tiers:`, `# Tier sessions:`, `# Tier unattributed:`) rather than as rule lines, because `tier_core 543` read back by `readTierLedger` or the tier compiler would be 543 rules named `tier_core`. `--min-days` filters rules, not the tally: a tier is the evidence that judged the filter being applied, and dropping it from the table would delete the measurement of the decision itself. The script prints the split with its coverage and warns when some sessions predate the axis — counted, not guessed at.
  - **The committed dropbox carries it now.** The fourth browser run is the first export written since the axis existed, the re-merged `ledger/ledger-hits.txt` reports `# Tiers: tier_privacy 60, tier_ads 25, tier_core 24, tier_annoyances 10, tier_unclassified 6` and `# Tier sessions: 1 of 4`, and a test asserts the committed file is **byte-for-byte** what merging the committed session exports produces — which is what "reproduced" means in practice: the session JSONs are committed beside it for exactly that reason.
  - 36 new tests across the export path — the tally's refusals and partial-coverage summaries in `ledgerExport`, the card's rendering and its no-table case in `ledgerExportCard`, persistence resume semantics in `ledgerSession`, the merge's durability and coverage accounting in `ledgerAggregate`, the dropbox pins in `ledgerCut` (including the byte-for-byte reproduction of the committed file), and the evidence-weighted plan itself in `tierPlanInput` — exercised on a real captured session (`fixtures/browser-ledger-session.json`, the first tiered export) against copies of the curated tier baselines, where the browser-reported tally and the host-join's reconstruction of it are both pinned so the gap between them stays measured rather than assumed.
- **The Unbound Reachability Check Now Re-Runs Itself, And Says So When A Deployment That Was Blocking Stops (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - **The gap.** The check answers *is this deployment blocking right now?* and the pane had a **Check now** button and nothing else — so the verdict on screen was the verdict from whenever a person last thought to look. That is the wrong shape for a check whose entire value is noticing a change: every deployment breaks while nobody is looking. The resolver's cron stops running, a router update drops the `include:` line, a reload is missed, the copy ages past the last compile, and a card that still says **live** from three days ago is exactly the failure this feature existed to prevent.
  - **The hub re-runs it every 15 minutes** for a deployment somebody has engaged with — an address they typed, or a verdict this hub has already produced — started beside the other background timers at launch and cleared on quit, with `unref` so the timer can never be the reason the process stays alive. The gate is re-read **every tick**, not once at startup, so a machine that has never used the pane never queries a resolver on a timer and a machine that starts using it starts being watched at the next tick rather than the next launch. The first tick is a full interval after launch, not at it: a check fires DNS queries at whatever address is configured, and doing that before anyone has looked at the pane is a surprise nobody asked for.
  - **The announcement rule is narrow on purpose, because silence is what makes it worth reading.** It takes a deployment this hub has **already seen blocking** (`live` or `stale`) and speaks only when that stops being true (`stale` or `not-loaded`). A machine that never deployed Unbound hears nothing, ever. A first check has no history to have regressed from, so `unverified` → `not-loaded` is the first honest answer, not an alarm. And the same finding twice is one finding: `stale` → `stale` stays silent, which is what stops a resolver left broken for a week from producing a notification every fifteen minutes until the user turns notifications off for everything.
  - **`resolver-unreachable` is deliberately not announced.** It is what a laptop that suspended, roamed, or lost Wi-Fi reports on the next tick, and alerting on it would spend the feature's credibility on events where nothing is wrong. `feed-offline` is excluded for a different reason — the hub's own server being stopped does not stop the resolver blocking from the copy it already has. The rest (`canary-unconfirmed`, `inconclusive`, `canary-nonexistent`) are the check admitting it could not tell.
  - **The alert quotes the verdict, it does not restate it.** `headline` and `nextStep` come from the same `classifyUnboundReachability` call the card renders, and the confirmation age is formatted with the existing `formatUnboundAge`, so a notification and the pane cannot describe one failure two ways. A machine with notifications off still gets the verdict stored and the pane updated, and the transition is written to the log rather than dropped.
  - **The pane follows the check instead of holding a mount-time verdict.** A finished check is pushed to the renderer on `unbound-reachability-updated`; the row breakdown from a manual run is dropped with it rather than left beside a newer headline it no longer describes. The listener is unsubscribed on unmount, and the whole feature is opt-out by never being engaged with rather than by a setting nobody reads.
  - **The decisions are pure and the wiring is pinned from source.** [unboundWatch.ts](../packages/electron-app/src/unboundWatch.ts) holds the cadence, the engagement gate, the state predicates and the alert rule with no Electron, no clock and no store, so the interesting part is a function of persisted verdicts. **20 new tests**: the cadence is clamped (a stored `0` or `200` cannot become a resolver busy-loop), the gate's four cases, and the **full alert matrix generated from the state list** rather than hand-written — so a verdict state added later cannot slip past the suite unasked. `index.ts` cannot be imported without starting Electron, so its wiring is asserted by reading it: started at startup, stopped at quit, `unref`'d, the previous snapshot read **before** the check that overwrites it, one tick in flight at a time, and the push subscribed and unsubscribed.
  - **The two boundaries are open flags, not fixes** (27 and 28 in [docs/open-flags.md](../docs/open-flags.md)). `stale` is true in the window between a compile and the resolver's next cron fetch, so a tick landing there announces a regression the operator is about to fix automatically; the honest grace is the operator's refresh interval, which lives in a cron entry on the resolver host and which this hub never learns, so choosing one silently would be worse than recording it. And nothing is checked while the app is closed — a timer in a desktop process is a monitor, not an alarm.
- **The Unclassified Residual's Share Was A Floor Rather Than A Dial, So `--shares tier_unclassified=0` Still Shipped 19,933 Rules (`@blockingmachine/core`, `@blockingmachine/browser-extension`)**:
  - **`allocateBudget` hands capacity out in proportion to what each tier is still holding back**, and that is the right rule for a tier whose candidate pool is bounded by evidence: if `tier_security` only has 800 candidates for a 3,600-slot share, its unused slots should not go unused. The unclassified tier is bounded by nothing but the input list, and it holds **104,818 of the 117,303** hosts on this repository's real hub list — so it held essentially every leftover candidate and its share behaved as a floor. Measured across shares of 0, 5, 12, 20, 30 and 50 on that list, it shipped between **19,933 and 21,699** of the 30,000 rules: a spread of 4%, which is what a dial that does nothing looks like. The only place that showed was the per-tier omission line, which reads as ordinary overflow.
  - **It is the one tier where "turn this off" and "ship twenty thousand rules" are the same command.** Every other tier's contents are claims — a classifier verdict, a vocabulary match, a publisher category — and an operator shrinking a share is making a judgement about those. This bucket's only claim is that nothing could place it, so a share of zero has to mean zero.
  - **`HARD_CAPPED_TIERS` excludes it from pass two, and nothing else changes.** Shares of 0/12/30 now ship **0 / 3,214 / 6,923** where they shipped 19,933/20,470/21,125. The categorised tiers are untouched: a tier that under-uses its share still has it redistributed, because making *every* share a ceiling would reintroduce the bug pass two exists to prevent — a thin category wasting slots the user could have had. A test asserts that directly, by measuring that no categorised tier loses a rule when the residual is switched off and that at least one gains measurably.
  - **The cost is stated in the same breath, not absorbed.** On the shipped curated baseline the freed slots have nowhere to go — 12,495 of the 30,000 used at a share of zero, against 30,000 before — because the categorised pools are small and `tier_core` ships 32 curated rules. That is capacity no tier is entitled to, and it is the correct price for a tier whose contents are the absence of evidence. The rules are still in the bundle, `tier_unclassified` is still opt-in, and the omission line names every host that did not fit.
  - **At a budget too small for the share to floor to one rule, the tier ships nothing.** Pass 1 has always floored each cap, so `tier_security` at a share of 12 out of 112 has always got nothing from a budget of 8; the residual now behaves like its neighbours instead of like a tier that ignores its share. One existing test moved from a budget of 8 to 400 for that reason, with the reason written down next to it rather than left as a magic number.
  - 4 new tests in `tierCompiler.test.ts`, pinned by the counts the compiler prints rather than by a return value: zero ships zero and the omission names every host, four shares give four different answers, the baseline is re-seeded between runs (the compiler writes over the curated directory it reads as vocabulary, so sharing one directory between two runs would compare the first run's output rather than the flag's effect).
- **The Shipped Blocklist Cut Is Re-Derived Weekly From A Browser Ledger, So Which 30,000 Rules Ship Stops Being A Concatenation Order (`@blockingmachine/core`, `@blockingmachine/browser-extension`, `@blockingmachine/cli`)**:
  - **The decision this makes.** MV3 guarantees an extension 30,000 static rules and the hub compiles **122,801 distinct hosts**, so *which* 30,000 ship is the entire packaging decision. `compile-tier-rulesets.mjs --hits` already knew how to rank that cut by the browser's own rule hits, and `package-extension.mjs` called it with **no arguments** — so the ranking was never used, and the cut was decided by which upstream list got concatenated first. That is a fact about how the list was built, not about anyone's traffic, and it was the last unmeasured step in a pipeline whose every other input is measured.
  - **A dropbox, because a CI job cannot collect browsing data.** `ledger/ledger-hits.txt` is a committed accumulation of the extension's hit-ledger exports; `ledger/README.md` documents adding to it and what it deliberately does not hold (**`<count> <rule>` — no URLs, no titles, no timestamps, no initiators**, because the merge reads rule text and counts out of each export and drops the rest); `ledger/local-runner.plist` is the optional launchd job that fills it from a downloads folder for anyone who would rather the ledger stayed off a public repository. `merge-browser-ledger.mjs` grew **`--from <dir>`** for this — a week of browsing is a pile of files in a downloads folder, and naming each one is the part people get wrong. Inputs are taken in **name order, not `readdir` order**, because the merged file records its input list in its own header and the merge is re-run weekly: a directory listing that varied by filesystem would make the weekly job a coin flip.
  - **The weekly job is a re-derivation and a guard, not a generator — and the script says so.** `.github/workflows/weekly-tier-compile.yml` runs Mondays 04:17 UTC and opens a PR when the artifacts move. It is **one script**, `scripts/weekly-tier-compile.sh`, because what CI runs weekly and what you run when a derivation looks wrong have to be the same code. It rebuilds the hot set from the ledger, compiles the tier cut from it, reports how many hosts carried measured evidence and how many were **promoted over input order**, runs `check:hotlist` and `verify:mv3`, and refuses to start if it would discard tier changes already in the working tree.
  - **The tier rulesets are compiled and then put back, on purpose.** The repository ships the curated baseline and compiles the real cut at package time; committing 30,000 rules a week would produce a diff nobody can read and a check that cannot see a real change. The run report carries absolute input paths, so it is gitignored — a log, not an artifact.
  - **`check:hotlist` had to become provenance-aware, or this could not have shipped.** The gate compared the checked-in list against one checked-in trace fixture, which was correct for as long as the list came from that fixture. A ledger-derived list would have made it fail the artifact it exists to protect, and the tempting fix — dropping the check — removes the only thing keeping a hand-edited hot list honest. The generated header now records the flags that produced it (`! Derivation: --list … --hits …`) and `--check` **replays them** when the caller names no input, exactly as `check:tiers` is already provenance-aware about the tier plan. The writer and the reader live together in core's `formatHotList` / `parseHotListDerivation`; the reader tolerates a flag it does not know, so a newer writer cannot break an older check.
  - **`package-extension.mjs` now passes `--hits` when the dropbox holds evidence**, and omits it when it does not. Both `package-extension.mjs` and the weekly script ask the same question of the same file, and the predicate is pinned against what the dropbox actually contains — passing `--hits` at an empty file would exit 2 instead of falling back to input order.
  - **The dropbox ships empty, and every consumer says so rather than pretending.** No browser has reported a session, so the weekly run is a no-op that exits 0 with `no rule hits yet`, packaging omits `--hits`, the compiler reports `no rule-hit evidence`, and `check:hotlist` names the trace fixture it used. A synthetic ledger was considered and rejected: a fabricated ranking of 68 hosts is a worse basis for 30,000 slots than an honest input order, and it would be indistinguishable from a real one in every number the pipeline prints. That state is recorded as open flag 25 rather than described as done, because the failure mode of this work is being reported as complete.
  - 6 new core tests for the provenance round-trip, 6 new in the browser extension for the dropbox, the directory scan's reproducibility, the tamper-detection that makes the gate worth having, and the packaging wiring — the ones that need compiled core skip cleanly when it is absent, the way the BIND cases do.
- **Every Deploy Hub Recipe's Copyable Directive Is Now Checked Against The Target's Own Documentation (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - **The drift this catches.** A recipe's whole value is the line it asks the user to paste — `include:` for Unbound, `zone`/`response-policy` for BIND, `actionsfile` for Privoxy, `Key=` settings under `[Service]` for systemd — and those heads are documented vocabulary, not interchangeable spellings: named.conf takes `include "…";` where unbound.conf takes `include: "…"`. A recipe that drifts to a neighbouring product's grammar still renders perfectly plausible markup, and nothing rendered it before this suite.
  - **Each recipe is rendered for real, in a DOM environment, and every button is clicked.** Static markup cannot reach a copyable payload — the handlers are not in it — so `deployRecipeDirectives.test.ts` renders each pane with `createRoot`, clicks every button, and collects what `handleCopy` was actually given, beside every `<code>`/`<pre>` line the recipe prints for hand-copying. Both BIND mechanisms and the daemon pane's real install scripts (generated by `DaemonManager`, not a fixture) are covered.
  - **Each line is classified under the target's own grammar, then judged by a vocabulary the target's documentation lists.** `key:`/`key=` clauses, `keyword …;` named.conf statements, `$`-directives in zone files, `TYPE,…` Surge rules, `[Section]` headers and systemd `Key=` settings are checked against per-target lists written from unbound.conf(5), the BIND 9 ARM, the Privoxy manual, dnsmasq.conf(8), the Surge rule grammar and systemd.unit/service/exec(5). Commands face the same check against the target's documented executables plus generic host tools — `rndc` offered by a recipe that is not BIND's fails. URLs, paths, resource records and prose are not directives and are skipped.
  - **14 tests**: one per rendered recipe, plus the failure path itself — an undocumented clause, a cross-grammar `include` in a Privoxy recipe, and a typo'd `rncd` all produce violations that name the target, the recipe, the surface, the line and the head. Targets that must offer directives have statement floors so a pane that stopped printing them cannot pass vacuously.
- **Every Button In Every Deploy Hub Pane Is Now Click-Tested, Which Caught A Real Wiring Bug (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - The directive suite above proves the payloads are documented; `deployPaneClicks.test.ts` proves the *wiring* — that the button beside a field copies *that field's* value and that a labelled action reaches the handler for the service the pane is about. Each of the 11 panes is mounted in jsdom with every handler prop replaced by a recorder, and every rendered `<button>` is clicked once.
  - **Completeness is the load-bearing check, in both directions.** The spec must list every button the pane renders — a new button shipped without coverage fails with the rendered labels — and every entry must match a rendered button. Expected payloads are written by hand rather than derived from the helpers under test, and every `handleCopy` key is required unique, because two buttons sharing a key both flash "Copied" together.
  - **The bug it caught, first run:** the BIND pane's action row was not mechanism-gated, so under *Shared null zone* the buttons copied the RPZ payloads — a `zone "rpz.blockingmachine"` stanza pointing at the null file and a `response-policy` line the recipe itself says does not exist — plus `rndc reload rpz.blockingmachine`, which cannot apply a change to the *zone set*: added and removed stanzas need `rndc reconfig`. The row is now gated: RPZ keeps its stanza/policy/reload trio; the null zone offers the `include` line and `rndc reconfig`, with the helper comment recording that `reconfig` does not unload a stanza that disappeared.
  - State-dependent surfaces get their own mounts: BIND under both mechanisms, the daemon pane with the install scripts shown and hidden, the Pi-hole/AdGuard Home panes with a configured target URL — the push-reload buttons are correctly disabled without one. On jsdom the suite uses `jest-environment-jsdom`, already in the workspace for the extension's tests.
  - A deliberate mutation — wiring the Privoxy restart button to copy the `actionsfile` line — fails the suite on the payload mismatch. 14 tests; the suite runs in ~40ms of clicking.
- **The Unbound Reachability Check Is Now Run Against Real Resolvers, And It Found Two Defects It Could Not Have Found In A Unit Test (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`, `@blockingmachine/cli`)**:
  - **The gap this closes.** The pane's central claim is empirical — a resolver that loaded the compiled drop-in answers NXDOMAIN for a domain inside it, while that domain still resolves on a second resolver. The 28 unit tests for it were written by the same person who wrote the classifier, and every answer in them is a hand-built object that agrees with the claim, including the `nxdomain` one. A suite whose evidence is authored beside its assertions cannot fail the way this can. So `scripts/unbound-reachability-live.sh` stands up four genuinely configured Unbound instances from this project's own artifacts — the deployment the recipe describes, one that never loaded the file, one holding the **CLI's** drop-in, and one that refuses `example.com` — and asks the shipping code to classify them over real sockets. It runs both containerised (Ubuntu, Unbound 1.19.2) and `--native` against this host (1.26.1); both exit 0 and leave nothing behind.
  - **Eight of nine verdicts are right, and the ninth is the finding.** `live`, `stale`, `canary-unconfirmed`, `not-loaded`, `inconclusive`, `resolver-unreachable`, `feed-invalid` and `unverified` all read correctly off the wire. The `cli-artifact` scenario does not: the CLI's Unbound formatter emits `redirect` + `local-data "…" A 0.0.0.0` where core's emits `always_nxdomain`, so the same 110,506 zones block *differently on the wire* — and the check reads NXDOMAIN as proof, so a working CLI deployment is reported `not-loaded` and told to go check its `include:` line. `dig` confirms it: core's artifact answers NXDOMAIN, the CLI's answers NOERROR carrying 0.0.0.0. Both block; only one looks like a failure. The same drift has a second instance on the command path — `export -f unbound` is missing from `ExportCommand`'s formatter group, falls to the AdGuard `!` default, and writes a file `unbound-checkconf` rejects with **43 errors** while reporting `success: true`.
  - **The second defect is that an address the parser accepts is an address the probe cannot use.** `dns.Resolver.setServers` takes an IP only, and the call sits one line above the `try` in `queryUnboundResolver`, so a hostname throws past `classifyLookupFailure` and out of the IPC handler as an exception rather than a verdict — and a router-hosted Unbound behind a name is one of the two likeliest things to type. Unbracketed IPv6 mis-parses too, quietly: `::1:5353` becomes host `::1:5353` on port 53, which queries an address that cannot exist and reports `refused`.
  - **Both defects are open flags, not fixes** (23 and 24 in [docs/open-flags.md](docs/open-flags.md)) — choosing one Unbound blocking mechanism is a decision about what a blocked name should look like on the wire, and it does not belong inside a verification run whose job was to find it. The full verdict table for both versions, and the two rig bugs that each produced a confidently wrong answer before being fixed, are in [docs/unbound-reachability-live.md](docs/unbound-reachability-live.md).
  - **The part that stays is a suite, not a rig.** `unboundReachabilityLive.test.ts` is the same experiment against real sockets, reading a drop-in and resolver addresses from the environment and skipping cleanly when they are unset — 10 tests, none satisfiable by a hand-built answer, free in CI.
- **BIND Gets Both Of Its Blocking Mechanisms, And The RPZ One Was Silently Under-Blocking (`@blockingmachine/core`, `@blockingmachine/cli`, `@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - **The defect first, because it is the reason this is not a feature entry.** The `bind` export emitted one bare RPZ QNAME trigger per blocked domain — `ads.example.com CNAME .` — and a bare trigger matches *that name only*. The RPZ draft is explicit: *"To control the policy for both a name and its subdomains, two policy RRsets must be used, one for the domain itself and another for a wildcard subdomain."* So **every subdomain of every blocked domain resolved normally on a BIND resolver**, while the same rules sent to Unbound (`local-zone: "ads.example.com"`), dnsmasq (`address=/ads.example.com/0.0.0.0`) or Shadowrocket (`DOMAIN-SUFFIX`) all blocked the subtree — because `||ads.example.com^` means the domain *and* its subdomains. Nothing reported it: the zone loaded, the header said BIND, and the subdomains quietly came back.
  - **Proved on BIND 9.20.29, not argued.** With only the bare record, `ad.doubleclick.net` resolved (NOERROR). With `*.doubleclick.net CNAME .` added, the same query returned NXDOMAIN. The child allow was checked the same way, on domains that exist upstream so a working passthru is distinguishable from a name that simply fails: `www.doubleclick.net` came back NOERROR with no RPZ SOA in the response (a genuine passthru) while `ad.doubleclick.net` returned NXDOMAIN *with* the SOA (a policy rewrite). Each blocked domain is now a pair, and each allowed child is a pair — the bare passthru alone would have released `www.doubleclick.net` while the parent's new wildcard re-blocked everything beneath it.
  - **`bind-null` is the second mechanism, and it is not a variant of the first.** One 3-line file holding an SOA and an NS and nothing else answers authoritatively for every name beneath whatever origin it is loaded under, so a single file can stand in for every blocked domain — confirmed by loading two unrelated zones from one file. What changes per compile therefore inverts: for RPZ you replace the *zone*, for the null zone you replace the *configuration*, and the recipe says so explicitly because a recipe that gets it backwards looks configured while serving yesterday's rules.
  - **The null zone is offered second, with its two real costs stated rather than buried.** A null zone has exactly one behaviour, so an allowed subdomain has no stanza that could release it — the artifact records it as a comment and does not honour it. And it answers **NXDOMAIN for subdomains but NODATA at the apex**, because the apex exists (it holds the SOA and NS) while only its children are absent; RPZ is NXDOMAIN for both. Both measured on 9.20.29.
  - **Installing BIND is what caught a bug no amount of reasoning would have.** The `bind-null` artifact is a *named.conf fragment*, not a zone file, and its comments were `;` like the RPZ artifact's. BIND's ARM is explicit that *"the semicolon (;) character cannot start a comment, unlike in a zone file"* — there it ends a statement. `named-checkconf` rejected the fragment outright. It now uses `#`, and a negative-control test re-comments it the old way and requires BIND to reject it, so the choice is tested rather than asserted.
  - **The tests run against real BIND and skip cleanly when it is absent.** `named-checkzone` answers "does BIND parse this zone" and `named-checkconf -z` answers "does BIND load these zones" — a stronger claim than a regex over a file, and the only thing that could have caught the `;` bug.
