On top of both, the suite boots a **running `named`** on a loopback port with the emitted fragment `include`d verbatim and digs it: `ad.doubleclick.net` answers NXDOMAIN, the `doubleclick.net` apex answers NOERROR with zero answers (the NODATA the fragment warns about, proved rather than asserted), the `www.doubleclick.net` exception is NXDOMAIN — the comment saying it is not honoured is checked the honest way — and a name the server does not own answers REFUSED, which is what keeps "everything is NXDOMAIN" from passing vacuously. Both failure directions were mutation-verified: an empty fragment and a corrupt zone file each fail the digs. Homebrew splits the tools across `sbin` and `bin`, so both are probed and a missing BIND skips the block rather than failing it, because most CI runners and contributors have none.
  - **The CLI reuses core's pair builder rather than restating the rule**, and that reuse is the point: the CLI already had a parallel formatter that had drifted once. Also threaded `bind-null` through `EXPORT_FORMATS`, the IPC validator, Settings and the CLI's format list — and the format-coverage test added earlier this session caught the new format having no deploy mapping on its first run, which is the gap it was written for.
  - 18 new core tests (1275, up from 1257), 18 in the electron app (377, up from 359) and 1 in the CLI (77, up from 76) — the CLI one pins each artifact's comment character, since that was wrong twice.

- **The Home Assistant Add-on Serves The Shadowrocket Rule Set, So A Phone Subscribes To The Add-on Instead Of A Desktop Hub (`@blockingmachine/homeassistant-addon`, `@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - The Shadowrocket recipe pointed at the desktop hub's LAN feed, which only answers while that process is running and that machine is awake. A rule set that stops arriving is nearly always a rule set pointing at a laptop. The add-on is already a long-lived service on the same LAN, so it now serves the rule set itself at **`/shadowrocket.conf`** (aliases `/shadowrocket.txt`, `/ruleset.conf`), and the Deploy Hub tab offers that URL as a second, copyable feed box next to the hub's.
  - **The rule set is rendered from the same DNS feed as the Unbound drop-in, and the two now share one rule-to-host parser** (`hostRules.js`). That sharing is the point rather than tidiness: the feeds are two renderings of one source, and a host one of them fails to parse is a host that is sinkholed in the resolver and still reachable through the phone — a failure that presents as "the phone still shows ads" and is very hard to trace back to a parser. The invariant is asserted directly, comparing the host sets both feeds derive from the same input.
  - **Three properties of the format are load-bearing and are pinned rather than assumed.** The rules live under a **`[Rule]` section** (a bare list is not read by the app), comments are **`#`** (`!` is AdGuard syntax a Shadowrocket config rejects), and rules are **`DOMAIN-SUFFIX`** (`DOMAIN` is exact-host only and misses every subdomain — the defect the desktop export had to be repaired for).
  - **And the one that is easy to get backwards: allows are emitted before blocks.** A Surge-style rule set is first-match-wins, so a child allowed under a blocked parent has to precede the parent that would swallow it. The test uses `example.com` and `sub.example.com` *specifically* because the child sorts after its parent — a single sorted list would interleave them into `example.com,REJECT` first and the `DIRECT` would never be reached. This is the reverse of Unbound (longest match) and Privoxy (last match), so one ordering constant could not have served all three; the desktop exporter already emitted `DIRECT` before blocks, and this matches it.
  - Unlike the Unbound feed, the allow is **kept** rather than dropped: this format can express one, and dropping it would re-block a host the user explicitly released. The add-on's count is genuinely lower than the desktop export's, and the pane says so, because the add-on renders from the DNS feed and has no syntax for the rules a rule set cannot carry.
  - **The Unbound feed was refactored onto the shared parser, and its 11 existing tests are the safety net** — all stayed green, which is what makes the extraction safe rather than merely plausible.
  - Verified end to end against a running add-on: publishing a `dns.txt` with a parent block, a child allow, three other block shapes and a cosmetic rule yields `DOMAIN-SUFFIX,sub.example.com,DIRECT` **first**, the four blocks after it, the cosmetic dropped, and an `/unbound.conf` agreeing on the same four hosts.
  - 13 new add-on tests (24 total, from 11) and 6 in the electron app.

- **Every Export Format Is Now Proven To Have A Deploy Target, So The Unbound-Style Gap Cannot Reopen (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - `privoxy`, `shadowrocket` and `bind` were emitted, advertised in the CLI examples and published as subscribe URLs for years with **no tab in the Hub, no recipe anywhere, and no row in the README's format table** — a user who compiled one got a file and no way to use it. Nothing in the build could see it, because each piece was individually correct: the formatter emitted the syntax, the validator accepted the name, the picker offered the choice. The gap is only visible by walking the formats and asking what happens to each one, which is why it survived an audit that had already found the other two.
  - `exportFormatDeployCoverage.test.ts` walks **`EXPORT_FORMATS` — the pipeline's own list, the same array the exporter validates against** — and requires every member to resolve to either a target in the [Deploy Hub registry](../packages/electron-app/src/deploy/deployTargets.tsx) or a recorded reason why that format is platform-neutral on purpose. Adding a format now fails the build until someone has decided where it deploys, which is the decision all three closed formats had to make anyway.
  - **The two answers are disjoint, and neither may name a format the exporter cannot emit.** A row present-and-empty would satisfy the headline test for the wrong reason, which is why `domains` and `plain` are *absent* from the deployable table rather than listed with nothing in them, and why a stale row is its own failure: the format was renamed or removed and the table kept answering for a file the exporter no longer writes.
  - **`domains` and `plain` are exempt with the reason recorded inline, and a reason is mandatory.** The cheap way to silence the test is to add a format to the exempt list with an empty string, which would reproduce the original gap with a comment attached. They are exempt because naming a client is the choice those two formats exist to avoid — one domain per line is meant for whatever the user already runs.
  - **The gap also pointed the other way, and that is checked too.** Every Hub target must be reachable from a format or be one of three declared format-agnostic sinks (`pihole`, `home-assistant`, `system-daemon`); a tab reached from no format is one that can never say which file it wants.
  - **A second, quieter failure: three hand-maintained lists have to agree for a format to be selectable at all** — the formatter's union in core, the IPC validator in [index.ts](../packages/electron-app/src/index.ts), and the picker in [Settings.tsx](../packages/electron-app/src/Settings.tsx). Add a format to one and the other two go stale silently, leaving a tab pointing at a file the app refuses to compile. `FilterFormat` is erased at runtime and cannot be walked, so the link between it and the runtime list is a **compile-time guard** (proved by adding a format to the union and reading `TS2322: Type 'true' is not assignable to type 'never'`), and the other two are read from source, scoped to the format select because Settings renders 23 other `<option>` elements.
  - **`adguard` and `abp` share a row on purpose.** Core emits byte-identical output for them — both reach the same exporter branch and the same header generator — so a target that deploys one deploys the other, and listing them separately would imply a difference that is not there.
  - **Verified by mutation, not by reading it.** Dropping `privoxy` from the table without an exemption fails two tests; renaming a target fails two; a format in the picker the pipeline cannot emit fails one; a format the IPC validator forgot fails one. Each recipe is also asserted to build a feed URL from the LAN address and never to emit the `<your-computer-ip>` placeholder the pane shows before the server has one — and `bind` is pinned as having *no* feed URL, because BIND cannot fetch a zone over HTTP.
  - **What this does not claim, stated plainly: it proves coverage, not correctness.** A recipe can exist, name a real target, and still be worded wrongly — which is exactly [open flag 19](docs/open-flags.md), where `actionsfile <url>` may be a directive Privoxy does not accept. This test passes that line happily, and flag 19 now says so. Closing it needs the recipe checked against a running proxy, which no unit test can do.
  - 16 new tests (electron 353, up from 337).

- **The Hot Set Is Proved Equivalent To The Full Export, Per Request, On The Traffic It Came From (`@blockingmachine/core`, `@blockingmachine/cli`)**:
  - Making the measured hot set the default install for every overflowing browser (the entry below) rests on a claim that was asserted only in aggregate. `hotlist.test.ts` compared `blockedHosts`, `blockedRequests`, `allowlistedHosts`, `exceptionRulesFired`, `uniqueHosts` and the fired rule *set* between the two lists. Totals cannot see a substitution: a list that blocked `a.example` and missed `b.example` while another blocked `b.example` and missed `a.example` reports identical counts. Six tests now assert the property directly instead — for each of the session's 232 requests, the verdict from the hot set equals the verdict from the full export.
  - **The single-request decision was lifted out of `replayRuleHits` rather than reimplemented in the test.** `decideRequest` is now exported, and the aggregate replay is a caller of it, so there is one implementation of the precedence a path rule outranks a bare zone block and an exception outranks both. A test that reimplemented those lines could keep agreeing with itself after the rule underneath it changed — the failure mode this file exists to catch.
  - **`allowed` and `exception` are kept apart, deliberately.** Both mean "not blocked", but an exception is a rule that *fired* and said let this through, and that is the decision a trimmed list is most likely to lose: a hot set that keeps the blocks and drops an allowlist does not under-protect, it starts blocking requests the full export deliberately released. So one test asserts the fired exception *rules* are the same six, verbatim, rather than merely that six exceptions fired somewhere.
  - **"Across every scope" is counted, not assumed.** A test comparing only hostname rules would report agreement for the whole list while saying nothing else. The run prints what it actually compared — **hostname 127, exception 12, path 5, no rule matched 88** — and asserts that shape, so every bucket is non-empty and each agrees. Verified by mutation: deleting one exception fails four tests, deleting one hostname block fails three, and the exception test correctly keeps passing when only a block is removed.
  - **It also covers something the counts could not reach at all.** The measured trace records hostnames, so nothing in the suite had ever exercised a path-scoped rule *as one* — the hot set's one path rule wins on its host alone, and the scope bucket above could be misread as covering path matching. One test attaches a real URL, derived from the rule's own text and checked against the matcher before use so it cannot pass on a URL the rule does not govern, and asserts both lists block it *by URL*.
  - **And it pins where the invariant stops.** The shipped `hotlist.txt` was derived from the whole session, so there is no traffic inside it the set has not seen, and testing the boundary against it finds nothing. A second hot set is therefore derived from half the pages: **0 disagreements on the 78 requests it came from, 57 on the 154 held out**. Both halves are asserted, since a test checking only the disagreement would pass just as happily on a set that disagreed everywhere.
  - 6 new tests (CLI 76, up from 70). The honest characterisation of what this does *not* cover is unchanged and still measured: the hot set is exact for its derivation traffic and keeps 38 of 93 blocks on a held-out page split (flag 16).

- **A Chrome urlFilter fidelity gap in the path matcher, found by that work and deliberately left open (`@blockingmachine/core`)**:
  - The matcher models Chrome's `^` separator as `[\/\?#]` or end-of-URL. Chrome documents it as *"matches anything except a letter, a digit, or one of the following: `_`, `,`, `-`, `.`, or `%`"* — a negated class, so `=`, `+`, `@`, `~` and every other non-alphanumeric character are separators to the browser and not to the model. Since `validatePathFilter` hands `||host/path…` to Chrome **verbatim**, the replay is meant to model Chrome, and here it is narrower: a path rule can be counted as decided by a request the browser would decide differently.
  - **The same investigation produced a fix that had to be reverted, which is the more useful half.** Probing showed `||host^*/ads.js` failing to match the plain `/ads.js` it was written for — the `^` was compiling to a *consuming* separator, so the regex needed two slashes where a URL has one. The obvious "fix" was to make it a zero-width lookahead. That is wrong: Chrome's `^` demonstrably **consumes** a character, since the documented `||example.com^ads.js` matches `/ads.js` by having the separator stand for the slash itself. ABP semantics would make it zero-width; Chrome's are not ABP's, and the extension ships these rules as Chrome `urlFilter`s. Reverted, and `url-rule-matcher.test.ts` — which pinned the consuming behaviour correctly, with the reasoning in the test name — stayed green throughout.
  - The character class is a separate gap from the consuming behaviour and is left as **open flag 18**, because changing it moves every path-scoped number the project reports, including the 6,230-of-7,628 decidability figure, and no trace in the repository has URLs that would show whether the change helped or hurt.

- **The Measured Hot Set Is Chosen By The Budget, Not By A Flag (`@blockingmachine/browser-extension`, `@blockingmachine/cli`)**:
  - The trimmed hot set existed but nothing selected it. A client whose rule budget could not hold the full export got **whatever prefix fitted**, with the tail dropped and no record of what went — so the alternative to the measured set was not "no protection" but an uncharacterised, unmentioned fraction of it. `chooseRuleSource` in [dnrManager.ts](../packages/browser-extension/src/background/dnrManager.ts) now decides between the two from the budget alone: **the full export when it fits, the measured hot set when it does not**. No flag, no setting, no configuration.
  - **This is the default path, not an edge case, and the number is why.** The shipped export compiles to **127,820** dynamic rules against Chrome's **30,000**-rule cap, so an ordinary browser overflows. Run end to end against the real `genericBrowserRules.txt` over a real server: at the 30,000 default cap the full export would have pruned **97,820** rules and the client now installs the **43** the hot set compiles to; at a 1,500-rule budget it would have pruned 126,320, and it still installs 43. The 43 rather than 77 is scoping — the 77 are ABP lines, and a scoped rule expands into one DNR rule per site it names. With no hot set served, the same export installs 1,500 and says why.
  - **The decision is a pure function so it can be asserted directly.** Inferring the answer from what ended up installed would let a wrong answer pass as long as something plausible was installed. It is budget-only and deliberately independent of what is currently installed, so a preserving call cannot flip the source between one application and the next — whether the export fits is a property of the export and the cap, not of the browser's momentary state.
  - **Both of the ways this can go wrong are handled rather than assumed away.** If the hot set *also* overflows, the full export is kept and pruned — it would then be not a smaller list but a differently-shaped one, and choosing it trades a truncation for a truncation. And an empty or absent hot set falls straight through to today's pruning, which is why the fallback had to keep working rather than being allowed to fail. When the full export fits, nothing is logged about the hot set at all, so the one message that matters is the one that gets read.
  - **The owned-filter set and the installed plan are read from the same source, and that is a safety property.** `ownedFilters` is the set of installed filters a call supersedes — everything in it is removed from the browser on the promise that the plan puts it back. Reading one from the full export while the plan came from the hot set would retire thousands of rules and replace them with the measured handful: a silent loss of protection rather than a visible error. A test pins the superset relationship directly — nothing removed that the plan does not also reinstall.
  - **The hot set reaches the client from the feed, and its absence is a normal state.** `blockingmachine serve` publishes it at `/v1/hotlist.txt` beside the full list, and the extension fetches it alongside the rules from **wherever the full list came from**, with the filename swapped, so a deployment whose feed is not the local default is not asked to configure it twice. The endpoint answers `200` with an empty body when there is no measurement rather than `404`ing, because a 404 reads as a fault in the server and would be the more surprising of the two to debug; an empty body is treated as absent rather than as "a measurement that blocked nothing", which would mean installing zero rules. A failed or unreachable hot-set fetch never fails the sync — it is a resource consulted only in an emergency, and trading the full list for it would be backwards.
  - **Writing that last part found a real leak in the existing code.** A rejected `fetch` never reaches a `clearTimeout` placed after the `await`, so the pending 5-second handle survived every failure — in an MV3 worker that is a worker kept awake for nothing, on a code path whose most common outcome is now "no hot set here". Both fetches go through one `fetchFeedText` helper that clears in a `finally`. The test asserts the timer count is zero after a rejecting fetch, and it was checked by reinstating the old pattern: it fails.
  - **And the hot set is fetched concurrently, because it has four candidates of its own.** Awaiting it before the full list would mean a deployment whose host drops packets rather than refusing the connection spends **twenty seconds** discovering it has no measurement before the full list is even requested. It is started and awaited at the end instead, so it costs no latency on top of the sync. The test asserts the full list is requested while the hot set is still outstanding, and was checked against the sequential version, where it fails.
  - 32 new tests (extension 546, up from 516 — 19 on the chooser and the wiring, 11 on the sync client; CLI 70, up from 68). The chooser covers all six outcomes, the budget honoured rather than defaulted, user rules eating into it, the hot plan really being the hot set's own plan rather than a truncated full one, and a full export past Chrome's default cap selecting the hot set so the day the export is small enough to fit, the test says so. The sync client's load-bearing cases are the negative ones — no hot set, an empty one, an unreachable one, a timeout, and a full list that is unreachable while the hot set is served. The wiring pins that a global pause still installs nothing, that omitting the option entirely leaves the old behaviour untouched, and that nothing is removed that the plan does not also reinstall. The CLI tests cover the endpoint serving the file verbatim (header comments included, since the header is where the list states what it was measured on) and the no-hot-set case reporting rather than failing.
  - **The honest characterisation is in the README and in open flag 17, because this change promotes a measured limitation to a default.** A per-session hot set keeps 40.9% of blocks on a held-out split (flag 16) and blocks **nothing at all** on a host that session never visited. That was stated for an opt-in artifact and is now stated for what every overflowing browser installs. The extension logs it on every apply — the count is kept, because the hot set is the point rather than an error, but the reason and the phrase "protection is limited to the traffic that measurement saw" belong where someone will actually see them.

- **The Popup's Components Can Be Tested By Pressing Them, Not Just Read As Strings (`@blockingmachine/browser-extension`)**:
  - The extension had **505 tests and not one of them rendered a component**. `testMatch` was `**/*.test.ts` and the environment was `node`, so a `.tsx` test could not have run even if it existed, and there was no DOM to run one in. Every popup component — `ElementScanPanel`, `LedgerStatusCard`, `LedgerExportCard`, `TierPlanBasis`, `TierDriftNotice`, `PopupApp` — was covered only where its *logic* could be pulled out of it. The panels are presentational by design (the element-scan panel's own comment says so: props in, markup out, no `chrome.*`, no state), which means the markup was testable and nothing else was. The element-scan panel had a 227-line `renderToStaticMarkup` suite for exactly that reason, and it ends by naming its own limit: *"What this cannot check is clicks: a static render has no event loop."*
  - **The setup is two Jest projects, not one environment switched per file.** `node` keeps `**/*.test.ts` on `testEnvironment: node` and is byte-for-byte what it was; `dom` takes `**/*.test.tsx` on jsdom with `@testing-library/react` and the jest-dom matchers. Splitting on file extension means the environment a test runs in is readable from its path, and it means `@testing-library/jest-dom` — which has nothing to attach to without a DOM — never loads into the background and content-script suites. A per-file `@jest-environment` docblock would have worked and would also have let a background test reach for `document` and pass because a DOM happened to be around. Adding jsdom to the node project instead would have done the same.
  - **Ten render tests for the element-scan panel, all of them about the wiring the static suite cannot reach.** They query by role and accessible name, so they also check the panel is operable without a CSS selector, and they assert *arguments* — because for the bulk action the argument is the whole point. `onHide` takes an optional selector list and **omitting it means "hide everything the scan found"**: real elements on a real page, no confirmation and no undo in this component. Nothing tested that. Nor that each group's Hide passes *its own* selector rather than closing over the first, nor that a busy sweep locks every button, nor that the harvest export — which drains the buffer, so a double press loses the first file — cannot be pressed twice.
  - **The tests were checked by breaking the component, because a render test that cannot fail is a screenshot.** Three mutations: the bulk button passing a selector list instead of nothing, every group's Hide closing over `groups[0]`, and the busy lock dropped from the bulk button. Each was caught, and **none of the three was caught by any of the nine existing static tests** — the blind spot was exactly as wide as that suite's header said it was. The component was restored and the tree is clean.
  - **The setup file's `require` does not carry types, which `tsc` found immediately.** Jest passed all ten; `npm run type-check` failed on every DOM matcher, because types do not travel through a `require` in a `.cjs` file. Declared once in `src/types/jest-dom.d.ts` (a `.d.ts` with an import, so nothing reaches the bundle) rather than per test file, and pointed at `/jest-globals` to match the runtime entry — the matchers have to augment the `expect` these tests import from `@jest/globals`, which ESM requires.
  - 10 new tests (extension 515, up from 505; 36 node suites unchanged, plus 1 dom suite). Three dev dependencies: `jest-environment-jsdom`, `@testing-library/react`, `@testing-library/jest-dom`. The one high-severity advisory in `npm audit` is pre-existing and unrelated — `brace-expansion` under `@electron/node-gyp`, present in the committed lockfile before this change and touched by no package added here.
  - Writing them found a real defect that is recorded rather than absorbed: **every group's "Hide" button has the same accessible name**, on the one action in the panel that removes page content. The render test reaches those buttons by their containing `.ai-group`, which is the right way to check the wiring and quietly works around the problem, so it is open flag 13 rather than a test that encodes the defect as correct.
- **The Privoxy Action File And The BIND RPZ Zone Are Served By The Home Assistant Add-on Too (`@blockingmachine/homeassistant-addon`, `@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - **The gap this closes.** Both deploy targets are fetch-once artifacts — Privoxy reads a local file via `actionsfile`, BIND reads a local zone and `rndc reload`s — and the only host answering for that fetch was the desktop hub, which stops answering whenever the laptop sleeps. The add-on is already the long-lived service on that LAN and already renders `unbound.conf` and `shadowrocket.conf` from the published DNS feed, so the two file-based targets now get the same treatment: `/privoxy.action` and `/db.blockingmachine.rpz`, rendered on request from `dns.txt`. Every feed-backed target in the Deploy Hub now has a host that answers independently of the desktop — and because the add-on *renders* rather than serving a compiled file, these URLs answer whatever export format the desktop is set to.
  - **The Privoxy feed is the format's real semantics, not a renamed list.** `{+block{Blockingmachine Blocklist}}` opens before any pattern (a pattern with no action above it belongs to nothing), every host is emitted `.host` — the leading dot is what covers subdomains — and allows land in a `{-block}` section *after* the blocks, because Privoxy is last-match-wins. The Shadowrocket feed orders the same exception the opposite way, and the module comment says why rather than leaving a reader to "fix" one of them.
  - **The BIND feed carries the load-bearing details the desktop learned the hard way.** `;` comments (`#` is a parse error in a zone file), the ARM's SOA/NS preamble a zone cannot load without, and two records per name — `host CNAME .` plus `*.host CNAME .` — because a bare QNAME trigger matches that name only. Allows emit `CNAME rpz-passthru.` pairs, resolved by longest match rather than file order. A host both blocked and allowed resolves to the allow — two contradictory RRsets at one trigger is undefined, so the block is suppressed the way the desktop's precedence pass suppresses it.
  - **The null-zone mechanism deliberately is not served.** Its file is three fixed lines and what changes per compile is the `named.conf` fragment — so there is no per-compile artifact for a feed to carry, and it cannot honour an allow anyway, which the `bindHomeAssistantUrl` comment says.
  - **Panes surface it the way the Shadowrocket pane does.** A "Stays up when this desktop is off" box copies the add-on URL, and each recipe gained an *optional* "or fetch it from the add-on" step with the `curl` command — the hub feed keeps its job as transport, and the add-on is the same transport from a host that does not sleep.
  - 22 new add-on tests covering the translation shapes, dedup, contradiction resolution, section ordering and the every-line-is-legal pins both feeds already use; desktop helper and pane anchors extended, including the check that the null-zone recipe does *not* offer the URL. On top of those sits `feedAgreement.test.js`, the cross-feed suite this change made possible: it spawns the add-on against a fixture `dns.txt`, fetches every feed route over real HTTP, and parses each artifact back to a host set with extractors written in the test — not the renderers' own parser. All four feeds must agree on the blocked set; the one honest divergence (Unbound cannot express an allow, so a child exception stays sinkholed under its parent) is asserted explicitly rather than smoothed over. Ordering mutations in both directions were verified to fail it.
- **The Add-on's Feeds Can Be Locked Behind A Token (`@blockingmachine/homeassistant-addon`)**:
  - **`feed_token` is a new masked option** (`password?` in the schema, plumbed through `options.json` → `FEED_TOKEN` like every other option). Empty is the default and changes nothing — the LAN-open behaviour is preserved exactly.
  - **When set, the whole surface is gated, not just the feeds.** `/v1/protection` pauses blocking for the network and the dashboard embeds rule previews plus the feed URLs, so gating only `/dns.txt` would protect the list while leaving the killswitch and the same data open. Every endpoint — feeds, REST API, dashboard — returns `401` with a `Basic` challenge.
  - **Three credential shapes, because the consumers differ.** `?token=` for subscription URLs that can't send headers (Shadowrocket remote configs, adlist URLs, cron fetches); `Authorization: Bearer` for scripts; `Authorization: Basic`, which is also what a `user:token@host` userinfo URL arrives as — so AdGuard Home, Pi-hole and curl all work. The comparison is `timingSafeEqual`, and the token is never written to the add-on's log.
  - **One deliberate non-feature, stated in the docs.** `X-Ingress-Path`/`X-Hass-Source` mark traffic that came through HA's ingress proxy, but anything that can reach the port can set them by hand — so they are not accepted as proof. The consequence is honest: with a token set, the sidebar panel may show a browser login prompt instead of loading inline.
  - **The dashboard prints token-carrying URLs** — `feedUrl()` appends `?token=` to every feed row and to the `feedServer` block in `/v1/status`, gated the same way, so a copied URL works as pasted. A diagnostics row states the mode (`token required` / `open (LAN trust)`).
  - `feedAuth.test.js` spawns the real server against a fixture data dir and pins all of it: every path 401s without the credential (with the challenge header and no feed content in the body), a forged `X-Hass-Source: core.ingress` request is still refused, `/v1/protection` POST is gated, each accepted credential shape returns 200, wrong credentials get no partial answer, and the CORS preflight stays open. The mutation check — disabling the gate — fails on `/` returning 200 instead of 401.

### Fixed
- **The LAN feed server served whatever file shared its output directory, and the event stream had no guard at all (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**: an audit pass over the running server found three exposures in the same surface. The arbitrary-name file branch resolved `join(outputDir, basename(pathname))` — safe against escape, but `basename` strips the directory part, so `GET /config.yaml`, `GET /../package.json` and `GET /tsconfig.json` all served non-feed files a user keeps beside the compiled lists (`package.json` returned 200 live). Serving is now allowlisted through `feedServing.ts`: the segregated endpoints, `malware.txt`, `hotlist.txt`, the legacy list names, the configured save-path basename, and the `processed_<format>` export family — everything else is a 404, and the 404's own directory listing is filtered by the same predicate so it cannot name what the allowlist withholds. `/v1/events` carried no guard: a web page's `EventSource` could subscribe cross-origin under the response's `Access-Control-Allow-Origin: *` and read `remote_control` broadcasts and browsing-derived telemetry, and a configured `feedToken` gated mutations while the stream stayed open — it now takes the same origin guard plus the token whenever one is configured (token-unset keeps flag 45's documented open-by-default LAN model). Two smaller hardenings rode the pass: a stalled SSE subscriber that stopped reading is now evicted once its buffered backlog passes 512 KB instead of accumulating every event in memory, and `/v1/compile` answers `alreadyRunning` when `compileInFlight` is set rather than firing an IPC and claiming a compile it did not start. Verified on the live server: the non-feed and traversal requests all refuse, the feeds still serve, a foreign-origin subscribe is refused while a tokenless `curl` still connects. Closed flags 49 and 50 in `docs/open-flags.md`.
- **A picked element's tab telemetry recorded the page URL as its domain (`@blockingmachine/browser-extension`)**: the `ELEMENT_PICKED` handler called `emptyTabTelemetry(tabId, url, url)` — the third argument is the domain field — so a tab whose first tracked event was a manual pick carried a full URL where the popup and export read a host. The domain is now derived through `siteFromUrl` like every other caller.
- **The match ledger silently lost blocks in four different ways, so a page that was being filtered showed an empty popup (`@blockingmachine/browser-extension`)**: measured end-to-end in a real browser, `onRuleMatchedDebug` wakes a suspended worker but drops events in flight during the cold start — and a gate skipped `getMatchedRules` entirely whenever the live API existed, so what delivery missed was gone forever. The poll is now the backstop on every build: a persisted cursor and a timestamp-paired live log keep each match counted exactly once across both paths. Three more losses fell out of the same audit — concurrent match events raced their `get`→mutate→`set` telemetry round-trips (a burst's parallel writes clobbered each other; now serialized per tab), the poll scoped to the active tab even though the feedback permission returns every open tab in one call (background tabs' blocks were unrecoverable; now reclaimed), and a `tabId < 0` record — a match that outlived its tab — was dropped entirely instead of reaching the ledger. Session exports can now honestly say `feed: 'hybrid'`, and `docs/open-flags.md` records the finding as closed flag 48.
- **"Download extension package" spawned a second Electron window instead of building (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**: the handler ran webpack via `process.execPath` — which inside Electron's main process is the Electron binary itself, not Node — so the click booted a whole second app instance (extra window, doubled startup logs, a build that never resolved and a button stuck on "Building…"). The same latent class existed in `daemonManager` — its `spawn(process.execPath, [daemon.js])`, launchd plist and systemd unit all named the Electron binary as a Node interpreter. Every spawn site now sets `ELECTRON_RUN_AS_NODE=1`, so the binary runs the given script as plain Node (verified: the extension builds and the process exits instead of hanging as an app).
- **`type-check` Now Reads The Test Suites It Was Quietly Skipping (`@blockingmachine/core`, `@blockingmachine/cli`)**:
  - **The exclusion that kept tests out of `dist` also kept them out of the check.** Both packages' `tsconfig.json` excluded `src/__tests__` so `tsc` could never emit test files into `dist` — but `type-check` ran the same config, so type errors in tests compiled nowhere except an IDE. `tsconfig.test.json` in each package extends the base with the whole `src` tree and `noEmit`, and `type-check` now runs both passes: production emit-config first, test-inclusive check second.
  - **The first run found exactly the errors the blind spot predicted**: possibly-null `elementActionCalibrationPair` dereferences, an `AddressInfo` imported from `node:http` instead of `node:net`, NodeNext-extension-less imports, a `{constructor: 'x'}` literal colliding with `Object`'s own type, an `ElementEvalCase` literal missing required `family`, a `let res` tsc could not prove assigned, and `elementCandidates` feeding element-class probabilities into the domain `TriageCandidate` shape — that last now passes through a named `ELEMENT_CLASS_TO_THREAT` map instead of an accidental `as`-shaped silent drop.
  - The CLI's `typeRoots` also pointed at a package-local `node_modules/@types` that hoisting leaves empty; the check config points it at the monorepo root so `describe`/`it`/`expect` resolve. All four workspaces pass; the extension and Electron suites were already covered (their tests import `@jest/globals` explicitly).
- **A Full-Repository Audit Found And Fixed The Bugs The Flags Could Not See (`@blockingmachine/core`, `@blockingmachine/browser-extension`, `@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - **Regex safety admitted complements it could not model.** `refusedRegexReason`'s first sets read `[^a]` as literal-`a`-or-`^` and `\D`/`\W`/`\S` as their lowercase selves — so the provably exponential `([^a]|b)+`, `(\D|\w)+` and `(\S|x)+` shipped past the check that exists to refuse exactly them. Negated classes are now modelled as their complement, and `ruleModifiers` stopped misparsing the `$` anchor inside `@@/regex$/` as a modifier separator, which had been dropping every exception regex ending in `$` before the safety check could see it.
  - **Allow matches were recorded as block hits, and scoped rules reinstalled unscoped.** The DNR match pipeline reconstructed a rule's source line from its `urlFilter` alone — an `allow`/`allowAllRequests` match counted as a block, and a `$domain=` or `$important` rule came back as a bare `||host^` that the own-ledger merge would reinstall *globally*. The rule snapshot and static index now carry action and initiator scope, only real block actions feed the ledger, and `@@` lines keep their prefix.
  - **The hit ledger could lose hits and retry an impossible payload forever.** `LedgerSessionRecorder.load()` replaced in-memory state with whatever storage answered — clobbering hits recorded during the await — and a session larger than the quota failed every flush identically. Loads merge, and the flush prunes to a bounded payload instead of looping on a size it cannot change. Session splits keep the ending day's `endedAt`, invalid stored `feed` values coerce to `unknown`, and cap evictions surface as `rulesDropped` rather than shrinking the export silently.
  - **A stale sealed credential could shadow a newer plaintext write; a redirect could carry a token across origins.** `readSecret` preferred `${key}Encrypted` whenever it parsed — the opposite precedence from `sealSecretField`, which treats surviving plaintext as the newer write — so a token rotated during a keychain-less session read back as the old one. Plaintext is now authoritative on both read paths, and a working cipher migrates it in place. `sinkholeFetch` strips `Authorization`/`Cookie`/`Proxy-Authorization` on cross-origin redirects (same-origin hops keep them), on both the native `fetch` path and the local-TLS `http` path.
  - **Generated shell commands interpolated user values unquoted.** `unboundFetchCommand` put `feedUrl`, `configPath` and the bearer token into a `curl`/`sh` line verbatim — a token containing `$(…)` executed on the resolver. Every interpolated value is shell-quoted, the fetch downloads to a temp file and atomically renames it into place (a failed pull can no longer truncate the live config), and the report-back POST cannot mislabel a successful fetch as failed when the report itself fails.
  - **The element classifier's CNAME feature consulted the wrong table.** `cnameCloak` matched `resolvedCname` against the host-kind table, which covers 35 of the 176 `KNOWN_CLOAKED_TARGETS` entries — 141 documented cloak destinations (`adroll.com`, `liveramp.com`, `openx.com`, …) silently never fired. The feature now matches the cloak table as a registrable suffix; the table lives in `cloakTargets.ts` so the content-script bundle can read it without pulling `node:dns`. `resolvedCname` also survives `renderSnapshotSource` (a promoted case was losing the field its verdict relied on), folds the DNS root dot out before matching, and `elementSignature` keys an unidentifiable element by its resource host or first text token instead of a bare tag — so a `hide` on one anonymous `<script>` can no longer generalise to every script.
  - **UI surfaces now name what they show.** The Rule Inspector renders `annoyance` verdicts instead of a blank chip, the quarantine filter gains the `Consent/Annoyance` pill, the three credential panes warn when the OS keychain is unavailable (matching the Settings card), the hot-set staleness denominator counts shipped rules rather than all installed ones, and `Scheduled refresh` rows appear on every Unbound verdict — a persisted cron failure is evidence even while the feed is down.
  - **The AI Radar page could not scroll and clipped content on both axes.** `.main-content-scroll.view-ai-radar` ran a fit-to-viewport variant (`overflow-y: hidden` + `height: 100%`/`overflow: hidden` on `.ai-radar-container`), so the 10-row offender table pushed the nav tabs and every tab's content below the fold with no way to reach them, and the top-bar grid's `fr` tracks let the watchdog card's nowrap cadence line force its column wider than the container, clipping off the right edge. The page now scrolls like every other view (`.main-content-scroll`'s `overflow-y: auto`), the grid uses `minmax(0, …)` tracks with `min-width: 0` on both cards so content shrinks instead of escaping, and `App.tsx` no longer crashes the whole tree on an unguarded `window.electron.getSavePath()` when the preload bridge is absent. Verified on the live app over CDP: `scrollTop` reaches the 273px overflow max and the tab bar plus full scout panel are visible at bottom scroll.
  - **`forge.config.cjs` declared `port`/`loggerPort` where plugin-webpack never reads them.** They were nested under `renderer`, but the plugin reads `config.port`/`config.loggerPort` from the top level — so the committed values were dead and the renderer dev server always bound the plugin's default 3000 (failing `EADDRINUSE` whenever another dev server held it). The options now live where the plugin reads them, and `FORGE_RENDERER_PORT` overrides the bind (validated, documented in the package README); the `index.ts` dev fallback uses the same env var so the two cannot disagree.
  - **The vocabulary derivation's compound token could escape its own zone.** `vocabularyCandidatesFor` built the `zone.sld` pair by label position — `labels[len-3].labels[len-2]` — so `metrics.westpac.com.au` minted `westpac.com`, a different registrable domain outside the `.com.au` subtree the listing attests (flag 41's compiled-tier dry-run caught it live: the token would have fired on the bank's `.com` apex). The pair is now built from `decomposeDomain`, which knows `com.au`/`co.uk` are public suffixes — `metrics.westpac` stays inside the zone and is refused as generic there, and the boundary is pinned in the derivation suite.
  - **Tests and docs were realigned.** `secretsStore`, `sinkholeFetch`, `element-ai`, `ledgerSession` and the reachability suite pin each fix as a regression; `tierVocabularyDerivation.test` passes `tierHosts` like the driver it replays; `elementWeightFitting`'s module note and `open-flags.md` entries (41, 46, 9, 15/16 corpus counts) are restated to what the shipped artifacts actually measure.
  - **`@@||host/path` died at compile time; `@@||host^` + a path was silently an allowlist for the whole zone.** The exception branch of `CompiledDomainRuleSet.compile` anchored on `@@||host^` and kept only the host: a rule like `@@||bbc.co.uk^*/adverts.js` — "let the BBC's own adverts.js through, nothing else" — allowlisted every request to `bbc.co.uk`, while the `@@||host/path` form failed the hostname extraction and was dropped outright. The shipped list carries 816 such rules: 796 dead on arrival, 20 allowlisting far more than they say.
  - **`exceptionRequestUrlMatcher` compiles them against the full request URL**, the same machinery a path block already uses, and the evaluator keeps them in a dedicated index (`pathExceptions`) that a hostname-only `evaluate()` cannot fire — an allowlist is the unsafe direction to widen, unlike a block. A bare `@@word` still refuses the URL route: reading it as a substring allowlist would *widen* it too.
  - **The replay applies them per request, with the documented precedence.** `createReplayDecider` consults `matchPathException` after the zone-exception check and before the path-block one: a matching path exception lets exactly its URLs through, a regular one still loses to an `$important` zone block, and an `$important` one wins it back. On the re-captured URL trace the honest movement is visible: allowlisted hosts fall 86 → 23 as the zone-widened BBC/BBCI exceptions shrink to their paths, the fired-exception count rises 6 → 10 as previously-dropped rules (`@@||cdn.cookielaw.org/consent`, `@@||gstatic.com/recaptcha/`, …) start doing their job, and one path-decided block moves to its exception (Path-decided 17 → 16).
  - 9 new tests pin the contract — both old failure modes, path-exception-beats-path-block, `$important` precedence both directions, the hostname-trace fallback, and the `@@word` non-widening — and the two pins the fix honestly moved (a fixture exception count of zero where every match was the widened reading; a derived hot set of 34, not 35, rules) moved with named reasons rather than silent lowering.
- **First-Party `sponsored` And `advert` Copy No Longer Hides At 98% (`@blockingmachine/core`)**:
  - **A disclosure word was a definitive ad marker on its own.** The container-markup rule treated `sponsored` or `advert` in markup as sufficient evidence to hide — which hid `sponsored-story-300x250` and `first-party-advert-label`, a publisher's own paid content and a house-ad label, at 98% confidence. `destroyedContent`, the counter that exists precisely for content the user came for disappearing, carried both under named allowances.
  - **Disclosure words now need evidence the money is someone else's before they can hide.** `AD_DISCLOSURE_WORDS` — `sponsored`, `advert`, `advertisement`, `advertising` — can no longer be the definitive marker alone and cannot serve as a corroborating hint toward `hide` unless the element carries a delivery attribute, an ad-network resource, a vendor path, or a cross-origin frame. They still *suggest*: the word is real ad vocabulary and pointing is honest; removing a publisher's own revenue block is not something the markup alone can decide. Container nouns and vendor names (`adslot`, `adthrive`, `taboola`…) are unchanged — those make a structural claim, not an editorial one.
  - **The third-party cases kept hiding — on the evidence, not the word.** `sponsored-feed-card`, `sponsored-listing`, and `sponsored-question-module` gained the `data-*` delivery attribute a real sponsored unit ships, so they hide on the same rule rather than on an exemption. `adthrive-wrapper` and `consent-gated-ad-slot` never needed the exception.
  - `DESTROYED_CONTENT_ALLOWANCES` is deleted in both evaluation suites and `destroyedContent` is pinned at `[]`; the two flag-14 cases now `suggest` at 58%. The scored set drops 101→99 as the two ceiling violations move inside their permitted band, and `elementWeights.generated.ts` re-derived on the new evidence (`fittedEce` 0.0873 against reference 0.0864 — inside the margin). Open flag 14 closed.
- **The Harvest Queue No Longer Collapses Two Modules That Share A Token (`@blockingmachine/core`)**:
  - **`div|promo` was one candidate no matter which element it was.** The candidate key was the classifier's signature — its grouping token — so the NYT's `.g-promo-slim` strip and `.live-updates-promo` rail merged into one entry with two sightings, and the second click read as a duplicate of the first. Worse, `event-promo-card` — a case written for `event-promo` — retired the merged pair *and* the Guardian's separate `div|promo`, so one case closed three different modules' reviews across two news sites.
  - **The key is now signature plus leading class-or-id**, and the promoted match runs on the same pair: a corpus case covers a candidate when its signature and leading identifier both match, falling back to bare-signature coverage only when the case names no identifier at all (a tag-carried token like `amp-ad`, where there is no module to distinguish). A case written for one module now closes only that module's review.
  - **The matcher moved into core** as `corpusCaseCoversHarvestProposal`/`harvestProposalIsPromoted` — it used to live untestably in the `.mjs` driver — and matches the band by containment: a leave-only case still drains an element-scoped `keep` proposal, while a weaker written case can no longer pretend to cover a stronger recorded claim.
  - The regenerated artifact shows the honest shape: 11 candidates (the NYT pair split), 6 promoted rather than 8 — the two `div|promo` keeps and the Guardian's await a case that is actually about them. 6 new tests pin the split, both fallback directions, and band containment both ways. Open flag 6 closed.
- **A Harvested Decision Now Records Its Scope, And A Shape-Wide Band Requires A Shape-Wide Choice (`@blockingmachine/core`)**:
  - **A click ruled on one element; the proposal claimed the whole shape.** `proposeHarvestEvalCase` turned any `hide` into `minAction: 'hide'` — "remove this shape everywhere" — and any `keep` into `leave`–`leave`, so pasting the proposal verbatim landed a generalisation nobody checked. The corpus's most expensive claim was the one the evidence covered least.
  - **`HarvestedHumanDecision.scope` records how far the ruling reaches.** Absent means `element` — what one click establishes — and the bands soften honestly: element `hide` proposes `suggest`–`hide` (identify it, don't claim the shape must go) and element `keep` proposes a `suggest` ceiling (hiding is a defect, pointing is not). `scope: 'shape'` — set by a reviewer on the decision in the harvest file, never inferred from sightings or confidence — is the only path to a `hide`–`hide` or `leave`–`leave` proposal. The proposal carries `harvestScope` through, so a promoted case keeps which evidence it was built from.
  - **`alreadyPromoted` matches by containment now.** A corpus case covers a proposal when the case's band sits inside the proposal's — a leave-only written case still drains an element-scoped `keep`, while a `suggest`–`hide` case no longer pretends to cover a `hide`–`hide` claim. The regenerated queue keeps its shape: 8 promoted, 2 awaiting review, with the file's only `hide` now proposing `suggest`–`hide`.
  - The picker deliberately still asks one question — scope is asserted where the evidence is reviewed, the only place it can honestly be answered. 5 new tests including the flag's verify: a one-sighting `hide` cannot raise the must-hide count on its own; an explicit `scope: 'shape'` can. Open flag 7 closed.
- **The Hostname Calibration Gates Are Now Margins Over A Reference Head, The Way The Element Ones Are (`@blockingmachine/core`)**:
  - **The absolute bars survived the metric's moves twice already.** `ece <= 0.15` and `brier <= 0.12` were written against a metric whose scored set has since changed meaning — they stayed green while the denominator and the confusion axis were both corrected, which is exactly the knife-edge failure the element suite stopped carrying when its gates went reference-relative. The numbers meant "no worse than a line someone once drew", not "no worse than the alternative".
  - **The reference is the strongest vocabulary-free head.** `MiniAiClassifier` gained the `weights` option `elementClassifier` already had, and the suite derives `REFERENCE_WEIGHTS` from `MODEL_WEIGHTS` by zeroing every feature that reads a name or a list — keywords, CNAME lookups, safe-infrastructure, brand-spoof — leaving only the shape features to score. It is a real competitor rather than a strawman: 97.7% triage accuracy on the corpus, ECE 0.065 and Brier 0.026, against the shipped head's 0.043 and 0.019. The shipped head is *better* calibrated than the reference, so the margin (`+0.005`, plus a `+0.02` absolute headroom) only binds on regression — a deliberately worse table fails it without any literal being lowered, which is what the flag's verify asked for. Non-degeneracy guards (`reference ece > 0`, `brier > 0`) keep a collapsed reference from vacating the bound.
  - The learned GBDT was tried as the reference first and rejected honestly: trained on a different distribution (list positives vs Majestic negatives), it scores ECE 0.38 on this corpus, and a bound that loose is no bound.
  - 1 new test; the flag-8 verify runs end to end — a sign-negated weight table still triages at 81.5% but fails the margin on both statistics. Open flag 8 closed.
- **The Element Calibration Now Covers The Permitted Band's Edges, And Eleven Permitted-Only Cases Gained A Real Floor (`@blockingmachine/core`)**:
  - **A ceiling was a stated expectation the metric never read.** The 57 permitted-only cases (`minAction` unset, `maxAction` above `leave`) produced no calibration pair whatever the model did — including a `hide` on a `suggest`-capped case, which the corpus states is *forbidden*. `elementActionCalibrationPair` now scores any verdict above its ceiling as `{confidence, 0}`, so a flag-14 destroyed-content hide (`sponsored-story-300x250`, `first-party-advert-label`, both hidden at 98%) is calibration-visible rather than only safety-counted. In-band verdicts on permitted cases stay unscored: there is still no ground truth to grade them against, and padding the average with ungraded passes is the failure the exclusion exists to prevent.
  - **Eleven cases could honestly state a floor.** The rule used for the conversion: where `expected` accepts no `Content` read, restraint is not an acceptable answer — the corpus already claims the element *is* an ad, nag or tracker, so the product must at least point at it. `native-ads-unit`, `newsletter-popup-inline`, `adblock-wall` (now a exactly-`suggest` band: required to point, forbidden to remove), `sticky-promo-bar`, `addthis-widget`, `twitter-share-anchor`, `sticky-share-rail`, `fingerprintjs-on-public-cdn`, `ad-server-cookie-sync`, `cname-masked-ad-server` and `fingerprint-vendor-on-own-cdn` gained `minAction: 'suggest'` — their own notes argue "may suggest, never hide", which implies "must at least suggest". The 46 that keep `Content` in their accepted reads stay permitted: leaving them is a verdict the corpus endorses.
  - **`calibration.overacted` joins `unacted`** so the scored set's composition is fully stated — the report line reads `101 of 220 cases stated an expectation about acting, 8 dodged by staying silent, 2 above their ceiling, all scored as errors`.
  - Re-measured: scored **85 → 101** of 220, Brier 0.0033 → 0.049 (the eight silent requirements and two ceiling violations are charged now), ECE 0.066, delta vs reference +0.0009 — both heads move together because both miss the same cases. `elementWeights.generated.ts` provenance re-derived on the corrected metric.
- **A Must-Hide Case The Element Model Leaves Alone Can No Longer Exit The Calibration Average (`@blockingmachine/core`)**:
  - **The leave-exclusion swallowed exactly the error it was meant to measure.** `elementActionCalibrationPair` scored a `suggest` on a must-hide case as the out-of-band decision it is, but a full `leave` returned `null` — the case left the scored set entirely, so the calibration average shrank (and could *improve*) precisely when the model got worse at the one thing it was required to do. The three `missedHides` allowances the shipped model already leaves alone were that silent case live: `scored` was 85 of 220 with them invisible to the metric.
  - **A required action dodged is a decision made, and it scores like one.** A `leave` below a required band now produces `{predicted: confidence, actual: 0}` — the stated class-confidence charged against the wrong call, so a confident silence costs more than a hesitant one. Unrequired leaves stay unscored: their confidence is a class probability, and correct restraint would only pad the average — the over-padding that made the metric's floor a knife-edge in the first place. The scored set is now co-extensive with *every case the corpus states an expectation about*, verdict regardless, which is what makes it robust without leaning on the `missedHides`/`undersoldHides` pins to carry the load.
  - **`calibration.unacted` says how much of the scored set the model answered with silence.** The report prints it inside the calibration line — `88 of 220 cases stated an expectation about acting, 3 dodged by staying silent, scored as errors` — so the composition of the average is stated rather than implied.
  - Re-measured under the corrected metric: ECE 0.066, Brier 0.020 over 88 scored cases (was 0.038/0.0033 over 85 — the difference is the three silent misses now charged). The reference head is run through the same harness, so the relative gates still hold; `elementWeights.generated.ts` was re-derived so its `actionCalibration` provenance records the corrected numbers. `element-ai-evaluation.test.ts` pins the new contract: a forged must-hide case the model leaves increments `scored` and `unacted` and raises ECE rather than dropping out — flag 5's verify, run end to end. Closes open flag 5.
- **The Hostname Evaluation No Longer Lets A Prediction State Escape The Matrix, And Every Unaccepted Answer Is Named (`@blockingmachine/core`)**:
  - **The class axis was five labels; the model can emit seven.** `evaluation.ts` iterated `Clean`, `Advertising`, `Telemetry/Analytics`, `Consent/Annoyance` and `Malware/Phishing`, so a `CNAME Cloaking` or `Unknown` verdict fell out of the confusion matrix *and* every per-class denominator while still counting in `total` — the confusion cells summed to 216 on a 217-case corpus, and an `Unknown` answer could never register as a false positive anywhere. A model retreating to `Unknown` would have looked *better* in precision while the report quietly lost cases — the same silence the element metric's leave-exclusion had. The axis is now `THREAT_CATEGORIES` itself, so an eighth category can never need a second audit to be seen.
  - **The corpus contradicted itself once.** `px.ads.linkedin.com` was entered as `ad()` at one line and `telemetry()` at another — the model's `Advertising` answer was accepted by one entry and rejected by the other, so the corpus guaranteed a phantom miss, a phantom `Telemetry → Advertising` confusion cell, and a doubled denominator. The corpus's own convention for genuinely overlapping vendors is a multi-label set; the domain is now a single `adOrTelemetry` entry, and the suite refuses any domain labeled two ways.
  - **Accepted alternates were filed as the work queue.** The confusion matrix keys on the first accepted label, so six DGA cases answered `Malware/Phishing` — a label their `[Advertising, Telemetry, Malware]` sets allow — rendered as `↳ Advertising → Malware/Phishing` with example domains, indistinguishable from real blur. Cells now carry an `accepted` count; a cell whose every answer is allowed prints `·` with no example list, and only the unaccepted remainder is the work queue.
  - **The misses nobody listed.** A case filed under the wrong nuisance category is not a false positive, not a false negative at triage level, not a coverage gap and not a lexical miss — `visualwebsiteoptimizer.com → Unknown` lived in `correct`'s complement alone. `exactMisses` now names every unaccepted answer with its reason, the report prints the triage-correct subset under its own heading, and the test pins `exactMisses.length === total − correct`.
  - **The calibration denominator is stated.** Every corpus case produced a pair, but the report never said so, and an empty pair list computes ECE 0 — a vacuous pass if a future filter ever made cases ineligible. `calibration.scored`/`total` are now reported and the gate asserts `scored === total`, matching the element metric's lesson.
  - Re-measured after the audit: 216 cases, exact accuracy 98.6%, triage 99.1%, lexical recall 100% of 88 name-evident threats, macro F1 0.903, ECE 0.043, Brier 0.019 — 216 of 216 scored. Three new gates (domain uniqueness, matrix completeness, the named-miss set) plus the previously vacuous reason assertion made real (`toBeTruthy` — `typeof === 'string'` passed for `''`). `tierVocabulary.generated.ts` re-derived so its recorded corpus verdict matches the corrected measurement. Flag 8's absolute-vs-relative gate concern is unchanged and stays open.
- **Every Flag In `scripts/` Now Refuses A Named Value It Cannot Honour, Instead Of Silently Answering With A Different One (`scripts`, `@blockingmachine/browser-extension`)**:
  - **The loose parser every script carried had one signature: a question asked, answered by a different question.** `--in` followed by nothing — or by another flag — became a bare flag and the script ran on the default it was meant to replace, so `--hits` with no ledger compiled the unmeasured plan the flag exists to ask for. A typo'd flag parsed as a flag nobody read and did nothing, so `--skip-tier` ran the compile it was typed to skip and `--dry-rnu` published for real. `Number('abc')` answered a malformed `--min-days` with `NaN`, which filters every session out and reads as "the exports held nothing" rather than "the flag was unreadable". A stray positional was skipped entirely. Each is the same failure `--residual` already refuses in the compiler, and the audit found it in ten scripts.
  - **`scripts/argv.mjs` is the shared refusing parser** — a value flag at end of argv or followed by a flag-shaped token is refused as a missing value, `--name=` is an explicit empty value rather than an absent one, a boolean carrying `=value` is refused, unknown flags and surplus positionals name themselves in the error, and a bare `--` ends the options so a dash word after it is a positional like any other. Scripts adopt it whole; the three that take positionals (`release`, `bump-version`, the unbound harness) state how many. `parseArgvOrExit` prints the refusal and exits 2 at the top of each script, before anything is read or written — the refusal list in each error names every flag the script does take, so a typo is correctable without opening the source.
  - **The compiler keeps its own parser** — `--input tier_ads=list.txt` is a spec no generic shape expresses — and gained the same contract in it: `--name=value` for every value flag, `-`-shaped values refused, an unknown argument refused by name, and `--input tier_adz=file` refused as a non-tier rather than compiled as a filename. A file genuinely carrying `=` still parses, because a path's separators are what makes its name untier-shaped.
  - **Tags are no longer sanitized into different tags.** `release.mjs --tag` with nothing after it used to fall through to version inference, and `--tag=x!` was rewritten to `x` — publishing under a dist-tag nobody typed. All three publish scripts and `release.mjs` now refuse an explicit tag that is not a valid npm dist-tag spelling; inference from the version only runs when no tag was named.
  - **The two shell harnesses already refused** (`unbound-reachability-live.sh`, `weekly-tier-compile.sh` — `*) exit 2`), and `capture-request-trace.js` is a browser-console paste with no argv: the audit covered them rather than skipping them.
  - 45 new tests: the parser's contract exercised through `node --input-type=module -e` in `scriptArgv.test.ts` (repeats kept, `--name=value`, `--` end-of-options, the empty-value refusal, every unknown-shape class), a refusal probe per script run as a real subprocess, the unbound harness pinned from source since it only runs beside compiled siblings, and four compiler refusal tests in `tierCompiler.test.ts` covering unknown arguments, missing and flag-shaped values on every path flag, `--input`'s non-tier name, and the inline `=` spelling honoured end to end.
- **Every `--json` Path Now Leaves Through One Writer, And One Suite Parses Stdout Across All The Commands (`@blockingmachine/cli`, `scripts`)**:
  - **The contract had one writer and one exception.** The CLI routes `coverage`, `ai-scan`, `ai-crawl` and `tier-plan --json` through `writeJson` — a bare `process.stdout.write` of a pretty-printed document plus a newline, no timestamp, no colour — because a payload through the colourised winston transport is a document that does not parse, and `jsonOutput.test.ts` already pinned it. `compile-tier-rulesets.mjs --json` was outside that fence: its own `console.log(JSON.stringify(...))` produced the same bytes today, which is exactly how every divergent writer looks on day one, and the scan that bans hand-rolled payloads never saw `scripts/`.
  - **`scripts/stdout.mjs` is the second runtime's writer, byte-identical on purpose.** The compiler cannot import the CLI's module — it is a plain `.mjs` that must run before `dist/` exists — so the contract is stated twice in code and once in the test that enforces it: `JSON.stringify(payload, null, 2)` plus a newline on stdout, diagnostics on stderr. The scan in `jsonOutput.test.ts` now reads `scripts/*.mjs` as well as `commands/*.ts`, so a hand-rolled JSON print fails the suite whichever side of the package boundary it lands on — and the writer-existence check pins both modules so the ban cannot be satisfied by deleting the feature.
  - **`--check --json` was a silently ignored flag.** The check path returned before the `--json` branch, so the document a CI job asked for was answered with the human line — the same silent-replacement class as the argv audit, one level up. The check verdict is now a payload through the same writer: `{ check: 'compiled-plan', ok, staleTiers, total, budget }` when a compilation is recorded, `{ check: 'curated-baseline', ok, problems, declared }` when it is not — the two checks are different questions and the document says which one ran. Diagnostics stay on stderr, where a pipe cannot hear them.
  - **The shared stdout-parsing test now spans the boundary.** `jsonOutput.test.ts` runs the compiler as a real subprocess and feeds its stdout through the same `parseJsonStdout` the CLI commands are held to — document shape, no colour, nothing wrapped around it, stdout empty on a refusal — plus the `--check --json` verdict pinned fresh and stale end to end. The `--json`-declaring command list is derived from the directory rather than written down, so a command added tomorrow with the option and no writer call fails the suite without the suite having to know it exists.
  - 3 new tests beside the existing seven: the compiler's `--json` parsed as a pipe would read it (a real compile, its own numbers in the document), a refusal leaving stdout untouched, and `--check --json` reporting `curated-baseline` on a compilationless checkout then `compiled-plan` naming the tampered tier after one.
- **The Weekly Cut Now Has A Real Ledger To Weight It, And Two Things That Made Every Run Worthless Were Found By Running It (`@blockingmachine/browser-extension`)**:
  - **`ledger/ledger-hits.txt` shipped empty**, so the `--hits` ranking the packaging step asks for was never used and the 30,000-rule cut was decided by concatenation order. `scripts/ledger-browser-run.mjs` is the producer: it drives a real Chrome over the DevTools Protocol with the unpacked extension, browses a scripted page list, and exports **the extension's own ledger format** — the same file the popup's *Export hit ledger* button produces, built from `hitLedgerSessions` plus the open `hitLedgerActiveTally` — which `ledger:merge` then folds into the dropbox. Four runs — three of 46 pages and one of 20 (**158 pages**) — gave **1,733 hits across 57 distinct rules**, and the fourth also carries the browser's own per-tier split: `tier_privacy` 60, `tier_ads` 25, `tier_core` 24, `tier_annoyances` 10, `tier_unclassified` 6. `npm run tiers:weekly` is no longer a no-op.
  - **`--load-extension` loads nothing on this machine.** Chrome started that way exposed only its own component extension and no service worker for ours, so a run would have browsed every page with nothing installed and reported an empty ledger as a finding about the network. The extension is now installed through the browser session's `Extensions.loadUnpacked`, and everything downstream is keyed on the id it returns rather than on a URL shape — matching on the *shape* picks one of Chrome's own component extensions, which is what the first version did.
  - **A default install enables `tier_core` alone, so five of the six tiers could not be measured.** A rule that is not installed cannot match: the first successful run produced **16 distinct rules, every one of them `tier_core`** — a ledger that says nothing about the five tiers the tier plan exists to weigh. The script now enables every shipped tier and *verifies* it before the first page loads, which is also the state the plan is about: the overflow case where the static budget is the constraint.
  - **A third thing, found by reading storage rather than code:** the open tally is persisted only every 25 hits, so a short run leaves it in the worker's memory and writes nothing. The export normalises the tally's `Record<rule, count>` into the list a closed session carries, and says so when a run falls under the threshold instead of emitting an empty file.
  - **The provenance is written down, because flag 25's objection was to a ledger that would be indistinguishable from a real one.** This is an automated session over the script's own site list, not a person's browsing — and the file still holds `<count> <rule>` and nothing else: no URL, no title, no timestamp, no initiator. It also ran on a machine with **AdGuard on the host and AdGuard Home on the LAN**. Neither can *add* a match, because a request the extension blocks is decided before any lookup happens; both can reduce the third-party traffic a page loads, so the totals are depressed relative to a clean network. The ranking between rules is unaffected and the absolute volumes are not, and `ledger/README.md` says exactly that.
  - **What it does not claim, and flag 25's closure says so too:** one automated afternoon on one day carries **no recurrence across days**, which is the thing a ledger is for. And on the current input the ranking **promotes 0 hosts** — all 57 hit rules already shipped, and the tiers that fired were not capacity-constrained, so 16,373 rules ship with or without the ledger. The mechanism is exercised end to end; the evidence is thin, in the specific ways recorded.
- **A Watch Tick In The Window Between A Compile And The Resolver's Next Fetch Announced A Regression That Was About To Fix Itself (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - **`stale` means the resolver's copy is older than the last compile**, which is true and entirely expected between a compile and the resolver's next fetch. Unbound has no remote-list feature, so that fetch is a cron entry on the resolver host — an hourly one, a daily one, whatever the operator wrote. So someone who compiled at 09:00 with a resolver that fetches at 09:05 was notified at 09:00:15 about a deployment working exactly as configured, and the next tick found it current again with nothing to say. That is the worse half of a feature whose value is being believed.
  - **The grace is one cadence, measured from the compile.** From the compile rather than from how many times the watch has looked, because the question is whether the resolver's next fetch has had a chance to happen. A cadence rather than a *learned* refresh interval, and that is a deliberate choice: the interval the operator configured lives in a cron entry this hub never reads, and inferring it from observed fetch gaps is a guess from a history that may be one sample long. A guess in the long direction is a **missed** alert rather than an extra one, and this alert is cheap to miss once and expensive to miss for a week. `unboundWatchStaleGraceMs` clamps whatever it is given, so a stored `0` cannot turn it into an immediate announcement.
  - **Holding an announcement on the transition would have meant never making it.** The second and third observations of the same finding are `stale` to `stale`, which the no-repeat rule rejects — so the grace had to be carried by state rather than by suppression. `staleAnnouncedAt` is stamped on the snapshot the announcement leaves behind, `index.ts` folds it into the one it persists, and `classifyUnboundReachability` clears it when the deployment is current again so the next regression can speak. A deployment that went stale, recovered and went stale again a month later would otherwise stay silent for ever because its own past looked like a finding already spoken about.
  - **`not-loaded` is deliberately unaffected.** A resolver answering normally for a name its own file blocks is never the expected state, so there is no window in which it is a false positive and it still fires on first observation. That asymmetry is why half of this feature can be trusted without a configured refresh interval, and the tests pin both halves separately rather than leaving the rule as one sentence.
  - **`compiledAt` was added to the verdict** because a snapshot keeps only the verdict and the watch needs the same fact the check used. A `stale` copy ten minutes behind a compile the resolver has not fetched yet and one ten minutes behind a compile it never will are the same string otherwise.
  - 12 new tests: one minute after a compile is silence and one cadence is not; a finding persisting across ticks announces **once**, with the stamp the caller must remember; the boundary is inclusive; a compile dated in the future counts as elapsed (a skewed clock must not hold an alert for ever); an unparseable or absent date announces rather than suppresses, because absence of evidence the window passed is not evidence it has; and the wiring is pinned from source, since `index.ts` cannot be imported without starting Electron.
- **The Hub Reported A Working Unbound Deployment As Broken, And `export -f unbound` Wrote A File Unbound Cannot Load (`@blockingmachine/cli`, `@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - **Two defects found by the live verification run, both with the same root: this project had two answers to one question.** Unbound has two documented ways to block a local zone. `always_nxdomain` — what core emits — answers **NXDOMAIN**. `redirect` + `local-data "…" A 0.0.0.0` answers **NOERROR carrying an address that routes nowhere**. Both block. The Hub's reachability check read only the first, so a deployment running the CLI's artifact was reported `not-loaded` with the advice *“confirm the `include:` line and that a reload has run”* — for a resolver that had the include, had reloaded, and was blocking 110,463 domains. The advice would change nothing, and the user would never learn the file they deployed was working.
  - **`export -f unbound` never reached a formatter at all.** `ExportCommand`'s `switch` grouped `privoxy`, `bind`, `bind-null`, `domains` and `plain` and left `unbound` out, so it fell to the `default` arm: the AdGuard `!` header followed by raw browser rules, in a file named `filter-list.unbound`. `unbound-checkconf` rejected that with **43 errors** (`unknown keyword '||ads.example.com^'`, the `@@||child…^` exception passed through verbatim) and the command still reported `success: true` — a file that looks produced, carries the format's name, and blocks nothing because it cannot be loaded. `unbound` is now in the group, and the CLI's own formatter **delegates to core's** rather than restating the rule: one artifact, not two, which is the same fix the `bind` pair builder already got when its parallel formatter drifted once.
  - **The check learned the second blocking syntax, which is what rescues deployments already running it.** A canary answered NOERROR carrying `0.0.0.0`, `::`, `127.0.0.1` or `::1` is now *blocking* — and it is held to exactly the same evidence NXDOMAIN is: no reference resolver and it is `canary-unconfirmed`, a reference that also cannot resolve it and it is `canary-nonexistent`. `every` address must be a sinkhole address, so one real address beside `0.0.0.0` is `not-loaded`, which is the honest direction to be wrong in. The converse is caught too: a resolver whose **control** query is sinkholed (`local-zone: "." redirect`) answers the canary identically, and accepting that would be the exact failure the control query exists to prevent — so it is `inconclusive`.
  - **The sentence names what the resolver actually answered.** `describeAnswer` reports `NOERROR with 0.0.0.0 (blocked)` rather than `resolves to 0.0.0.0`, so the pane, the notification and a user's own `dig` output agree. Claiming NXDOMAIN for an answer that carried an address would be a claim the wire does not support.
  - 9 new reachability tests — the predicate asserted directly across every address and state, the two evidence paths, the stale variant, the mixed-address refusal, and the sinkholed control — plus the artifact assertions in `commands.test.ts`: `local-zone … always_nxdomain` present, `local-data` and browser rules absent, `server:` present, and the exception as a `transparent` zone. [docs/unbound-reachability-live.md](../docs/unbound-reachability-live.md) keeps the original finding as written, with the resolution noted above it, because the rig's `cli-artifact` scenario exists so this can be re-measured rather than taken on trust.
- **Four Defects From The Flag List, Three Of Which Made A Healthy Machine Look Broken (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`, `@blockingmachine/browser-extension`)**:
  - **The promotion gate could never pass its own coverage check, because nothing wrote the record it reads.** `gate.py`'s `shadow_scored` needs `>= 1000` domains scored, and `shadow.py` derives that number from per-sweep `summary` records **and from nothing else**. The kind was documented in `shadow.py` and written by `synth_shadow.py`; only the shipping app omitted it. The consequence was specific and total: a sweep that scored 30,000 domains and agreed with production about every one of them left **no trace at all**, so the gate read `0 domains scored (min 1000)` however long the app had been running. The app now appends one summary per sweep that scored at least one domain, carrying the counters `runShadowComparison` actually returned — a summary that disagreed with its own sweep would be worse than a missing one.
  - **The summary carries the one field it cannot currently fill honestly, and says so.** `modelVersion` is the promotion version from `manifest.json` when the shipped weights have one and **`null`** when they do not, which is the state today — the shipped export predates the ceremony. A summary that named a version nothing could verify would let a gate report present unversioned evidence as though it identified a model. So `coverage()` splits its totals and `gate.py` prints the split: *2,100 domains scored (min 1000); 1,200 of 2100 scored by weights with no promotion manifest (model version unknown)*. Excluding unversioned sweeps would have made the ceremony impossible to start; reporting them without saying so would make a count mean more than it does.
