  - **`coverage()['days']` no longer counts a sweep that scored nothing.** An empty watchdog tick produced a summary of `evaluated: 0`, and the day-count is derived from record timestamps — so a day on which the model saw nothing at all would have been presented to the gate as a day of shadow. "We ran" is not coverage, so the app writes nothing for an empty sweep.
  - **A hostname or an IPv6 address made the reachability check throw instead of answering.** `dns.Resolver.setServers` takes an IP only, and that call sat one line above the `try` — so a router-hosted Unbound behind a name, which is one of the two likeliest things an operator types, left the IPC handler as an unhandled rejection rather than a verdict. Unbracketed IPv6 was worse, because it failed quietly: `::1:5353` parsed as host `::1:5353` on port 53, queried an address that cannot exist, and reported `refused`. Both now return a `resolver-unreachable` verdict with the same `headline`/`nextStep` shape as every other outcome and the reason — what was asked, what Node's resolver accepts — in `detail`.
  - **Eight buttons on the element panel's most destructive action had one identical accessible name.** Each group's `Hide` button announced as `Hide`, so a screen-reader user heard the same word eight times with nothing to tell them apart, and the button list was meaningless out of context. The name now carries what is being hidden (`Hide 12 matching elements`).
  - **The one nondeterministic input in the electron suite, and the failure it could not be credited with.** `radar-heat-map.test.ts` drew from `Math.random()`, so its heat-map fixture changed per run; it now uses a seeded counter. This is recorded as a **candidate, not a cause**: one run in twenty failed on a single test and the output was lost, and nothing here can prove the two were related. That is open flag 29's text, kept as it was written rather than quietly upgraded to a fix.
  - 5 new tests for the summary record (the counters equal the returned ones; the version is `null` today; the record's key set is **exactly** the five fields and holds no hostname, which is what makes it safe to write unconditionally; an empty sweep writes none; three sweeps write three, because the log is append-only and three Python scripts read it). The two assertions in the sample suite that counted "records that are not samples" were corrected rather than left passing — the log holds three shapes now, and a counter that included the summary was reading it as one more domain scored.
- **`--shares` And `--budget` Both Answered A Value The Operator Named With A Different One, And `--shares` Answered Its Own Documented Spelling With Nothing At All (`@blockingmachine/browser-extension`)**:
  - **The flags decide which 30,000 rules ship.** `compile-tier-rulesets.mjs --shares` sets each tier's cap on the MV3 static-rule budget and `--budget` sets the total, so these are the packaging decision the whole pipeline exists to get right, argued for in the header and configured with flags. `--shares` was the last branch in `parseArgs` with the shape `--residual`, `--hits`, `--attribution` and `--security` had already given up: `if (TIER_IDS.includes(tier)) options.shares[tier] = …` drops anything it does not recognise and says nothing.
  - **Three ways to be ignored, all silent.** `--shares privacyy=40` was dropped outright. `--shares tier_ads=60,privacy=10` applied the first half and dropped the second, because the parser split on `:` while every example in the repository is written with `=` — so the *documented* form did nothing at all, and only `tier_ads:60` had ever worked. `--shares tier_ads=abc` set the tier's share to `0`, which is a legal value: the plan that came out was a real plan with the ads tier starved, not an error.
  - **It now refuses, and refuses the whole argument.** Every part is validated before any of it is applied, because applying the valid half of `tier_ads=60,privacyy=10` is the same silent answer one clause further along — and a half-applied split is the hardest kind to notice later, because it looks deliberate. Names go through `normalizeTierName`, so the prefix-less spelling `--residual` accepts is accepted here too, and a value that is not a whole number of points is refused rather than becoming zero. Both separators parse: `=` is what the docs and `--input` use, `:` is what this parser used to split on, and breaking an existing invocation to fix a typo would be a second silent no-op.
  - **`--budget` had the same shape, and `parseInt` made it worse.** `--budget abc`, `--budget 0`, `--budget -5` and a bare `--budget` all compiled on Chrome's guaranteed 30,000 — a different number than the one asked for, delivered as a working plan. The nastier one: `parseInt('1e6')` is **1**, so a request for a million rules produced a one-rule plan, and the run only failed later and for an unrelated reason (curated hosts that would not fit). The post-hoc guard that reset the value has been removed rather than narrowed, because with every `--budget` now validated there is nothing left for it to repair.
  - **`--shares` is in the `--help` header.** It was the one flag on this script whose syntax nothing in the repository stated — the header comment *is* the help output, by design, which is why the ambiguity between the two separators survived this long. The header now says what a share is (points out of a hundred, scaled to whatever total the given tiers add up to, so `core=40,ads=24` is a two-way split rather than a two-tier budget) and what is refused.
  - 4 new integration tests beside the `--residual` ones in `tierCompiler.test.ts`, run against the real compiler: a misspelled tier and a partly-valid one both exit 1 with the valid tiers named and no tier file touched; eight bad values across the two flags are refused by one parameterised assertion each, so a failure says which spelling broke; and the accepted forms are pinned by *effect* rather than by a return value — `tier_ads=100,tier_privacy=1` inverts the default split, `ads=100,privacy=1` produces the byte-identical plan, the reversed pair favours privacy, and `tier_ads:60` still moves the split rather than being ignored. Suite 44, all green.
- **The M5 Drift Stage Had No Input, Because the Sampling Rate Was an Implicit Default (`@blockingmachine/core`, `@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - The learned GBDT ships in shadow mode and logs every disagreement between itself and production to `learned-shadow.jsonl`. The promotion gate's drift stage does not read those. Disagreements are by definition the domains where this model differs from the lists, so a population of exceptions says nothing about what normal traffic looks like, and PSI over it cannot tell a distribution shift from a coincidence. `drift.py` reads the *sample* records instead — a random slice of everything scored — and it exits with **"no sample records in the shadow log"** while the app looks completely healthy, which is the worst shape a missing input can have.
  - **The slice was never switched on, and the reason was a default parameter.** `shadowScoreWatchdogDomains` took `sampleRate = 0`, and the watchdog hook called it with two arguments. Every sweep ran, every disagreement was logged, and the drift stage read a log that contained no samples by construction. `sampleRate` is now a **required** argument with no default, so TypeScript refuses a call site that has not stated a rate, and the hook passes `LEARNED_SHADOW_SAMPLE_RATE` (0.01) explicitly. A rate nobody chose cannot quietly be zero.
  - **The rate is one named constant with the arithmetic behind it, not a magic number in a call.** PSI bins each feature into ten buckets and compares production proportions against the baseline, so the smallest bucket needs enough observations to be comparable at all, and `gate.py` is sized at `min_shadow_days: 7` / `min_shadow_scored: 1000`. A lower rate does not make the app more private — it makes the bin empty and the gate un-runnable — so the shipped value is the conservative end of "enough" rather than the smallest number that produces a file. The library's own default stays 0, because a library that logs a slice of everything by default is a library that cannot be used for anything else; the app states its rate.
  - **What the log holds is now written down, and the document is held to the log.** [docs/learned-shadow-privacy.md](../docs/learned-shadow-privacy.md) states the file's location per platform, the exact field list, and what is deliberately absent: no URL or path (the DNS log has one per query, the sample keeps the host), no client identity, no page content, nothing transmitted — the file is read offline by the training scripts on the machine that wrote it. A hostname can still be sensitive, which is the reason the rate is a number a person can find and a file a person can delete, and the reason the document does not pretend the disclosure is nothing.
  - **Sampling is independent of the disagreement check, and that is the whole mechanism.** `runShadowComparison` draws the sample for every evaluated domain regardless of verdict, so a sampled disagreement is written twice (once per kind) and the slice stays unbiased. The test that would have caught the original bug measures both kinds being present in the slice, and would fail at zero if sampling were ever gated on disagreement.
  - 11 new tests (electron-app 337, up from 326; no core change). They run the real shipped weights and the real log writer into a temp directory, mocking only Electron's `app`, and they pin: the rate is 0.01 and a fraction; a 10,000-domain sweep writes the sample slice; agreement-only records are a real share of it; a rate of 0 writes none; a sample record's key set is **exactly** the seven documented fields, so a field added for debugging convenience fails the suite; no URL, path or client key can appear; the log lands inside `userData`; and a fresh module registry with unresolvable weights returns null and writes nothing, rather than logging a sample it cannot score. The synthetic sweep is a grid of plausible hostnames, not `host0.example0.com` — the shipped model blocks the latter at 0.996, which made every domain a disagreement and turned the independence test into a measurement of the fixture; a test now asserts the fixture really is a mix so that cannot recur silently.
  - **The next thing that will stop the ceremony is recorded rather than discovered later.** `shadow.py` documents a third record kind, a per-sweep `summary` counter, that nothing writes — so `coverage().scored_domains` is always `0` and `gate.py`'s `shadow_scored` check fails at `min 1000` however long the app has run. That is open flag 11. The stale instruction in `drift.py`'s own error message ("Enable sampleRate in the Electron shadow hook") now says what reaching that state actually means: no sweep has written yet.
- **The Privoxy Recipe Now Describes A Local-File Deployment, Because `actionsfile` Never Took A URL (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - **The recipe's core instruction was a directive the manual does not document.** The pane rendered `actionsfile http://192.168.1.x:9191/privoxy.action` and told the user Privoxy "fetches a remote actions file itself and re-reads it on its own schedule." The Privoxy manual documents the directive's value as a file name resolved against the config directory, in both versions checked — a feed address in it resolves as a literal filename, matches nothing, and the proxy starts normally with no rules loaded. That is open flag 19, which recorded the suspicion because the parser could not be read from this machine; the rewrite takes the manual as authoritative, which produces the correct recipe under the documented semantics either way.
  - **The pane is now BIND-shaped: compile, copy, point, restart.** The feed box showing a URL to subscribe to is gone; in its place is the action-file name the deployment uses end to end (what the feed serves, what the copy saves it as, what `actionsfile` names), a `curl -fsSL <feed> -o /etc/privoxy/<name>` copy command that keeps the hub feed earning its place as transport, the `actionsfile <name>` config line, and an explicit `sudo systemctl restart privoxy` — the reload is explicit because Privoxy reads action files at startup and has no reload signal, so replacing the file while it runs leaves the old rules in effect.
  - **The steps state the contract the old wording denied.** `PRIVOXY_STEPS` is now `compile → copy → actionsfile → reload → ordering`: the copy step names the config directories a packaged install actually uses, the `actionsfile` step says outright that the value is not a URL ("a feed address in it is the deployment that reads as plausible and loads nothing"), and the reload step exists because the file is only read at startup. The ordering note and the leading-dot explanation are unchanged.
  - **The BIND contrast is corrected rather than left flattering the wrong target.** `bindDeploy.ts` described itself as "unlike the Privoxy and Shadowrocket targets, where the client re-fetches on its own" — half of which was never true. The contrast is now drawn against Shadowrocket alone, and the module comment names the two local-file recipes as having the same shape.
  - **The directive-vocabulary gate now covers the new payload**, and found a flaw in itself doing so: `curl -fsSL …` and `actionsfile file` are the same `word args` shape, so a strict statement-first classifier judged commands as undocumented config statements. Lines now collect every grammar shape they match and pass when any reading is documented — a fix to the checker's logic, not an exemption for these commands.
  - 11 tests rewritten or extended across `privoxyDeploy.test.ts`, `deployPanes.test.ts` and `deployRecipeDirectives.test.ts`; flag 19 is closed in `docs/open-flags.md` with the honest caveat recorded — Privoxy's parser was still never read, and the live `show-status` check the flag asked for remains undone, so what shipped is the recipe the documentation describes.
- **Every Other Deploy Hub Recipe Was Audited Against Its Target's Documentation, And Three More Were Wrong The Same Way (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - **The dnsmasq cron recipe was inert twice over.** The pane told OpenWrt users to `curl` the feed into `/etc/dnsmasq.d/blockingmachine.conf` and run `reload`. dnsmasq(8) is explicit — *"SIGHUP does NOT re-read the configuration file"* — so the downloaded conf was never applied, and `/etc/dnsmasq.d` is not OpenWrt's confdir anyway (the generated config uses `/tmp/dnsmasq.d`, per-instance on current builds, and the jailed daemon cannot see `/etc` at all). The recipe now fetches a **hosts-format** file into an `addn-hosts` path — the one input SIGHUP does re-read — so `reload` is finally the honest verb. OpenWrt needs no setup at all (its generated config already carries `addn-hosts=/tmp/hosts`); other dnsmasq hosts add one `addn-hosts=` line once.
  - **AdGuard Home does not take `file://`.** The pane offered a "native file:// path" as a blocklist source; AGH's `validateFilterURL` accepts http/https or a bare absolute path matching `filtering.safe_fs_patterns` — a `file://` URL is rejected outright ("only http https allowed"). The button now copies the bare path and names the real constraint: AGH must run on this filesystem and the path must match a safe pattern (`data/userfilters` is covered by default).
  - **`unbound.conf` is regenerated on two of the three Unbound flavours.** The recipe said "add the `include:` line to unbound.conf" for all of them. On OPNsense that file is rebuilt from templates — the include belongs in a `/usr/local/etc/unbound.opnsense.d/` drop-in (or pfSense's Custom options box); on OpenWrt the UCI-generated config appends `/etc/unbound/unbound_ext.conf`, which is where the line goes. On Debian/Ubuntu the line is unnecessary — the shipped `unbound.conf` already picks up `unbound.conf.d/*.conf` via `include-toplevel`, so a manual include would parse the file twice. `UnboundTarget` now carries `includePlacement` per flavour.
  - **Smaller wording corrections that were not full defects:** the hosts pane's flush command (`killall -HUP mDNSResponder`) was labelled "macOS & Linux" — mDNSResponder does not exist on Linux, so the tag is now macOS-scoped and the step names `resolvectl flush-caches` for systemd-resolved; Shadowrocket's "re-fetches on its own schedule" now says the accurate thing — re-fetch on open, background refresh when iOS is allowed it; AdGuard Desktop's `file://` label now names AdGuard for Mac / Mini (where the subscription loader strips the scheme — verified in its source) rather than implying Windows accepts it.
  - **The dnsmasq pane gained the format warning its siblings already had** — every consumer it names reads hosts-format lists, so a non-`hosts` export now warns instead of silently feeding `||x^` rules to a parser that cannot read them.
  - **What the audit could not do is recorded, not presumed done.** These are documentation-faithful corrections verified against manuals and source (`validateFilterURL`, AdGuard Mini's subscription loader, OpenWrt's package README, OPNsense docs); none ran against a live daemon. That ceiling is open flag 42 — a live rehearsal per target, the shape the Unbound reachability check already proves.
  - 15 tests new or updated (`dnsmasqDeploy.test.ts` is new; unbound `includePlacement` pinned per flavour; `resolvectl`/`systemd-resolve` added to the directive checker's generic command vocabulary; a dnsmasq statement floor added so the `addn-hosts=` directive cannot silently disappear).
- **The AdGuard Home Pane Now Shows The Direct-Mode Warning It Never Had (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - Settings has always warned when a direct-mode URL cannot work — the address is Home Assistant's web frontend (port 8123 or an HA UI path, which answers with a login page, not an API) or a Nabu Casa cloud URL (which only proxies HA itself). The Deploy Hub's AdGuard Home pane offered the same URL field with no warning, so the mistake Settings guards against was silently repeatable in the pane.
  - The amber banner is now `src/components/AdGuardDirectWarning.tsx`, rendered by both surfaces. The component computes the warning from the URL and port itself rather than taking it as a prop, so the two call sites cannot drift on *when* it shows — only on where it sits. In Settings it keeps its place under the port field; in the pane it sits between the URL/port row and the credentials row.
  - `adGuardDirectWarning.test.ts` pins the two failing shapes and the two quiet ones, and pins the sharing at the source level: both files must render `<AdGuardDirectWarning` and neither may carry the banner's inline styles — a copied `<div>` would render identically and drift silently. `deployPanes.test.ts` gains the render-level pin: the warning appears in the pane under a direct-mode HA-frontend or Nabu Casa URL, and does not fire when the same 8123 address is used under HA REST API mode, where that is the right address.
- **Features With Working Backends But No Reachable Surface Are Now Reachable (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`, `@blockingmachine/browser-extension`)**:
  - **The element-corpus harvest had a complete pipeline and no door into it.** `get-element-harvest`/`select-element-harvest`/`clear-element-harvest` were registered in the main process and typed through preload since the harvest shipped, but no renderer ever invoked them — the documented Settings pane did not exist. Settings now carries an Element Corpus Harvest card that reports the chosen file's records, hosts, labelled decisions, rejected lines, newest capture and would-queue count, distinguishes a missing chosen file from no file chosen, and offers select plus forget-&-delete actions over the existing IPC.
  - **Mini-AI feedback could be taught but never untaught — and the first fix landed on an unreachable path.** `tuneMiniAiFeedback` is wired into the Rule & AI Inspector's feedback row, and `reset-mini-ai-feedback` has had a main-process handler all along — but nothing called it, so a mistuned domain could only be fixed by wiping the store. A Forget Learned action now sits beside Flag as Threat / Mark as Safe in the Rule & AI Inspector's feedback row — the view `App` actually navigates inspections to — and reports "Forgotten" vs "Nothing stored" from the handler's `removed` flag rather than claiming a delete that did not happen. (`ruleInspectorFeedback.test.ts` pins the button to the reachable view: an affordance wired only into the Radar's local inspector panel never renders, because the app hands the Radar an `onNavigateInspector` callback and leaves that view.)
  - **The popup's Home Assistant card took a URL and token on faith.** `TEST_HA_CONNECTION` and `haBridge.testConnection` existed but no message ever invoked them. A Test connection button inside the card probes the form's unsaved URL and token — catching a bad pair before Save writes it — and reports through the existing inline note.
  - **The export card now says when the ledger lost evidence.** Every session reports its own `rulesDropped`, the per-session cap's eviction count, and the exported JSON carried it — but the card's summary never aggregated it, so a file that shed its cold tail presented identically to a complete one. `LedgerExportPayload.rulesDropped` sums it across the included sessions and the card renders a Dropped at cap row only when the sum is non-zero. Pinned by a payload-aggregation test in `ledgerSession.test.ts` and a render pair in `ledgerExportCard.test.ts`.
  - **The extension's jest mapper missed `@blockingmachine/core/tiers`.** `ledgerExport.ts` imports `isTierId` from the package — which resolved to the ESM `dist` build Jest cannot `require`, failing every suite that touched the file. The tier vocabulary now maps to source like the other core entries, so a tier rename cannot leave the ledger counting against a stale list.
  - What the audit left alone: `update-*`/`receive`/`removeAllListeners`/`removeProcessProgressListener`/`set-sources` are preload stubs with no main-process handler (the auto-updater is not implemented); `get/set-filter-sources` shadow the `getSources`/`saveSources` the app actually uses; `ADD_CUSTOM_RULE`, `RESET_RULE_HIT_STATS` and `GET_EXTENSION_STATUS` stay API-only — free-text rules bypass the hub's source of truth, resetting measured stats destroys evidence, and the status aggregate duplicates what the popup composes.
- **The Compile's 90% Stall Was Quadratic Exception Resolution (`@blockingmachine/core`)**:
  - `resolveDnsPrecedence` scanned the whole exception map for every block rule, then re-allocated the entire active-block map once per exception, then nested effective exceptions over active blocks — three O(blocks × exceptions) passes inside the "Generating filter lists…" stage every DNS-format export reaches. On a ~190k-rule compile with a few thousand `@@` rules that is hundreds of millions of string comparisons plus per-exception copies of a ~190k-entry array: the UI sat at 90% doing nothing a progress callback could report through.
  - Every one of those scans was a suffix-cover check, and only a domain's own ancestor zones can cover it. The three loops now walk the label chain (`a.b.example.com` → `b.example.com` → `example.com` → `com`) against `Map` lookups — O(domains × labels) instead of O(blocks × exceptions) — with a precomputed `importantActiveBlocks` set so the override pass needs no modifier re-parse. Semantics are unchanged: an important covering exception still outranks non-important ones, the most specific ancestor still wins, and `subdomainExceptions` still records every blocked ancestor.
  - Measured on 190k blocks × ~2,600 exceptions: the old exception-covering scan alone took ~16s (the full old resolve carries two further scans of the same order) against ~0.45s for the complete new resolve — over 35× on the hot path. The full 47-suite core corpus passes unchanged, and a side-by-side replay of both implementations over the same rule set produces identical output.
- **Concurrent Syncs Could Race The Browser's Rule Budget, And A Refused Apply Logged As A Zero-Rule Success (`@blockingmachine/browser-extension`)**:
  - `onInstalled`, the worker-start reconcile, the live stream and the popup's manual sync could all call `syncAndApplyRules` in the same window with nothing coordinating them. Two syncs fetched and ingested the same ~230k-rule list in parallel, then both applied: the second apply's `getDynamicRules` ran before the first committed, so it planned its batch on top of a full family and the browser refused the entire update for exceeding the dynamic-rule cap. The refused call returned 0 and the caller logged "Successfully applied 0 dynamic DNR network rules" — claiming an empty ruleset while the first apply's rules were still installed.
  - Syncs are now single-flight (`syncInFlight`): a caller that asks mid-sync joins the in-flight run — it is fetching the same feed, so "the latest" is the same list either way — and the reference clears when the run settles. Applies are now serialized on `applyChain`: every caller queues behind the last so no two applies can interleave their read-the-rules / write-the-rules pair, whether triggered by a sync, a site pause or a hand-saved rule. Ordering is preserved, so a later decision still lands on the earlier apply's committed state.
  - The success log is gated on `lastApplyError === null`: `applyCurrentRules` returns 0 both for a refused batch and a genuine empty install, and only the flag tells them apart. A refused batch now prints only the warning it already did, and `lastAppliedRuleCount` keeps the last *successful* install's count rather than being overwritten with 0. `syncConcurrencyWiring.test.ts` pins all three gates.
- **A Refused Clipboard Write Crashed The Renderer Into The Dev Overlay (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - Four views called `navigator.clipboard.writeText` bare — Deploy & Sync's Copy buttons, Rule Browser, Modules and the Rule & AI Inspector — so the DOM permission model's `NotAllowedError` refusal became an unhandled rejection the webpack overlay renders as "Uncaught runtime errors" over the whole window.
  - Every Copy affordance now routes through `copyTextToClipboard` (`src/clipboard.ts`): the preload's `copyToClipboard` bridge first — Electron's `clipboard` module and the `copy-to-clipboard` IPC handler have no DOM permission gate — with the DOM API only as a fallback when the bridge is absent. The helper returns a boolean, and every call site now shows its "Copied!" indicator only on success instead of claiming a write that was refused.
  - The preload bridge itself was made honest along the way: it previously fire-and-forgot the IPC invoke with `.catch(() => {})`, so a main-process failure was unreportable; it now returns the handler's `{ success }` result. `clipboard.test.ts` covers the preference order, both refusal paths and the never-reject contract.
- **The AI threat feed published flagged-but-unproven domains, and old verdicts never expired (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`, `@blockingmachine/core`)**:
  - **Both auto-quarantine gates were wrong in opposite directions.** The watchdog sweep read `autoQuarantineEntropyDga` inverted — `if (!autoQuarantine) return true` quarantined *every* flagged domain when the toggle was off — while the live radar's `autoQuarantine ||` quarantined every flagged domain when it was on. Neither gate could ever be satisfied with itself: one side of the toggle always bypassed the evidence bar entirely. That is how conf-70 single-signal trackers like `windsurf-telemetry.codeium.com`, `use1-turn.fpjs.io`, `sentry.found.com` and RFC-2606 fixture hosts like `beacon.example.com` entered the ledger, and once stored they fed `ai-threats.txt` forever — nothing re-judged an old verdict.
  - **One shared gate now (`quarantineGate.shouldAutoQuarantine`).** Quarantine requires a confident verdict (≥85%, matching the feed's own published threshold) or a critical rating; the toggle widens the gate to exactly what its label says — DGA/high-entropy heuristic detections — and nothing else. A risk of `high` at moderate confidence is a review lead on the radar, not a published verdict.
  - **Quarantined verdicts are re-judged at every launch.** `revalidateQuarantine` re-screens the persisted store against the current classifier and releases entries it now reports clean — the sweep that clears out verdicts recorded before the infrastructure guard shipped, or under either broken gate. Inspector-sourced rows (a person's own choice) and entries the rescan cannot judge are always kept, and the write re-reads the store so a sweep's new additions can't be clobbered by a stale snapshot.
  - **Vendor/platform tables caught up to the real misses:** `cursorvm.com` (Cursor's agent-VM pods), `windsurf.com`, Elastic's hosted zones (`elastic.co`, `elastic.cloud`, `found.com`, `found.io`, `found.no`) and `vercel-dns-016.com` — each was quarantined in a real deployment before the fix. `paypal.workers.dev`/`metamask.glitch.me`-class brand squats on third-party hosting still flag, deliberately — that is a genuine phish pattern, not a miss.
  - `quarantineGate.test.ts` covers the gate's four quadrants (clean, confident/critical, high-but-unproven, toggle-gated entropy) and the sweep's keep/evict contracts including the can't-judge and inspector-owned carve-outs.

### Changed
- **The Weight Fit's Regulariser Is Now Chosen Between Kinds — The Prior Had To Beat Early Stopping, Not Just Its Own Absence (`@blockingmachine/core`, `scripts`)**:
  - **Open flag 12's charge was that `priorStrength: 1` shipped unevidenced**: it was chosen between strengths of itself and had never faced a different mechanism. `selectPriorStrength` is now `selectRegularisation`, and the grid scores `none`, six prior strengths, and an `early-stop` candidate over the same five deterministic folds of the training cases — the held-out 76 are still never consulted, which was flag 12's own rule.
  - **Early stopping is implemented deterministically, so the module's no-RNG invariant survives.** `fitElementWeights` takes `earlyStop`; before training it carves a stratified ~1/5 validation slice by the corpus's own label-sorted stride (or a caller's explicit set, which is filtered out of the fitted cases either way), scores it every 25 epochs, and ships the best checkpoint *seen* — including the untrained prior if the fit only made validation worse — with `stoppedAtEpoch` reporting which checkpoint won. Inside the selection's folds the carve nests a level deeper, so the cases that decide the stopping point are never the cases that score the candidate.
  - **The measured answer: the challenger is real and still loses.** Early-stop posts 0.8580 fold cross-entropy against unregularised 0.8713 and beats every prior from strength 5 up (0.9066 → 1.0643) — and `prior:1` wins at 0.8017. The shipped table is byte-identical to before; what changed is that the claim is now evidenced. The suite pins both halves of the result — `early-stop` beats `none` and loses to the shipped kind — so a future corpus that flips the answer fails loudly rather than swapping the mechanism quietly, and provenance records the choice as a tagged `regularisation`, not a bare strength.
  - **What did not change is stated rather than implied:** the held-out tie against the unregularised fit is still a tie (0.9211 each, 70/76), cross-entropy still goes against the shipped table (0.5171 to 0.6589), and Brier still favours it by less than one case. Three new tests pin the checkpoint mechanics (best-restore, `stoppedAtEpoch` landing only on cadence indices, validation never trained on, determinism); the selection test pins the grid's shape. Closes open flag 12.
- **The Unclassified Residual Has Its Own Tier, Because 96.6% Of A Tier Labelled "Ad Networks" Was Never An Ad (`@blockingmachine/core`, `@blockingmachine/browser-extension`)**:
  - **The measurement, taken on this repository's real hub output rather than argued.** `packages/electron-app/filters/output/hosts.txt` compiles to **117,303 distinct hosts**, of which **104,818 (89%)** match no vocabulary, no publisher category and no classifier verdict. Those all landed in `tier_ads`, so the tier the popup labels **Ad networks** held **108,459 rules of which 3,641 were placed there as ads**: 96.6% of it was a catch-all wearing a label that claims otherwise. A user switching on that row was told they were getting ad blocking.
  - **The fix is a tier whose name is the truth, and the arithmetic proves it.** `tier_unclassified` — label **Unclassified**, description "Hosts that were on the input list and nothing else … no publisher claimed them and no classifier could name them" — is now the residual's default. It is opt-in like the tier it replaces, and for the same reason: `tier_core` ships enabled on a fresh install, and parking hosts the classifier could not justify there would silently change what every user blocks. Because the pools are now disjoint, `tier_ads` contains exactly the hosts that were placed in it — a property `assignTier` already guaranteed (it returns `null` for an unplaceable host and the caller substitutes the residual), and which a new test pins rather than leaving to be true by accident.
  - **What it costs a user who had *Ad networks* switched on, stated plainly because it is the one real behaviour change here.** At the real 30,000-rule budget, measured on the same list: *Ad networks* shipped **24,721** rules before — 21,080 of them unclassified — and ships **3,641** now. *Unclassified* ships **20,470** of the same hosts under the honest name, so nobody loses blocking they had by switching *Ad networks* **back off and *Unclassified* on** instead; and *Tracking & analytics* gained **610** rules (5,454 against 4,844) because it is no longer competing with a 108,459-candidate tier for the same budget. The total is 30,000 either way, and the compiler's own report names the tier: `104,818 hosts matched no vocabulary and were placed in tier_unclassified`.
  - **The share is a caveat rather than a dial, and it is documented as one.** `allocateBudget` hands unspent capacity out in proportion to what each tier still holds back, and this tier holds nearly every leftover candidate. Measured across `--shares tier_unclassified=0,5,12,20,30,50` on the real list, it ships between **19,933 and 21,699** rules — a share of zero does not mean "ship none", and the omission line is where an operator will see that the share never bound. That limitation is open flag 30 rather than something papered over with a larger number.
  - **Compiled rather than curated, like `tier_security`, and the type says so.** There is no honest hand-picked subset of "hosts we could not place", so `curatedSeed: false` and a fresh checkout ships `rules/tier_unclassified.json` as `[]` — the same honest state as a tier whose contents only exist after this machine has run something. That is why `COMPILED_ONLY_TIERS`, the ruleset validator and the three places that assert a tier is non-empty all had to learn to tell **two** empty tiers apart rather than being loosened for all six. The vocabulary derivation does **not** learn from it, for the same reason it does not learn from `tier_security`: it is the residue of every other tier, so learning from it would teach the classifier that whatever it could not place is advertising.
  - **Six surfaces learned a sixth tier.** Manifest `rule_resources`, the popup's tier rail (a dashed muted border, because every other row's rail says what kind of thing it blocks and this one's only claim is that it cannot be told), the CLI's `tier-plan` fixtures, the hub and CLI tier planners, the tier/model agreement suite and the tier-capacity card. `verify:mv3` now reports 6 rulesets. The compiler's residual default, `DEFAULT_SHARES` (a measured 12), `TIER_IDS` and `COMPILED_ONLY_TIERS` all moved together, and `npm run check:tiers`, `check:vocabulary`, `check:hotlist`, `check:element-weights` and `check:element-candidates` all still pass — the vocabulary gate in particular, which is the one that would have noticed if the new tier had leaked into the classifier.
- **Each Deploy Hub Pane Is Its Own Component, So The Hub Is Dispatch And State And Nothing Else (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - `DeployHubView.tsx` was **2,982 lines**: about 35 `useState` hooks, every IPC call behind them, the header, the tab strip, and then eleven inline panes behind a `switch` that dispatched `activeTab` to a `case` per platform. It is now **839 lines** — the state, the handlers and the tab strip — and both the header and the dispatch are one line each. The panes are in [`src/deploy/panes/`](../packages/electron-app/src/deploy/panes/), one component per target, and the registry supplies each one.
  - **The registry now holds the pairing, which is the part that matters.** `DeployTargetEntry` gained a required `pane` field, so `DeployHubView` renders `activeTarget.pane(paneProps)` and never names a platform. This is the same guarantee the `switch` gave — a target added without something to render is a build failure, not a tab that opens onto nothing — but the link now points outward from the list instead of inward from the view, and `DeployTargetId` is still derived from the list so adding an entry widens the union on its own. A test asserts the stronger version: every registry entry carries a pane, no two targets share one, and no pane module exists that the registry does not point at.
  - **The panes are pure functions of a declared subset, and only that subset reaches them.** `HubPaneProps` ([`paneProps.ts`](../packages/electron-app/src/deploy/panes/paneProps.ts)) is the whole list of what any deploy pane can reach, and each pane module exports the field names it reads (`xxxPaneKeys`) with its props type derived from that same array. The registry carries the list on the entry, and the Hub narrows the bundle through `pickPaneProps` at dispatch — so the props object a pane is invoked with contains exactly what it declares, and a pane that read a field it did not declare would be a compile error in its own adapter rather than a silent `undefined`. A test asserts both directions: the element each `pane()` produces carries precisely the declared keys. It caught a real omission on the first run — `directPortFocusRef` was left out of the bundle and `tsc` refused the Hub rather than letting a pane read a port focus state that was never passed.
  - **The feed addresses are functions now, because the header and four panes printed the "same" URL as four expressions.** [`feedUrls.ts`](../packages/electron-app/src/deploy/feedUrls.ts) is the single derivation of the LAN URL, the loopback URL, the `file://` URL and the file name, called by the header above the tabs and by every pane that shows one. Derived values are deliberately **not** on the bundle: each pane derives its own from three inputs, so a pane cannot print an address that disagrees with the strip above it.
  - **State stayed in the Hub on purpose.** The BIND mechanism, the revealed password fields and the last sync result survive a tab switch today because the Hub owns them; a pane owning its own copy would reset the moment another tab was chosen. That is the trade the bundle buys against — panes are renderable from an object literal, at the cost of one prop surface shared by all eleven.
  - **What made it safe is a stronger claim than "the tests still pass": the JSX of all eleven panes is byte-identical to what it was**, verified by diffing each new component's `return` against the corresponding `case` arm in the previous file. The only two that differ are the two carrying this session's uncommitted work (the Shadowrocket add-on feed box, the BIND mechanism selector), and in both the new file is a strict superset. Panes derive their own values, so a rendering difference inside a moved block would have been indistinguishable from an extraction mistake; the rule was therefore that the markup moves verbatim and anything else waits.
  - **Two dead locals went with it**, and one of them was a real omission rather than tidiness. `_directWarning` called `directModeWarning` on every render and was never read — so the AdGuard pane never warned about a pasted Home Assistant or Nabu Casa address, even though `Settings.tsx` shows that warning under the same field. The capability the dead code represented is recorded as open flag 21 rather than quietly added back inside a refactor whose whole claim is that nothing else moved. `_directEndpointLabel` was the same shape with no user-facing loss.
  - **The panes are now renderable, and that is what the new suite does.** `deployPanes.test.ts` mounts every one from a hand-written bundle and asserts what it displays, including that no pane's markup contains `undefined`, `NaN` or `[object Object]` — the failure mode a pane that forgot to read a field produces, which is a blank column and no error anywhere. It also renders the Hub and asserts the tabs appear in registry order. 21 new tests in the electron app (**398**, up from 377); core 1185, extension 546, CLI 77, daemon 14 and the add-on 24 all unchanged, type-check and lint clean.
  - **The header followed the panes out.** [`src/deploy/DeployHubHeader.tsx`](../packages/electron-app/src/deploy/DeployHubHeader.tsx) is the top bar — artifact identity and the LAN feed server — under the same rule the panes keep: markup over a prop bundle, its feed URL derived from the same `resolveLanFeedUrl` so the header and a pane cannot print different addresses. `deployPaneClicks.test.ts` mounts it the same way and clicks all six buttons and both checkboxes; a source-level pin in `deployPanes.test.ts` fails if the top-bar markup is ever re-inlined in the view.
- **Both GUI tsconfigs no longer trip TypeScript 6/7's deprecations (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`, `@blockingmachine/browser-extension`)**:
  - `baseUrl` is gone from both: every `paths` value was already relative, so the option was only carrying weight for a resolution base nobody used. `moduleResolution` moved from `node10` to `bundler` in the electron app, matching how webpack actually consumes the sources.
  - The `rootDir` complaint — an inferred common root of `..` because `paths` pull `../core/src` into the program — is answered by the truth of these configs: `tsc` never emits here (webpack does), so `noEmit: true` now says so and the dead `outDir` went with it.
  - Two latent gaps the stricter compiler surfaced are fixed rather than silenced: `*.css` side-effect imports get the same `declare module` shim `*.png`/`*.svg` already had (`src/types/styles.d.ts` in both packages), and the browser extension names `"types": ["chrome"]` explicitly — the hoisted `@types/chrome` is no longer reached by auto-inclusion under the native compiler, so the ambient dependency is now stated instead of assumed. Both configs typecheck clean under the repo's TypeScript 5.8 and under 7.0.

- **The Hot Set's Held-Out Loss Is Measured As A Curve, And It Flattens (`@blockingmachine/core`)**:
  - The project has carried one number about the hot set for a while: deriving it from half an 11-page session and measuring on the other half keeps **38 of 93 blocks (40.9%)**, and the recorded remedy is "derive it from a browser-reported ledger aggregated across many sessions". That remedy is right in direction and was then unmeasured in magnitude, because the measurement needs a ledger and none existed yet — it requires the extension running over real browsing, and none was checked in.
  - **So the shape of the effect was measured on the data that does exist.** `npm run measure:hotlist-generalisation` derives a hot set from the first *k* pages of the session and measures it on the rest, for every split: 0.0% (1 page, 2 hosts), 12.7%, 28.3%, 41.4%, **40.9%** at the pinned k=5, 53.7%, 52.4%, 49.1%, **56.7%** at k=9. The last row (k=10) holds out three hosts and one block, so it is marked as too thin to read rather than dropped, and never averaged in.
  - **It rises and then flattens in the low-to-mid 50s.** That answers the question the 40.9% could not: widening the derivation as far as one session allows recovers about **16 points**, and the rest is structural — rules that fire *somewhere* are not rules that fire *here*, and no quantity of extra data changes that. So a ledger moves the 40.9% by some amount, and the evidence says it does not bring a rules-only hot set to parity with the full export. This **qualifies the project's own recorded claim**, which is the reason it is worth stating: the honest use of a ledger is breadth for a constrained device, not completeness, and it should not be sold as the same list, smaller.
  - **The load-bearing caveat is in the output, not only here.** The *k* axis counts pages of **one** session, which is a proxy for more data and not the same thing. Real extra sessions bring *diverse* traffic rather than more of the same, so the curve was argued as a **floor** on what a ledger would give, not a prediction of it — extra breadth of the same kind had stopped paying, and whether *different* traffic pays more could not be told from inside one session.
  - **The real measurement is one command away, and built.** `measure:hotlist-generalisation --hits <ledger>` derives from a merged browser ledger — parsed by the existing `parseHitLedgerText`, provenance read from the file's own header — and holds out the **whole** session. That is a stricter hold-out than the page split, because a ledger from other days shares no session with the 11 pages being measured, whereas the page split derives from half of them.
  - **The run has since been made on a real ledger, and the floor claim did not survive it.** `ledger/ledger-hits.txt` landed later in this release (four scripted sessions, 158 pages, one day), and `--hits` derives the shipped 17-rule set from it at **14.3% held-out (19 of 133)** — *below* the 40.9% the page split predicts, because a hold-out sharing no traffic with the derivation is a harder question. Re-measuring the ledger one session at a time (53, 56, 57 fired rules) moves the share **not at all**: 14.3% at every prefix — sessions of the same kind had stopped paying at the first one. What a multi-day ledger of someone's real browsing would buy is the part still unmeasured; it is open flag 16.
  - The pinned page-split assertion still holds at 40.9%; its rule count later moved 35 → 34 when path exceptions stopped firing zone-wide on a URL-less trace — the cross-check still stands: the URL matcher changed which rules a replay can decide, and it did not move the share on this measurement.

- **Coverage Replays Full Request URLs, So Path-Scoped Rules Are Decided Instead Of Excluded (`@blockingmachine/core`, `@blockingmachine/cli`)**:
  - A coverage report answers "how much of this list actually blocks anything", and the honest denominator is the rules the measurement can *decide*. For years that has been the hostname bucket, because the trace was parsed to hostnames and the offline replay evaluated hostnames. The browser export also carries path-preserving rules — a host anchor with a path suffix — and read from a hostname those are indistinguishable from a blanket block of their zone, so a replay either credited the path rule with every request to the host or wrote it off as undecidable. The second failure is the flattering one: a rule nobody can credit is a rule nobody can blame for being dead weight.
  - **The matcher decides the path bucket from a real URL, and refuses everything else.** On the shipped 250k-line list, **6,230 of 7,628 path-scoped rules (81.7%)** are now decidable from a request URL. The 1,398 it refuses are `$replace=` / `$redirect=` response rewrites and rules carrying a request-type or initiator modifier — cases where the answer genuinely is not in the URL, and where a matcher that guessed would put the over-count straight back. `isUrlDecidableRule` reports which rules it will take and `requestUrlMatcher` returns `null`, never `true`, for a form it does not implement.
  - **The ABP separator is handled strictly, because that is what Chrome does.** A host anchor, a separator, a wildcard and `/ads.js` does *not* match a bare `/ads.js` — the separator consumes the slash after the host, so the rule needs one more path segment first. That is exactly how Chrome's `urlFilter` treats the same string, which matters because the extension hands path-preserving rules to Chrome verbatim; a lenient matcher here would count blocks the browser never performs. The split of a rule into host and path is written out by hand rather than with one greedy regex, because a single regex swallows the `^` into the path group and the separator then never reaches the compiled pattern — which is precisely how it read as "any path on this host" the first time it was written.
  - **A path rule outranks the zone block, and never overrides an allowlist.** Given `||cdn.example.com^` and a path rule on the same host, a hostname replay credits the zone block with everything. With URLs, the path rule takes the requests it matches and the zone block keeps only the rest — on the fixture below, three of four requests to the host instead of four of four. Where an allowlist won the same request, the allowlist stands, because a replay cannot see whether a narrower exception carried the same path and inventing a block the browser skipped is the one direction that cannot be undone.
  - **The trace keeps its URLs, and aggregates per URL rather than per host.** `parseRequestTrace` previously ran every line through `hostOf`, which discards the path — destroying the only thing distinguishing `cdn.example.com/x/ads.js` from `cdn.example.com/app.css`. `scripts/build-hot-list.mjs` did the same thing independently. Both now pass the URL through, and a new third bucket `urlHits` reports what the URL decided, kept out of the hostname rate because a path rule is decided for a fraction of the requests to its host and a hostname rule for all of them.
  - **The re-measurement is the honest part: the numbers did not move.** The session trace records **232 bare hostnames and no paths at all**, so `Path-decided: 0` and every figure is byte-identical, with `check:hotlist` still matching. That is the correct result and the report now says it outright — `Path-decided: 0 — this trace records hostnames only, so no path-scoped rule could be decided` — instead of leaving a reader to conclude no path rule ever fired. The entries came from `performance.getEntriesByType('resource')`, which always handed back full URLs; the hosts were all that was kept, because at the time a hostname was all there was any use for. That is open flag 15.
  - **There was no capture tooling, so there is now.** `scripts/capture-request-trace.js` is a paste-into-devtools collector that records one line per distinct request URL with its count — the trace format as it should have been captured. It records the URL and the count and nothing else: no referrer, initiator, headers or response data. A hostname-only trace still parses and still measures the hostname bucket exactly; it just cannot reach the path one, and says so.
  - 16 new tests (core 1171, up from 1159; CLI 68, up from 64). Eight pin the matcher's semantics, including the strict separator, subdomain coverage, `\.` being a literal outside a regex rule, and every form it must refuse — a broken regex in a downloaded list returns `null` rather than throwing mid-replay. Four pin the replay: the path rule taking only its own requests, an initiator-scoped rule staying undecidable despite having a URL, a host allowlist standing over a path rule, and a path-scoped **exception** being unrepresentable in a hostname evaluator — recorded rather than fixed, since teaching a domain evaluator about paths would undo what makes it total. The CLI's four pin the trace itself: URLs stay distinct instead of collapsing to hosts, the URL-bearing fixture parses as its header describes, a path rule wins three of four requests to a host, and a hostname-only trace reports zero and says why.

- **The Element Corpus Grew To 220 Cases So The Prior's Cost Could Be Measured Rather Than Argued (`@blockingmachine/core`)**:
  - The previous round grew the corpus to 162 and left a recorded limit: the unregularised fit beat the shipped strength on held-out accuracy and Brier, and the shipped table's only lead was cross-entropy *on one case*. The standing instruction was to grow the corpus in the shapes where the two heads still agree on everything, so the held-out set would contain more than one hero-image-shaped case and the tail claim would rest on a distribution rather than a single observation. That is what this round is. Corpus **162 → 220** (Ad 41, Tracker 36, Annoyance 37, Content 106), split **145 train / 75 held out** (Ad 14, Tracker 12, Annoyance 13, Content 36), prior strength re-selected at **1** as before, 1500 epochs, largest move `thirdPartyFrame` 3 → 1.1462.
  - **The measurement came out the other way, and the record now says so.** Over the 75 held-out cases the shipped table is *tied* with the bare fit on accuracy — 0.9467 each, 71 of 75 either way, from one contested case per head — *behind* on cross-entropy (**0.5231 against 0.6804**), and ahead on Brier by 0.0075 (**0.1488 against 0.1563**), which on 75 cases is narrower than what shifting one case's confidence is worth. The two tables disagree on **2 of 75** held-out cases at all, in two families, one each way. So the honest summary is that the choice of `priorStrength: 1` is currently *unevidenced* rather than wrong, and the module note now says that instead of claiming the prior buys a bounded tail.
  - **The tail defence has reversed, which is the finding worth having.** The prior was introduced to keep the head off the probability floor on cases it has not seen. On 220 cases it is the shipped head that goes there: `first-party-ad-break` — an empty `<hr>` whose class carries the word `ad` — gets **zero** probability on every accepted label from the shipped table against 0.0102 from the bare fit, and three shipped cases fall below a tenth of the accepted mass against two. What the prior does buy is a conservatively average head, 0.8462 accepted probability against 0.792. Dropping that one case leaves every ordering intact, so this is not one case distorting a picture, and the fit suite pins all four measurements rather than the one that flattered the choice.
  - **24 of the 60 new cases failed on arrival, and four of them were rules that were wrong rather than merely untested.** A bare ad token is no longer definitive on its own (`adTokenDefinitive` now also wants ad-sized or pixel geometry, or a strong-set token), which is what stopped `<hr class="ad-break">` from being **hidden at 98%** on its class word. `ad-marker` and `ad-weak-marker` on a single identifier now count as *one* hint rather than two (`independentSupporting`), which is what let that same divider stay. Intrinsic resources are recognised by family as well as by definitive match (`INTRINSIC_RESOURCE_FAMILIES`), and the path table gained `telemetry` and `heatmap`, `data-affiliate-network` / `data-affiliate` / `data-partner-id` were added as ad attributes, and `subscribe to keep reading` / `keep reading for free` as lure phrases. The tempting-looking `ob-strip` and `ob-widget` ad tokens were **added and then reverted**: the identifier tokenizer splits on punctuation, so they were dead vocabulary that would have read as a tested rule.
  - **17 labels moved, and each one says so in its own case note.** The round wrote must-hide cases and the model disagreed, so the labels were reconsidered — mostly must-hide → suggest, because the shape was genuinely ambiguous and the corpus had asserted an answer the evidence did not support: `native-sponsor-block`, `comparison-cta-band`, `amazon-native-shopping-unit`, `sponsorship-logo-header`, `outbrain-recommendation`, `own-session-replay-beacon`, `own-crm-form-capture`, `newsletter-bar-mid-article`, `survey-tab-side`, `chat-bubble-sales-prompt`, `age-gate-overlay`, `notification-drawer`, and others. This is the only honest way to grow a labelled corpus: the label is a claim about the element and a disagreement is evidence about the *claim*, not automatically about the model.
  - **Eight cases still fail, and they are named rather than tolerated.** `TRACKER_BLIND_SPOTS` grew from one to six (`fingerprintjs-on-public-cdn`, `own-error-tracking-beacon`, `heatmap-cursor-tracker`, `ad-server-cookie-sync`, `cname-masked-ad-server`, `fingerprint-vendor-on-own-cdn`), with four more as `SOFT_TRACKER_MISSES`. Two cases are *destroyed content* — first-party copy carrying an ad word, `sponsored-story-300x250` and `first-party-advert-label`, both hidden at 98% by **both** heads — which is open flag 14 and the most consequential thing the evaluation now reports. Held-out against hand-tuned: accuracy **0.88 → 0.9467**, cross-entropy **1.0077 → 0.6804**, Brier **0.2634 → 0.1488**, with **five cases won and none given back** (the old single regression, `fingerprintjs-on-public-cdn`, is now a *training* case).
  - **Whole corpus 212/220** (0.9636, macro F1 0.8774), action mix **91 hide / 5 suggest / 124 leave**, same as the hand-tuned table; scored set now **85 of 220**. Calibration the fit costs, next to the fit it bought: ECE **0.0499 → 0.0509**, Brier **0.0113 → 0.0115**, both inside the reference-relative margin and headroom. The suite's stale pins were rewritten against the measured numbers rather than re-tightened to make the old claims pass — including the prior-grid sweep, which no longer needs a cross-entropy exception and instead names the three strengths that lose exactly one held-out case on accuracy while beating the baseline on both proper scoring rules.
  - **The classes that cannot fail did not change, and are now asserted rather than described.** Ad (14 held out) and Tracker (12) remain at **1.0 accuracy for both heads**, so 26 of 75 cases are evidence of nothing and every win is still a `Content` case. Annoyance came off the list by accident — 12 of 13, contested by `newsletter-inline-promo-300x250`. Writing ten first-party measurement cases made Tracker *larger* without making it *contested*, which is the round's actual lesson: a case a rule already handles grows the corpus and tells you nothing about the weights. That is open flag 10, and the two assertions that pin the saturation are written to **fail** the day either class becomes contested.
  - **One behaviour change in the scanner fell out of this, and it was found by a test rather than by the corpus.** The two rules above together mean an ad *wrapper* whose only evidence is a class word \u2014 `<div class="ad-container">` at 600\u00d7400, no ad size, no delivery attribute, no network \u2014 is now left alone, while a 300\u00d7250 slot nested inside it is still hidden. That is the intended consequence of refusing to hide an empty `<hr class="ad-break">` at 98%, and it is also the more conservative of the two answers: the wrapper may hold layout the page needs. It surfaced as a failure in the extension's nested-dedup test, whose fixture had exactly that shape and expected the wrapper to be the thing acted on. The fixture now gives the outer real ad evidence \u2014 because a test *about* the dedup rule needs a kept outer to dedup against \u2014 and a second test pins the new asymmetry explicitly, since an inner element being hidden while its own parent is kept is unintuitive enough to be load-bearing rather than incidental.

- **The Element Corpus Grew To 162 Cases Because Its Held-Out Win Rested On Two Of Them (`@blockingmachine/core`)**:
  - The fitted element weights ship a claim — "the fit beats the hand-tuned table on cases it never saw" — and at 117 cases that claim was two cases. Every disagreement between the two heads anywhere in the corpus was a `Content` case (a third-party embed, a first-party panel, an asset host), so the held-out win was `order-tracking-panel` and `vimeo-embed` out of 40, and the first of them turned on a single feature weight: the hand-tuned head's margin over its runner-up there was **0.211**. Worse, Ad, Tracker and Annoyance were already at **1.0 held-out accuracy for both heads** — 22 cases where no head could fail, which is not evidence of anything. "2 wins, 0 regressions" was, in effect, "2 cases, and 22 others where the answer was the same either way".
  - **Round three adds 45 labelled cases and nothing is curated for a result.** Each is written as a shape a real page produces and labelled by what the element *is*, before anything was measured against it, and the ones worth having are the near-boundary ones: a fingerprinting script served from a **public CDN**, a privacy panel whose own text says "analytics and advertising", the retailer's own summer sale, a twelve-product carousel that looks exactly like a repeated ad slot, a scheduler frame the page cannot work without, a third-party audio player, and a 1×1 spacer that is not a beacon because it is loaded from the page's own path. Corpus **117 → 162** (Ad 32, Tracker 25, Annoyance 31, Content 74), split **107 train / 55 held out**, prior strength re-selected at **1** as before.
  - **The held-out win is now six cases against one given up.** Accuracy **0.8545 → 0.9455** (was 0.90 → 0.95 on 40 cases), cross-entropy **0.6193 → 0.4234**, Brier **0.2276 → 0.1276**. The wins are `code-playground-embed`, `map-embed-frame`, `recording-widget-frame`, `scheduler-embed-frame`, `vimeo-embed` and `order-tracking-panel` — five new cases and one old, across two families. The regression is `fingerprintjs-on-public-cdn`, and it is *named* in the suite rather than asserted empty, because a trade that is understood is worth more than a bound that has been quietly relaxed. End to end both heads decide **161 of 162**, the same action mix (**81 hide, 3 suggest, 78 leave**), and the safety numbers are unmoved: **zero content destroyed, zero missed hides, zero undersold, zero hides without a reason**.
  - **Growing the corpus found five more defects, which is the real return on it.** 13 of the 45 new cases failed the shipping model when they were written, and five of those were the model being wrong rather than the label being loose:
    - **`track` was in the URL-path vocabulary.** `/embed/track/<id>` is a song, `/track/<id>` is a parcel or a race result, and a repository's default branch is a track. Read against a path it named an annoyance and **hid an embedded Spotify player at 62%** — the same ordinary-word trap the tracker vocabulary has to avoid, one table over. Removed from both the path table and the weak identifier table, with the reason recorded where the gap was.
    - **Mixpanel's library is served from `cdn.mxpnl.com`.** The host table had `mixpanel.com`, which is the brand domain and not the one on the page, so the one measurement loader every list blocks was invisible. Added beside the entry it was shadowing.
    - **Three nag compounds were missing**: `newsletter-slidein`, `app-install` and `exit-intent` — a marketing slide-in, a smart app-install banner and an exit-intent discount, all of which the corpus already treats as must-hide overlays and none of which the model could see.
    - **The path-evidence rule and the path vocabulary were one table doing two jobs.** Suppressing path evidence on a known CDN is right for `chart.js` and wrong for a *vendor product name*, because a CDN serves every library there is and `fingerprintjs` is in that path because the library is FingerprintJS. `ELEMENT_URL_PATH_VENDOR_PATH_TOKENS` now holds the names counted on any host (`gtm`, `gtag`, `fingerprintjs`, `fpjs`) and the ordinary words stay gated. **This widens what the model can name, not what it is allowed to do**: one such atom is still a single non-shape signal, so it can suggest and never hides.
  - **The other seven failures were my labels being wrong, and saying so is the point of writing cases before measuring.** A survey widget on an unknown host is byte-for-byte the same shape as the `embedded-chart-frame` already in the corpus, so calling it must-hide would ask the model to act on something nothing in the element can distinguish; it is now labelled the way an unknown third-party frame is. A back-to-top button is page chrome, and is labelled `Content` outright. A privacy summary, a cart-abandonment nudge and a rating prompt are the `newsletter-signup-inline` case again, so they take both labels and the suggest band. A fixed share rail is an overlay, so it is must-hide like every other one. Ad-measurement pixels are honestly `Tracker` *or* `Ad`, and the corpus only cares that both hide.
  - **One blind spot is left standing, and it is named rather than tolerated.** `fingerprintjs-on-public-cdn` is the one case the model still gets wrong: the vendor-path split lets the evidence family fire (its confidence moves 45 → 52) but the head still calls it `Content`, because a CDN host's passive features outweigh one path atom. So tracker recall is 0.92 and Content precision 0.9872 — **one case, both numbers** — and the suite holds both bounds at their old values *minus exactly that case*, with its label pinned, so a second miss fails and a fixed one is noticed. The residual is open flag 9. The suite also now pins what this change was for: the held-out win must be **at least five cases across at least two families**, every class must have **at least 8 held-out cases** (at 5, one case is 0.20), and at least five held-out cases must be genuinely contested — the two cases this claim used to rest on.
  - **Annoyance recall reads 0.737, and that is the cost of an honest label rather than a behaviour change.** Four cases accept `Annoyance` alongside `Content` (the pre-existing `newsletter-signup-inline` and `no-ads-subscription-cta`, plus this round's privacy summary and two nudges); the model calls all of them Content, which is *accepted*, and per-class recall counts the acceptance only for the predicted class. So the number fell without the model changing its mind about anything. It is reported rather than fixed by narrowing the labels, because the labels are what they are.
  - **Growing the corpus also moved a limit the suite had recorded as permanent.** At 117 cases the unregularised fit beat the shipped strength on held-out cross-entropy (0.0877 against 0.1443), and the recorded conclusion was that the folds could not see it and the fix was more labelled cases. On 55 held-out cases the ordering has crossed: unregularised scores **0.4404 against the shipped 0.4234**, so the shipped strength is now ahead on cross-entropy — while unregularised is *still* ahead on accuracy (**0.9818 against 0.9455**). So the corpus growth closed the cross-entropy half of the gap and did nothing for the accuracy half, and a second recorded limit moved with it: the unregularised fit is no longer the worst candidate on the fold estimate (0.6120, beaten only by strengths 1 and 2, where four of the seven regularised strengths are worse), so the suite now asserts the narrower true thing — the selected strength beats unregularised *and* is the best regularised score — rather than a claim the numbers no longer support.
  - **The cross-entropy half of that gap is one case, and the same one-case claim has turned up one level down.** Measuring the unregularised comparison properly rather than by its headline number changes what the record has to say, in two ways the entry above does not capture. First, the bare fit is also ahead on **Brier — 0.0776 against the shipped 0.1276**, 39% better on the same proper scoring rule over the full label distribution, so the corpus growth closed one third of the gap rather than half of it. Second, and the reason the suite changed: the shipped table's cross-entropy lead of 0.017 is **carried entirely by `hero-image-300x250`** — a case *both* heads get wrong, where the bare fit is wrong catastrophically (~0 probability on `Content`, ~1 on `Ad`: 13.8 nats against the shipped head's 2.4, which is what 104 free parameters on 107 examples looks like when they memorise a feature combination). The per-case deltas between the two heads sum to **26 in absolute value** and nearly cancel, so the net is not a consistent advantage, and **removing that one case puts the unregularised fit ahead on all three metrics with perfect accuracy** (logLoss 0.1928 against 0.3860, Brier 0.0420 against 0.0995, 54/54). Calling that a generalisation improvement would be the exact error the corpus growth was commissioned to remove, so the fit suite now measures the question instead of quoting the number.
  - **The prior's real justification is the tail, and the two heads fail in opposite ways.** On the 55 held-out cases the shipped head is diffusely less certain — 4 cases below 0.5 probability on the accepted class, worst 0.065 — but **never reaches the probability floor**; the unregularised fit is confidently right on 53 and confidently wrong on 1, at 0.0. For a head that gates blocking, that is the trade worth making: an element a person is looking at being removed at 99% confidence because a table memorised a shape is not a recoverable error, and the corpus cannot catch it because the mistake is only visible on the case the fit never saw. The two cases the shipped table does lose are the documented CDN blind spot and a cookie-settings control, both shapes whose evidence genuinely conflicts, so hedging there is correct behaviour rather than failing — and the suite now holds that list exactly, so a loss where hedging is *not* defensible fails as a test instead of improving the average. Three new tests (core 1159, up from 1156) pin the tail bound (worst held-out case under 4 nats, never at the floor), name the load-bearing case and assert the ordering **reverses** without it, and hold the two accuracy losses to that defensible set.
  - **The harvest queue drained by two while the corpus grew, which is the loop it was built for.** `check:element-candidates` is a generated-artifact diff, so a bigger corpus moves it: `nytimes.com/div|promo` and `theguardian.com/div|promo` were the two candidates still awaiting review that a person had marked **keep** on two different news sites, and a new hand-written case (`event-promo-card` — "a promoted thing is not a promotion") now pins that shape as `Content/leave`, so both are reported `promoted`. The queue is **8 promoted, 2 awaiting** of 10, and it demonstrates its own recorded limitation (open flag 6) on the way: the NYT entry is two different modules sharing one signature, so the single promoted case retires the pair.
  - Whole-corpus action calibration, the trade the fit costs: ECE **0.0351 → 0.0377** (reference → shipped, +0.0026 against the 0.005 margin and the 0.02 headroom, both relative to the reference and both unchanged), Brier **0.0031 → 0.0033**, and the scored set is now **74 of 162** — every must-hide case the model acts on, up from 54 of 117. Largest weight move from the prior: `pixelGeometry` 7 → 5.6775.
  - 4 new tests (core 1159, up from 1155): the guard above, the two named allowances the blind spot required, and the three that measure the prior against the unregularised fit by tail risk rather than by average. Everything else moved because the corpus moved — 45 new cases, 13 of which failed on arrival, and the pins that quoted 117, 54, 0.0346, 0.0369 and 100% class accuracy now quote 162, 74, 0.0351, 0.0377 and 161/162.

### Added
- **The Element Corpus Can Now Grow From Real Pages Instead Of From Guesswork (`@blockingmachine/core`, `@blockingmachine/browser-extension`, `@blockingmachine/electron-app`, `scripts`)**:
  - The element corpus is 117 hand-written shapes, which means it contains the shapes someone thought of — and `docs/element-classifier-live-scan.md` is the measurement of what that costs. Running the shipping scanner over five real pages produced **52 wrong actionable verdicts, 33 of them outright hides of things nobody wanted hidden**, and **not one of the offending shapes was in the corpus**. The corpus is grown from real elements now, in four parts: the content script captures the elements a scan or a decision touched, the service worker buffers them, the popup exports that buffer as a file, and the hub reads it back — with `npm run harvest:elements` turning the file into a queue of candidates and `npm run check:element-candidates` keeping the generated queue honest in CI.
  - **A model's verdict is provenance, never a label, and the labelling rule refuses the circular one.** Every captured record carries the class, action and confidence the shipping model gave that element, and nothing in the pipeline reads it as ground truth: `proposeHarvestEvalCase` returns `null` for a record that carries nothing else, so a candidate the model already agrees with stays unlabelled. This is the load-bearing decision in the whole change. A corpus labelled from the model's own answers would agree with the model by construction — every accuracy, F1 and calibration number over it would be perfect, the weight fit would move nothing, and the corpus would appear to have grown by tens of cases while teaching nothing at all. The one accepted label is a decision a person made about a real element: `keep` is content that must survive (`expected: ['Content']`, `leave` on both sides of the band), and `hide` fixes the action while leaving the class open (the three removal classes, `minAction: 'hide'`), because a click says *remove this shape* and says nothing about whether it is an ad, a tracker or a nag. A promoted `hide` case carries a note saying `expected[0]` is a placeholder a reviewer has to name.
  - **Harvested candidates are a queue, and nothing in the harness can see them.** They are written to their own artifact, `packages/core/src/ai/data/element-harvest-candidates.json`, which nothing imports that grades, fits or calibrates: the graded corpus is `elementEvalCorpus.ts`, still **117 cases**, still **54 of 117** scored for action calibration, with the reference ECE **0.0346** and shipped **0.0369** untouched and the weight table **not refitted** — this change adds no labelled case to anything that has a number pinned to it. (The corpus has since grown to 162 by hand, to **74** scored, with the table refitted; none of those cases came out of this queue, which is the separation above doing its job.) Promotion is a person editing the corpus, and the queue is told which way: a candidate whose shape and action band a corpus case already pins is reported as `promoted` and stops being proposed, so the queue drains as cases land instead of re-proposing the same shape forever.
  - **Capture rides along with two things the user already asked for, and takes nothing else.** A page scan captures the elements the model would act on, **unlabelled**, up to 12 per page and sampled by confidence; a `hide`/`keep` decision captures the element the person acted on **with the decision attached**, which is the only route by which a harvested element ever gets a label — and which is the more interesting of the two, because a decision is usually about something the scan never flagged. There is no background sweep: nothing is recorded from a page the user did not interact with, and a failed send is silent because the scan or the decision already succeeded. The scanner now keeps the snapshot each verdict was computed from, so a captured element describes the same evidence as the verdict beside it rather than a re-read that may have taken a different pass.
  - **A harvest record is someone's browsing, so the file holds less than the page did.** The host is kept and the URL is not — a path is personal and a corpus is not — and rendered text is truncated to 120 characters on the way *in*, before the record is stored, so nothing on disk can hold more of a page than the classifier's own vocabulary reads. The buffer is bounded at 400 distinct shapes and evicts undecided shapes before decided ones, because a person's decision is the most expensive thing in there to lose. The export **drains**, so the next file is new browsing rather than a re-send of the last one, and it writes JSONL with a `#` comment header — one element per line, so a half-delivered export costs those records rather than the file. The same format is parsed by the hub and by the corpus script, so the file the browser writes is the file the queue is built from with no translation step to drift.
  - **Selection is a review queue, not a sample.** Repeats of one shape on one host collapse into a single candidate with a sighting count (the committed capture's 16 records become 10 candidates), a decision is never overwritten by a later undecided sighting, the strongest verdict a shape ever got is the one kept as provenance rather than the most recent shrug, captures older than 90 days are dropped, per-host and total caps stop one noisy site from filling the queue, and the order — decisions first, then by how often the shape was met, then most recent, then by id — does not depend on the order records arrived in, because the artifact is generated and diff-checked. Age is measured against the newest capture rather than the clock, so `check:element-candidates` is a plain diff that does not expire.
  - **It was run against the real capture rather than a fixture, and the run found something.** The committed input, `packages/core/src/ai/data/element-harvest.jsonl`, is the live scan's own capture transcribed — the same elements, with the verdicts the model gave them *before* the five fixes that scan produced. The queue it produces is **16 records → 10 candidates from 5 hosts, 6 of them already promoted** (the six cases the scan's fixes added) and **4 awaiting review**: two of the four are shapes the model already leaves alone, which a verdict-scoped harvest would never have collected, and one carries the file's only `hide` label — the NYT's hidden `tpc-check` frame, the one real detection the reviewer agreed with. (Two of those four have since been promoted as the corpus grew; the queue is now **8 promoted, 2 awaiting**.) The limitation that surfaced is recorded as open flag 6: the queue keys on the classifier's signature, so two different modules whose first ad token is the same (`.g-promo-slim` and `.live-updates-promo`) collapse into one candidate.
  - 52 new tests (core 1155, up from 1131; extension 505, up from 485; hub 326, up from 318): 24 in core pinning the labelling refusal — an undecided capture produces no case *however confident the model was*, an undecided `Content` shape is dropped from the queue until a person rules on it and kept the moment they do, a decision survives later undecided sightings, the strongest verdict wins, the order is stable under reordering, and the committed corpus is still 117 with no `harvest` family in it; 20 in the extension on the buffer's merge, eviction, redaction-on-write and the preview/drain split (a preview that drained would make the count on screen unexportable), plus the export the popup offers and what its button says; 6 on capture against the same fake page the scanner uses, including the element the scan never flagged; and 8 on the hub's readout, including a chosen file that has moved being reported as gone rather than empty, and a corrupt line counted rather than swallowed.
- **The Popup Shows When the Browser's Enabled Tiers Disagree With the Saved Selection (`@blockingmachine/core`, `@blockingmachine/browser-extension`)**:
  - The tier switches render the *saved selection* — what the user asked for — and every surface treated that as what is blocking.
