  - 36 new tests: the two core artifacts pinned in `advanced-formatter.test.ts` (the section opens before the patterns, dot-prefixed hosts, bypass-after-block, no `{ +block {` left anywhere; the SOA, `CNAME .` rather than a per-domain zone, `rpz-passthru.`, and that the file never comments with `#`), the formatter's own output in `formatters.test.ts`, the CLI writing both against the real command, 25 for the two deploy-helper modules, and the tier compiler's `--check` split into its two provenance cases.
- **`check:tiers` Now Checks Something, and `--format` Exists (`scripts/compile-tier-rulesets.mjs`, `@blockingmachine/cli`)**:
  - `npm run check:tiers` exited 1 on every machine and was therefore kept out of both the sweep and CI. The cause was a category error, not a stale file: the repository ships the **curated baseline** (24/36/36/22 rules) while any machine with a compiled hub list derives a **30,000-rule plan**, and `--check` compared the two unconditionally — so it reported "stale" for a tree that was exactly as intended.
  - The check is now provenance-aware. `tierCounts.generated.ts` is written by every run and is the declaration of which state the tree is in, so a recorded compilation still compares byte-for-byte against a fresh plan (which is the check that can catch a genuinely stale release), while the checked-in `null` baseline is verified on its own terms: every tier present, a non-empty ruleset array, and holding exactly the count `rulesetTiers.ts` declares for it — the catalogue the popup reports from. A hand-edit that adds a rule without moving the catalogue now fails; a clean checkout no longer does.
  - Verified both ways by perturbation, not assertion: appending one rule to `tier_core.json` fails the check with `holds 25 rule(s) but the catalogue declares 24`, and restoring the file (confirmed with `cmp`) returns it to green. The new behaviour is pinned in `tierCompiler.test.ts` with a synthetic catalogue, and `check:tiers` is now part of the CI "Verify generated artifacts" step, where it previously could not be run at all.
  - `blockingmachine export --format <list>` makes the CLI able to select its own output at all; before this it always wrote `hosts`, `dnsmasq` and `adguard`, which is what made `privoxy` and `bind` unreachable from the command line. An unknown name is an error rather than something silently dropped.
- **Unbound Reachability: Proving the Drop-In Is Fetched and Loaded (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - The Unbound pane could tell a user their recipe was correct; it could not tell them whether anyone was following it, and the three failure modes are invisible from every other screen. A hub serving nothing, a scheduled `curl` that never runs, and an `include:` line that was never added all end with the resolver answering blocked names normally — which looks exactly like "no blocking configured".
  - New pure `src/unboundReachability.ts`. The third failure is observable, so it is tested directly: the canary is a domain taken *out of the artifact the resolver was just served* (`pickCanaryDomain` — shortest registrable name, because NXDOMAIN only proves sinkholing if the name would otherwise resolve), and `always_nxdomain` means NXDOMAIN proves the drop-in is loaded. A control query must resolve first, so a resolver that refuses names that should resolve is reported **inconclusive** rather than as a working blocklist, and a refused or timed-out query is **unreachable** rather than "not loaded". Verdicts name the fix: feed not answering, wrong format (`zones === 0`), nothing fetching, `include`/reload missing, resolver unreachable, no canary, or **stale** — blocking live with a copy older than the last compile, which is the difference between deployed and staying current.
  - The serve log is what answers "is anything fetching it": new `createFeedServeLog()` records every 2xx serve of a compiled file with peer, user agent and bytes, and the feed server feeds it. The check's own fetch is excluded by header (`reachabilityProbeHeaders()`) rather than by source address, because a resolver on this same machine connects from loopback *legitimately* and must still count — counting either one wrongly breaks the check in opposite directions.
  - **The canary is confirmed to exist before its NXDOMAIN is read as proof.** A domain that was never registered answers NXDOMAIN exactly like one the drop-in blocks, and the control query cannot tell those apart — it only rules out a resolver that refuses everything — so the same name is looked up on a reference resolver and the verdict turns on that answer: confirmed by a resolving reference is `live`, no reference (or one that cannot answer) is `canary-unconfirmed`, and a reference that *also* gets NXDOMAIN is `canary-nonexistent` — the check says it cannot tell rather than reporting a deployment as working on a name that does not resolve anywhere. `pickReferenceTarget()` prefers the address the user set, falls back to this machine's own configured resolvers, and refuses the resolver under test as its own reference (`::1` and `127.0.0.1` are one instance, not two), which is a configuration that can never confirm anything and is rejected at save time as well as at check time.
  - **The serve log is bounded per path, not just in total.** The hub serves several compiled files, so a browser extension polling `browser.txt` would push a single daily Unbound fetch out of a flat newest-200 window and the check would report "nothing has fetched it" for a deployment that fetched it an hour ago. Each path now keeps its own newest 25, with a total cap still keeping a scraped feed from growing the heap.
  - The resolver hint names the cost of the default rather than leaving the user to discover it: `127.0.0.1:53` is called a default the rows make visible, and when this machine resolves through something else, that address is offered as the thing to type.
  - New `src/unboundProbe.ts`: `parseUnboundResolverAddress()` tolerates a bare host, `host:port`, an IPv6 literal, or a pasted URL; `queryUnboundResolver()` uses a one-attempt `resolve4` against the named server, and `classifyLookupFailure()` separates the three outcomes Node hides behind one rejection (`ENOTFOUND` = the sinkhole, `ENODATA` = the name exists so the drop-in is *not* loaded, timeouts and refusals = silence). Collapsing those is how a dead resolver gets reported as a working blocklist. The DNS call is injected, so the classification is tested without a socket.
  - Reading and checking are separate IPC calls: `get-unbound-reachability` returns the persisted verdict so opening the pane never fires a DNS query on tab switch, and `check-unbound-reachability` runs one on request. The last confirmed-live time and newest known fetch persist, so history survives a restart while the serve log deliberately does not — "has anything fetched this *since the hub started" is the question, and a remembered yes would be misleading.
  - Deploy Hub: new presentational `src/components/UnboundReachabilityCard.tsx` renders the verdict, the rows with per-row tone, the remedy, and an editable resolver address (persisted as `unboundResolver`, `127.0.0.1` by default, savable as `host`, `host:port` or a URL), with *Save* and *Check now*. A blank field falls back to `127.0.0.1:53` — the resolver the hub usually sits on — rather than refusing to check, and the hint says the address is a default; a typo is an error instead, because querying localhost for an address the user got wrong would produce a confident verdict about a resolver they never named. The card is render-tested through `react-dom/server`, including the snapshot-only state the pane is in every time it opens.
  - **Verified end to end against real I/O, not only fixtures:** temporary probes stood up an HTTP server serving a real drop-in and hand-built DNS servers, and the full pipeline classified them — the probe's own fetch absent from the serve log while the unmarked one was recorded, `ENOTFOUND` read as the sinkhole, a `stale` verdict when the compile was newer than the fetch, `not-loaded` when the resolver answered normally, `resolver-unreachable` on a refused connection, `inconclusive` when the control did not resolve, and — with two DNS servers — `live` from a reference that resolves the canary and `canary-nonexistent` from a reference pointing at the resolver under test. Deleted afterwards.
  - **The build caught a module boundary, which is the only reason it is in this entry:** the reference helper started in `unboundProbe`, and the Deploy Hub's renderer needs it for the hints — so the renderer bundle tried to resolve `dns/promises` and the webpack build failed. The pure address work now lives in `src/unboundAddress.ts` (no `dns` import), leaving `unboundProbe` with the query. Aliasing `dns` to `false` would have hidden it until the first call.
  - 73 new tests (31 classification and serve log, 16 addresses and reference choice, 7 probe, 9 render, 10 hint text), including that the probe cannot manufacture its own evidence, that the serve log stays bounded and per path, that a resolver which NXDOMAINs the control is not read as success, that an unconfirmed canary is never reported as live, that a deployment cannot be its own reference in any spelling of loopback, and that staleness is never claimed when the fetch cannot be dated.
- **Shadowrocket as a First-Class Deploy Target (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`, `@blockingmachine/core`, `@blockingmachine/cli`)**:
  - The audit that followed the Unbound work turned up three formats with the same gap: `privoxy`, `shadowrocket`, and `bind` are all emitted, advertised in the CLI examples, and published as subscribe URLs, yet none has a deploy target in the Hub, a setup recipe anywhere, or a row in the README's format table. Shadowrocket was the one to close first — it has by far the largest user base, and unlike the other two its syntax is the syntax its client actually wants.
  - **The artifact had to be fixed before it could be documented, because it was not deployable either.** The export emitted a bare list of rules with no `[Rule]` section for the app to read them out of, used `!` AdGuard comments in the CLI path where a Shadowrocket config expects `#`, and — the real defect — emitted `DOMAIN,host,REJECT`, the *exact-host* form, so every subdomain of a blocked domain was missed. It is now `DOMAIN-SUFFIX`, under a `[Rule]` section, with `#` comments, in both core header generators and both CLI paths.
  - **Ordering is part of the format.** A Shadowrocket/Surge rule set is evaluated top-down and first match wins, so a child exception under a blocked parent is now emitted as `DOMAIN-SUFFIX,child,DIRECT` *before* the parent's `REJECT` — the job dnsmasq's per-zone specificity and Unbound's `transparent` zone do by construction, and the one thing a copy of the list could get silently wrong.
  - New `packages/electron-app/src/shadowrocketDeploy.ts`: the feed filename and URL, the warning naming the current format when the export would write something else, the four setup steps as data, and the `DOMAIN-SUFFIX` syntax note. A **Shadowrocket** tab in the Deploy Hub renders them, and the format is selectable in Settings and accepted by the IPC validator, without which the tab could only ever point at a file the app cannot produce.
  - The platform tab strip now wraps; nine tabs no longer fit one row of `nowrap` labels.
  - 17 new tests: 12 for the deploy helpers (feed name and URL, the placeholder host, the format warning, the step contract, the syntax note), 4 pinning the artifact in `advanced-formatter.test.ts` (the section header precedes the rules, `DOMAIN-SUFFIX` rather than `DOMAIN,`, bypass before parent block, an allowlisted domain absent but explained), and 1 CLI export test against the real command.
  - **Recorded here, closed below:** `privoxy` (valid syntax, no target or recipe) and `bind` (no target or recipe *and* an artifact that cannot load — its master zones point at `/dev/null` in the published file and a nonexistent `null.zone.file` in the core formatter, and BIND refuses a master zone whose file has no SOA) were left as their own piece of work rather than smuggled into this one, and the entry above does that work: `bind` took the design decision it needed (a Response Policy Zone, because the per-domain shape cannot load at all).
- **A Block-Ledger Source Readout, With Its Polling Quota (`@blockingmachine/browser-extension`)**:
  - The per-tab counts in the popup come from two different browser APIs, and they are not equivalent: `onRuleMatchedDebug` reports every match live with its request URL, but only to unpacked builds, while `getMatchedRules` works packed at the cost of batching, withholding the URL, and a hard rate limit. Nothing in the UI said which one was feeding the ledger, so a count looked the same either way — and a polled ledger that had simply burned its quota looked exactly like a page that blocked nothing.
  - New pure `src/shared/ledgerStatus.ts`: `ledgerFeedFromAvailability()` picks the path the browser will actually use (live wins, since it is strictly better), and `formatLedgerQuota()` renders a budget that reads correctly in all three states — a full budget is not "0 used", a partial one names what is left, and an exhausted one names when the next call becomes legal rather than showing a bare zero.
  - New `matchedRulesQuotaView()` in `src/background/matchedRules.ts`, computed from the *same* call log `canRequestMatchedRules` gates on so the number the popup reports is the number that decides the next poll. New `GET_LEDGER_STATUS` message (a synchronous read of in-memory state, so it answers even while the quota is spent), and a presentational `src/popup/LedgerStatusCard.tsx` rendered in the popup's Hub tab — live/polled/off badge, the match source, the remaining quota, and a detail line naming the API and its cost.
  - 23 new tests (13 for the pure status module, 5 for the quota arithmetic, 5 render tests for the card): the feed pick when both paths exist, the badge tone and API naming per path, the three quota phrasings plus the reset duration and a nonsensical negative reading, `describeLedger`'s one-liner, and that the view `agrees with the gate it exists to explain`. The card is render-tested through `react-dom/server`, including that it shows no rows before the first read comes back.
- **A Coverage-Derived Hot Set, Shipped and Proved (`@blockingmachine/core`, `@blockingmachine/cli`)**:
  - The coverage work ended on a measurement with nowhere to go: the report said a real session fires ~70 of 118,000 rules — read that as a *floor* for a session, since the head of the list grows as coverage grows — and then nothing shipped that fact. `npm run build:hotlist` now turns a measurement into a list: it keeps the rules the measurement saw **fire**, plus the exception rules that fired, and writes `packages/cli/filters/output/hotlist.txt` with a header stating exactly what it cost. `npm run check:hotlist` fails if the file drifts from a fresh derivation, and CI runs it after the build.
  - New pure `selectHotList()` / `formatHotList()` in `packages/core/src/coverage.ts`, sharing `minimumRulesForShare` with the coverage curve so "the head that carries 99%" means the same thing in both places. Two properties make the result safe to stand in for a bigger list on the traffic it was measured on: only rules that *won* a request are included (so every blocked host still matches its winner and every untouched host still matches nothing), and a rule is only shipped if the source list actually carries it (so a stale ledger cannot inject one). Exceptions are kept for the same reason — dropping a fired allow rule would make the trimmed list block *more* than the one it replaced.
  - New `replayRuleHits()` in `packages/core/src/ruleReplay.ts`. The CLI's trace report and the hot-set builder now derive their hits from one implementation instead of two: a hot set built by a different loop than the one that measures it is a hot set nobody has checked.
  - `blockingmachine coverage --hot` measures the trimmed list instead of the full export, and the missing-list failure now points at it. It is opt-in on purpose: a report measured against 77 rules answers a different question from one measured against 118,000, and the caller should be the one to ask it.
  - **Proved with the existing measurement.** Against the same 11-page trace the full export blocks **132 requests on 85 hosts** while allowlisting 11 hosts through 6 exception rules; so does the 77-rule hot set, with the *same* rules deciding them — and 100% of its rules firing against 99.941% dead weight in the export. What the trace is, it now says: eleven pages from one machine, content-heavy, recording requests as *attempted* (the sandbox refused connections to several third-party hosts, and a refused connection still counts — which is what a network blocker sees anyway). So both figures are floors rather than constants: a longer session exercises more of the source list, and the generated header states that. (The artifact has since moved: the shipped `hotlist.txt` is now the 17-rule set derived from the committed browser ledger — see the ledger entries above — and on this same trace it keeps 19 of 133 blocks, the held-out number rather than the self-agreement one.)
  - **The limit is measured, not footnoted.** Deriving a hot set from half the session's pages and measuring it on the other half keeps only **38 of 93 blocks (40.9%)**, pinned in the test. A per-session hot set is exact for the traffic it came from and loses most of what it has never seen, so it ships as an opt-in trimmed default for a budget that cannot hold the full list — with the limitation printed in its own header — never as a replacement for the full export. Deriving from a browser-reported ledger aggregated across many sessions was the recorded remedy for that number; the committed four-session ledger has since measured it at **14.3% held-out, flat at every session prefix** — the remedy's direction is now bounded rather than asserted (flag 16 keeps what a *real* multi-day ledger would still add).
  - 13 new tests (9 in core, 4 in the CLI): `selectHotList` (the head it keeps and its order, the share cut and the rule it names, exception retention, and that an absent rule cannot be shipped or counted as covered), `formatHotList` (an inert header that reads back as exactly the selected rules, and determinism), `replayRuleHits` (the hostname/scoped split, one count per winning rule, junk counts, and an empty measurement), plus four CLI proofs against the real list and trace: the shipped artifact *is* the measured winner set, it blocks the same traffic as the export, the page-split hold-out loses what it loses, and `--hot` resolves the trimmed list rather than the export.
- **Element Scan Panel Extracted and Render-Tested (`@blockingmachine/browser-extension`)**:
  - The popup's scan results had no render test at all: the JSX lived inline inside `PopupApp.tsx` behind browser state, and the suite has neither jsdom nor `@testing-library`, so nothing could exercise it. The panel is now a pure component (`src/popup/ElementScanPanel.tsx`) with typed props and its own exported result/group/evidence-row types, leaving `PopupApp` with only the state, the icons and the message plumbing.
  - 8 tests render it with `react-dom/server`'s `renderToStaticMarkup`, which is already available and runs under the suite's Node environment — no jsdom, no new dependency. They cover the idle scan button, the busy/disabled state (`Scanning…`), the group and per-signal evidence markup, the marker per strength (`●`/`○`/`·`), the "Show me where" and "Hide all N" controls, and the `hideCount: 0` case where the hide button must not appear.
  - A static render has no event loop, so these tests assert the rendered wiring and the branch taken rather than firing clicks; they exist to catch a panel that renders the wrong thing, not to replace an interaction harness.
- **Element AI Shows Its Working: Per-Signal Evidence in the Picker HUD and the Popup (`@blockingmachine/core`, `@blockingmachine/browser-extension`)**:
  - Both element-AI surfaces reported a *conclusion* — `Likely ad · 98% · corroborated` — and nothing about what produced it, so a user had no way to tell a verdict that rests on an ad delivery attribute from one that rests on a class word that happens to contain `ad`. The classifier now hands over the evidence that decided it, and both UIs render it.
  - `ElementPrediction` gained `definitiveFamilies` / `supportingFamilies` (the assessment already computed both — it just never left the module), and `ElementEvidenceDetails` gained `adAttribute`, `trackerAttribute` and `adMatchedOn`. The last one exists because the compound-edge rule reports the atom it matched (`ad`), which is the least useful thing to show for the most common real case: `adMatchedOn` carries the identifier that said ad (`ad-slot-container`), so the row reads `Ad markup "ad-slot-container"` instead of `Ad markup "ad"`.
  - New pure `elementAiEvidenceRows()` / `elementAiEvidenceLine()` in `shared/elementAiDisplay.ts`: family id → human label → the matched value → weight. Definitive rows first (`●`, they could act alone), then corroboration (`○`), then — last and dimmed — the analytics attribute the page put on the element, worded as `instrumentation, not a tracker`. Every field of the input is optional and every read defensive, because this runs in a popup that may be talking to a content script from a different build; a payload that reports families without their weight is listed as context rather than guessed at.
  - Picker HUD: an evidence block under the verdict, one row per signal, re-coloured by the verdict's tone, with a legend that appears only when both kinds are on screen. The verdict line itself is unchanged, and the tooltip now carries the reason *and* the signals in full. The block is rebuilt in place and holds no listeners — the HUD's buttons are created once, which is the property the class was rewritten for in the first place.
  - Popup: each scanned group carries its rows over the scan message (`ElementAiGroup.evidence`), rendered under the selector with the same marks and wording. The popup never sees the element, so the evidence travels with the group rather than being re-derived from a second implementation of "what counts as evidence".
  - The test helper had to grow for the HUD to be testable at all: `fakePage` could not dispatch a hover or run `window.setTimeout`, so no existing test had ever executed `createHud`/`refreshHud` — the picker's entire user-facing surface was unverified. It now records `window` listeners (`fireWindow`), installs an `Element` stub so `target instanceof Element` guards work, provides `setTimeout`, and supports `append`/`replaceChildren`/`firstElementChild`. Three tests drive a real hover and read the rendered HUD, and they found a real ordering bug while being written (elements created before the `Element` stub existed, so the guard rejected every hover).
  - 11 new tests: 7 for the display layer (including that an unrecognised family still renders rather than vanishing, and that a strength the payload did not state is not invented), 3 through the picker HUD, 2 for the group contract, plus 2 in core pinning the definitive/supporting split and the reported attribute names.
- **Learned GBDT Classifier + Training Package (M1–M5, `@blockingmachine/core`, `@blockingmachine/electron-app`, `packages/ai-training`)**:
  - A second, *learned* model that generalizes **beyond the filter lists**: Python trains, TypeScript serves. Training, evaluation, export and the promotion ceremony live in the new `packages/ai-training/` (3,411 lines, never shipped); serving is a dependency-free forward pass in `packages/core/src/ai/learned/` — `featureSpec.ts` (generated contract), `featurizer.ts`, `gbdt.ts`, `modelLoader.ts`, `shadowMode.ts`, `manifest.ts`, `sha256.ts`.
  - Trained on **22,932 labeled domains** (AdGuard tracking positives, Majestic Million negatives): held-out average precision **0.9747** against a ≥0.95 target, 112 trees, 366 KB artifact inside the 500 KB budget, **0.032 ms/classify** on-device with no dependencies and no I/O in the hot path. The loader validates format, `feature_version` **and feature order** (a reordered feature list throws rather than silently misrouting every weight), and the model never fails open into blocking — missing or mismatched weights fall back to lists-only mode.
  - **The v1 model does not pass the promotion gate, and that is the gate working.** 11 of 13 checks pass; it fails `precision_at_recall_90` (**0.9278 against the 0.99 design bar**) and `zero_confirmed_breakage` (6 distinct domains it genuinely false-blocks at t=0.92). No threshold was lowered to make it pass. The model therefore ships in **shadow mode only**: `packages/electron-app/src/learnedShadow.ts` scores every Sentinel watchdog sweep and appends disagreements (plus an unbiased ~1% sample for drift monitoring) to `<userData>/learned-shadow.jsonl`. Read-only — it never blocks or quarantines — and fail-soft, so missing or corrupt weights cannot break the watchdog.
  - The golden benign set became a **shipped allowlist** rather than a test fixture: 11 of 67 curated benign domains are lexically indistinguishable from trackers (`fonts.gstatic.com` scores 0.99; same depth, same token hit and similar entropy as `cm.g.doubleclick.net`), so `ai-weights/allowlist.json` is checked **before the model scores**, mirroring the existing `@@` whitelist philosophy. `packages/core/package.json` `files` now includes `ai-weights` so the artifacts ship with the npm package.
  - v2 (lexical + 8 behavioral/graph features) is an **honest negative** and stays experimental: behavioral observations produced no real lift (test AP 0.9745 vs 0.9747), because a tracker pixel and a benign endpoint are identical over plain HTTP. The TS side is v2-ready anyway — version-tolerant loader, `featurizeLearned(domain, obs)`, and NaN routed through each split's `default_left` exactly like the Python trainer.
  - **Two defects found by running the handoff in the real toolchain, which its own verification section flagged as untested:** (1) `packages/core/src/ai/index.ts` did not re-export `verifyLearnedManifest` (nor `ShadowSample`, `LEARNED_FEATURE_NAMES_V2`, `sha256Hex`, `parseLearnedManifest`, or the manifest/observation types), while `learnedShadow.ts` imports `verifyLearnedManifest` from `@blockingmachine/core` — so the Electron app **did not compile**. Confirmed by removing the export and watching `type-check` fail with `TS2724`, then restored. (2) `learned-model.test.ts` imported `LEARNED_FEATURE_VERSION` and never used it; it is now asserted against, so bumping the feature contract without re-exporting the model fails loudly rather than silently misrouting weights.
  - Verified in this toolchain (the handoff could only verify via Node type-stripping, no `npm install`): its 28-test jest suite passes as-is, including the Python parity contract (fixture vectors within 1e-9, end-to-end P(tracker) exact, 14/14 `ip_literal` cases), loader validation, allowlist overrule, manifest tamper detection and shadow sampling. `python3 test_fixtures.py` in the training package confirms the committed sources still reproduce the 23 fixture vectors.
  - The training package is committed as **sources + the frozen feature contract** (`*.py`, README, requirements, `bigram_table.json`, `fixtures.json`, `thresholds*.json`); its label store, observation DB, downloaded filter lists and `model_artifacts*` are gitignored as local build inputs, which is documented in `packages/ai-training/README.md` and its `.gitignore`. The one trained artifact that ships is `packages/core/ai-weights/model.json`, hash-pinned by the promotion ceremony. `npm` ignores the non-package directory cleanly (verified: still 8 workspaces), so `packages/*` needed no change.
- **Element Classifier Weights Fit From the Labelled Corpus (`@blockingmachine/core`)**:
  - The element classifier's statistical head — 4 classes over 25 evidence features — was **hand-written and never measured against an alternative**. It is now fit from the labelled corpus, and the replacement is checkable rather than asserted.
  - New pure module `src/ai/elementWeightFitting.ts`: full-batch Adam on soft-target cross-entropy with a **Gaussian prior centred on the hand-tuned table** (`fitElementWeights`), a deterministic stratified split (`splitElementCorpus`), hyperparameter selection on an **inner split of the training cases** (`selectPriorStrength`), scoring through the shipping path (`weightSetMetrics`, `weightSetActionCalibration`), and an A/B that names the individual cases that changed hands (`compareWeightSets`). No RNG, no clock, no I/O — the shipped numbers are reproducible from the corpus alone.
  - The prior is what makes the problem well posed rather than decorative: there are **104 free parameters and 72 training elements**, and the features are correlated by construction (every "strong token" family also sets its weak partner). Measured on the inner validation split, an **unregularised fit is catastrophic — cross-entropy 1.2517 against 0.0250** for the selected prior strength of 2. The prior is measured in *average-examples*, so "2" reads plainly as "worth two labelled elements".
  - **Held-out proof, on 38 cases the fit never saw** (72/38 deterministic stratified split): accuracy **0.9474 → 0.9737**, cross-entropy **0.4656 → 0.4493**, Brier **0.098 → 0.0797**, with **one case won and zero regressions**. Brier is the one that matters most, being a proper scoring rule on the full label distribution: it cannot be improved by getting more confident without getting more right. The win survives the **entire prior grid** (1–50), so it is not an artifact of the selected hyperparameter — accuracy improves at every point in it.
  - Soft targets come from the corpus's own acceptance rule: `expected` lists every label a case accepts, so the target distribution is uniform over exactly that set. Training on the canonical label alone would teach the model to be wrong on cases the corpus explicitly permits.
  - New `src/ai/elementWeights.generated.ts` (the table plus an `ElementWeightProvenance` record written by the same run) and `scripts/fit-element-weights.mjs` (`--write`, `--check`, wired as `npm run fit:element-weights` / `check:element-weights`). A test re-fits the table in-process and **fails if the checked-in numbers drift from the corpus**, naming the offending weight; a temporary perturbation was run to confirm the gate bites.
  - The hand-tuned table is kept as `ELEMENT_HAND_TUNED_WEIGHTS` — it is the control the fit is measured against *and* the centre of the prior — and `MiniAiElementClassifier` now accepts a `weights` override so a candidate can be measured **end to end through the full decision path**, not only on its own loss.
- **Shipped Tiers Graded Against the Embedded Classifier (`@blockingmachine/browser-extension`)**:
  - The extension ships two independent bodies of knowledge — the hand-curated static tiers and the Mini-AI classifier — and nothing stopped them drifting apart, which surfaces as a user-visible contradiction: the extension blocks a host while the AI Radar it also ships calls that host clean. New `src/__tests__/tierModelAgreement.test.ts` runs every domain in the shipped tier files through the classifier and fails on any disagreement with the tier that ships it.
  - Measured on the 118 curated hosts, the model **never contradicts a tier** — no host is classified into a different blocking family (no ad host called a tracker, no tracker called malware). It instead fails to *place* 46 of them: 12 ad, 13 privacy and 21 annoyance hosts, every one a `Clean` verdict at 74–88% confidence. That is the same weak band the triage cascade escalates, so these are the cascade's documented reason for existing rather than a defect in the list.
  - The confidence ceiling is asserted, not just observed: a `Clean` verdict at or above 90% would be the model *asserting* that a host the extension ships and blocks is definitively clean, which is the one direction a hand-curated list cannot tolerate. Measured ceiling is 88%.
  - `tier_core` is held strictly and absolutely — all 24 hosts recognised, a single gap fails the suite — because it is the tier enabled on a fresh install. `tier_annoyances` is the vocabulary gap in its purest form: 21 of its 22 hosts have no word in the classifier's category set at all, which is asserted as the measurable reason the tier ships disabled.
  - The disagreement set is pinned rather than the assertion loosened, and enforced in both directions: a host added to a tier the model cannot account for fails, and a *stale* pin fails too, so a model that learns a host it used to miss forces the record to be updated instead of rotting. A temporary perturbation was run to confirm the gate bites — adding the recognised `criteo.com` to the pin produced the expected stale-entry failure.
  - A hub compilation written by `npm run package:extension` puts 30,000 hosts in these files, and the model's blind spot covers roughly a third of a real blocklist, so the strict grading is reported as *skipped* (`describe.skip`) rather than passing without having checked anything. In that state the size-independent guarantees are asserted instead: the curated Core hosts must still be recognised (compiling is additive and never re-tiers a curated host), and `tier_core` must still ship nothing the model considers malware or phishing.
  - Wiring: the classifier is run **from source** through a new `@blockingmachine/core/domain-ai` alias in both `jest.config.cjs` and `tsconfig.json`, mirroring the existing `element-ai` mapping so a core change cannot silently diverge from the tiers on disk.
- **Per-Tier Block Attribution (`@blockingmachine/browser-extension`)**:
  - The tier toggles reported how many *rules* each tier shipped, which cannot tell a working tier from a dead one — a 23,523-rule tier that never matched looked the same as a 33-rule tier blocking constantly, leaving the user no basis to drop it. The rule-hit ledger now records which ruleset produced each block so a tier can be judged on the traffic it actually blocked.
  - Attribution is exact, not heuristic: the browser names the `rulesetId` that won a match, each tier ships one ruleset, and that ruleset's declared id *is* the tier id (`tierFromRulesetId`). It works in a packed build too — `getMatchedRules` withholds the request URL but still reports the ruleset.
  - New pure module `src/shared/tierAttribution.ts` (`buildTierBlocking`, `formatTierBlocking`, `tierFromRulesetId`, `TIER_HIT_MIN_SAMPLE`, `TierBlockingVerdict`). Verdicts are `productive`, `idle`, `unobserved`, or `disabled`, with `share` per tier.
  - The restraint is deliberate and tested. A silent tier is reported `unobserved` — never `idle` — until the ledger has seen 50 matches, because a freshly enabled tier or a quiet session is not evidence; an already-disabled tier is never called idle either, since being off is why it has no matches. Acting on a false idle verdict would switch off working blocking. Callers can set their own `minSample`.
  - `RuleHitStats` gained a tier tally alongside the filter histogram, persisted under the new `STORAGE_KEY_TIER_HITS`. The two are independent on purpose: a filter shipped in two tiers collapses into one histogram entry but must stay two tier entries, and a match is credited to its tier even when the rule file could not be read for the filter lookup — so the tier tally is the more complete reading. The tier key is only written once a tier has actually fired, so a ledger that has never seen a static block keeps the exact stored shape older builds read.
  - `clearTier()` forgets one tier, called when a tier is switched *on* (`noteTierTransitions`): matches it did or did not collect while off say nothing about it, and stale counts would let a tier that fired once months ago look productive forever. Re-applying a selection that leaves a tier enabled changes nothing, so applying a capacity plan does not wipe the attribution of every tier it keeps.
  - Popup: each tier row shows what it has blocked and its share of tier blocks; a tier that has been given a real sample and still never fired shows **Never fired** with a one-click **Turn off**. A summary line reports `1,000 blocks from 2 of 4 tiers`, and says plainly when there is not yet enough traffic to tell an idle tier from an untested one. New `.tier-usage`, `.tier-usage.idle`, `.tier-drop` styles.
  - 16 new tests: attribution and restraint in `tierAttribution.test.ts` (sample boundary, disabled-is-never-idle, share arithmetic, junk counts, malformed input), plus ledger coverage for the parallel tally, the unchanged stored shape, tier restore, and `clearTier`.
- **Capacity-Aware Tier Planner (`@blockingmachine/browser-extension`)**:
  - "Enable everything" is only correct while the browser grants the entire static budget, and that is not promised: Chrome's 30,000 static rules is a floor drawn from a global pool shared with every other installed extension, so a congested browser grants less than the extension ships. New pure module `src/shared/tierPlanner.ts` (`planTierSelection`, `formatTierPlan`, `DYNAMIC_RULE_LIMITS`) chooses which tiers earn the slots that remain, maximising total blocking inside the static *and* dynamic budgets.
  - Live capacity is derived from `getAvailableStaticRuleCount()`, which reports what can still be *added* — so capacity is enabled rules **plus** the available figure, not the figure alone. When the browser will not answer, the planner falls back to the guaranteed 30,000 and says so in the explanation (`capacitySource: 'browser' | 'guaranteed'`).
  - Exhaustive subset search rather than a greedy pass by benefit density: the suite pins a case where greedy loses (a 600-rule tier at benefit 500 beats two 500-rule tiers at 490 each — the same ordering bug the packaging compiler's redistribution had). Ties break toward *fewer rules used* so the next compilation keeps headroom, then by catalogue order so the plan is deterministic. Above 16 tiers it degrades to a greedy pass rather than an exponential blow-up.
  - Benefit defaults to coverage (a rule is a host, a host is blocking), but a caller holding measured evidence — the rule-hit ledger — can pass an explicit `benefit` per tier. The bases are never blended: a plan is all-evidence or all-coverage, because a blend would silently make an unmeasured tier look worthless; `benefitSource` reports which was used.
  - Pinned (`keep`) tiers are honoured even when the pins alone exceed capacity, with `overCapacity` surfaced instead of the selection being quietly discarded.
  - `bindingConstraint: 'static' | 'dynamic' | 'none'` and an ordered `explanation[]` of ready-to-render prose.
  - Fixed two prose bugs found by reading real output: a tier larger than the whole budget was told it "needs N more slots alongside the rest", which is nonsense for a tier no combination can fit — now reported as unfixable on its own (`aloneShortfall`) versus an exact shortfall alongside the kept tiers; and the dynamic-budget line asserted "the dynamic budget is the tighter one" even when the static budget was what forced the plan — now conditional, and otherwise says the static budget forced it. Also fixed: a `consider` closure assigning into a nullable object defeated TypeScript's narrowing, and pinned-but-over-budget selections were being dropped.
  - Popup: a **Recommended plan** card in the Static rule tiers section showing the one-line summary, an *Apply* button rendered only when `differsFromCurrent` (reusing the existing `SET_RULESET_TIERS` message), and the tradeoff in prose. New `.tier-plan*` styles.
  - Measured on a congested pool granting 12,033 slots against the 30,000-rule compilation: **6,477 of 30,000 rules fit** — Core shield, Tracking & analytics and Consent & nags stay on, Ad networks (23,523 rules) is dropped as larger than the whole budget on its own, and 5,556 static slots stay free.
  - 19 new tests: exhaustive-vs-greedy, live-capacity derivation, the 30,000 floor acting as a floor rather than a ceiling, pinning over capacity, benefit-basis selection, dynamic-budget reporting and watermark, nonsensical readings, malformed input, determinism, and both explanation branches.
- **Static Tiers Compiled From the Hub Blocklist at Package Time (`scripts/compile-tier-rulesets.mjs`)**:
  - The extension's static tier files held a ~118-rule hand-curated baseline, so a packaged release shipped ~118 static rules while the desktop hub on the same machine had already compiled a six-figure blocklist. `npm run package:extension` now compiles the hub's active output into `rules/tier_*.json` before the bundle is built (`--skip-tiers` ships the baseline instead).
  - New `scripts/compile-tier-rulesets.mjs`: parses every shape the hub emits (hosts, ABP, dnsmasq, Unbound, plain domains), refuses exceptions, cosmetics, scriptlets, DNS-only modifiers, loopback mappings, wildcards, bare labels and public suffixes, then assigns each host to a tier and fills a shared budget.
  - The budget is the whole problem: MV3 guarantees 30,000 static rules across *enabled* rulesets and the hub's list is 122,801 distinct hosts. The compiler caps the four tiers at a 30,000 total — so enabling any combination of tiers still installs, which is the invariant `verify-mv3-compliance.mjs` enforces — and reports what shipped and what overflowed instead of dropping input silently. Measured: 743,634 lines → 122,801 hosts → 30,000 shipped, 92,811 omitted.
  - Fixed during development: redistribution served tiers in catalogue order, pouring every freed slot into the first tier (12,000 cap became 19,903 rules) and starving the other three; it now spreads proportionally. The compiler also refused to write an empty declared tier, which the project's own validator rejects, and refuses to drop a curated host regardless of budget.
  - Curated hosts are read from the files already on disk and keep their tier, so compilation is purely additive; `--input tier_ads=list.txt` attributes a whole list to a tier exactly rather than classifying it; `--residual <tier>` chooses where unclassified hosts land (defaults to an opt-in tier, since most of a merged blocklist matches no vocabulary); `--check` fails a release with stale tiers.
  - Real counts are written to `src/shared/tierCounts.generated.ts` and read through the new `tierRuleCount()`, so the popup's capacity display stays truthful after packaging. Tier and capacity assertions in the test suite were rewritten as invariants rather than frozen baseline numbers — previously `npm run package:extension` would have broken `npm test`.
  - 11 new packaging tests (each compiled tier is validated with the extension's own `validateTierRuleset`) plus the suite verified green in *both* states: the curated baseline and a full 30,000-rule compilation.
- **AI Triage Cascade — Screen Locally, Escalate Only the Undecided (`@blockingmachine/core`, `@blockingmachine/cli`, `@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - `AiDetectorService` chose its engine *exclusively*: `provider: 'mini-ai'` got the free local classifier on everything, `provider: 'openai'` sent everything to the model and discarded the local screening pass entirely. New `cascade` config removes the fork — the embedded classifier always screens first, and `provider` becomes the **escalation backend**.
  - New pure module `packages/core/src/ai/triage.ts`: `assessAmbiguity` (near-tie margin, the classifier's own `suspicious` bucket, single-signal corroboration, conflicting feature contributions, the block/allow confidence band, and a `tentative` signal for clean verdicts below 95% — recognised clean traffic reports 99%), `planTriage` (ambiguity-first ranking with a stakes tiebreak and a total, input-order-independent tiebreak; a budget shared across a whole scan), `mergeTriageVerdict` (a tiebreaker can raise severity, but can never clear a verdict resting on name-based evidence — those are recorded as *contested*), and `summarizeTriage`.
  - Escalation targets ambiguity, not risk: a confidently-malicious host never consumes budget. Over-budget candidates are *deferred* with their local verdict intact, and a failed escalation degrades to the local verdict rather than to a gap.
  - `scanQueryLog` now plans one shared budget across the whole log instead of granting every domain its own allowance, and per-domain budgets are precomputed by that plan so the two paths cannot disagree.
  - Measured on the classifiers' own corpora: **0% escalation** on both the 205-case hostname corpus and the 67-case element corpus (zero added cost where the local model is decisive). On the 6 `list-dependent` hostnames the local screen passes 5 and plain screening catches 0; clean-traffic discovery mode catches **4 of 5**. The limitation is pinned by a test: the cascade escalates uncertainty, not knowledge gaps — `bat.bing.com` is called clean at 99% and no triage rule reaches it.
  - Fixed along the way: the scan cache key omitted `cascade`, so a budget-deferred result could be served for a domain later asked to escalate; `AiProviderConfig` was rebuilt field-by-field in the service constructor, so `cascade` never reached a scan at all; the budget was marked by candidate id, so duplicate ids pushed a plan past its own cap.
  - CLI: `ai-scan --cascade`, `--escalate-clean`, `--max-escalations`, plus a `Triage Cascade` section in the report (action, ambiguity, verdict source, and one line per firing signal). Desktop: a `Triage Cascade` card in Preferences with a model-call budget and a clean-traffic discovery toggle.
  - 51 new core tests (contract, service integration with a mocked transport, and corpus measurement).
- **Unbound DNS as a First-Class Deploy Target (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`, `@blockingmachine/homeassistant-addon`)**:
  - Unbound was already an *export format* but not a *deployable platform*, so its users were left to hand-roll the fetch/reload loop from prose in the Routers & DNS pane. The Deploy Hub now has a dedicated **Unbound DNS** tab, and the Home Assistant add-on serves the same feed.
  - New pure helper `packages/electron-app/src/unboundDeploy.ts` builds the feed URL, the `include:` directive, and the refresh one-liner, and reports *why* the feed cannot work yet — the pane shows that warning instead of advertising a URL that would never resolve, because Unbound has no remote-blocklist feature to point at a non-Unbound export.
  - Setup recipes cover OPNsense/pfSense (`configctl unbound restart`), Debian/Ubuntu/Raspberry Pi OS (`unbound-control reload`), and OpenWrt (`/etc/init.d/unbound reload`), each with its own drop-in path.
  - Add-on: new dependency-free `packages/homeassistant-addon/unboundFeed.js` renders any feed shape (ABP, hosts, dnsmasq, Unbound) into sorted, de-duplicated `local-zone` statements, exposed at `/unbound.conf` (aliases `/unbound.txt`, `/blockingmachine.conf`) with a live zone count in the dashboard and the JSON status payload; `export_format` now accepts `unbound`.
  - Exceptions are dropped rather than translated: a `local-zone` drop-in can only sinkhole, so emitting an allow rule as a block would invert the user's intent. Loopback/`.local`/`.arpa` hosts are refused for the same reason the app refuses them as block targets.
  - 25 new tests (14 desktop, 11 add-on) asserting the exact paste-able text, drop-in paths, format warning, and that every rendered feed line is a comment or a `local-zone` statement.
- **Live Reputation-DB Hot-Patch Pipeline (`@blockingmachine/core`)**:
  - The remote-patch system (commit `962568f`) downloaded patches but never applied them. A new live-list registry now installs merged lists into the exported classifier constants **in place**, so every consumer (`MiniAiClassifier`, `cnameResolver`, `AiDetectorService`, `ruleSynthesizer`) picks up pushed updates on its next call — zero call-site churn.
  - `refreshDb()` now installs patches by default and rebuilds the reputation suffix indexes (`AD_INDEX`, `TRACKER_INDEX`, `SAFE_INDEXES`) after every install; `getDbLists()` serves a cached merged snapshot for hot paths; `getDbRefreshStatus()` exposes install state for UI/diagnostics.
  - Loader hardening: multi-path db resolution for packaged apps (dist copy, monorepo-relative, cwd fallbacks), `setDbDirectory()` / `setDbCacheDirectory()` overrides (writable userData cache for asar installs), non-fatal manifest loading, and never-throwing `loadDb`/`loadDbSync` (degrade to bundled base lists).
  - `normalizeDbList()` lower-cases, de-duplicates, length-caps, and drops non-string entries from every patch list before install (suffix/token matching is case-sensitive against lowercased hostnames).
  - New public exports from `@blockingmachine/core`: `refreshDb`, `getDbLists`, `getDbRefreshStatus`, `BASE_DB_LISTS`, `setDbDirectory`, `setDbCacheDirectory`, `applyPatch`, `normalizeDbList`, plus the `DbLists` / `DbPatch` / `DbManifest` / `LoadedDb` / `DbRefreshStatus` types.
  - `packages/core` build now copies `src/ai/db/*.json` into `dist/ai/db/` and ships them in the npm package (added `src/ai/db` to `files`), so installed packages resolve the manifest.
- **Electron Main-Process Refresh (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - Desktop app calls `refreshDb()` once at startup and hourly thereafter (`dbRefreshTimer`, unref'd, cleared on quit), with the patch cache stored under `userData/reputation-db`.
- **Test Suite (`src/__tests__/db-hotpatch.test.ts`)**:
  - 14 tests covering `applyPatch` merge/removal semantics, list normalization, full HTTP fetch → live-install pipeline against a local server, cache persistence, stale-cache fallback on fetch failure, `disableRemote` mode, and bundled `remote-patch.json` validity.
- **Adaptive Watchdog Cadence from the Radar Heat Map (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - The Sentinel Watchdog now consumes `suggestWatchdogCadence()` end-to-end: the initial arm derives its first interval from the persisted heat map, every sweep (including empty ones, so quiet networks stretch and stale heat decays) records heat and re-applies the suggestion, and the timer re-arms only when the suggested interval actually changes — clearing an unchanged `setInterval` would otherwise restart the full countdown and continuously delay sweeps.
  - All heat producers feed the loop: watchdog sweeps, live-radar polls, and manual `ai-scan-querylog` scans (AdGuard and Pi-hole paths).
  - `set-ai-watchdog-config` invalidates a persisted `adaptiveIntervalMinutes` when the user changes `intervalMinutes`, and `setupAiWatchdogTimer` clamps adaptive values into the cadence function's bounds relative to the current configuration so stale suggestions can't mask a new setting.
- **Cadence State Surfaced in the AI Radar Watchdog Card (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - `applyAdaptiveCadence` now persists the suggestion rationale (`cadenceReason`) and refresh timestamp (`cadenceUpdatedAt`) alongside `adaptiveIntervalMinutes`; the rationale is cleared together with the interval when the base configuration changes.
  - The AI Radar watchdog cardlet shows an `Adaptive · ~45m` / `Fixed · 1h` chip (reusing the heat-chip styling) with the full heat-map rationale beside it, a tooltip containing the reason plus a relative "updated" time, and a 60-second refresh so background-sweep cadence changes surface without leaving the view. The line appears only while the watchdog is enabled.

### Changed
- **The Deploy Hub Tabs Are a Registry, Not Markup (`@blockingmachine/electron-app`)**:
  - The platform strip was nine hand-written `<button>` blocks; the label, icon, id and order all lived in JSX, so renaming or reordering a platform meant editing markup repeatedly, and nothing tied a tab to the pane behind it. The panes were nine `activeTab === '…'` guards threaded through the tree, and the one target that needs live state ran its refresh from inside its own `onClick`.
  - New `src/deploy/deployTargets.tsx`: the targets as data — id, label, a `summary` used as the tab tooltip, an icon, and an optional named `selectEffect` — with `DeployTargetId` **derived from that list**, plus `DEPLOY_TARGETS`, `deployTargetById`, `isDeployTargetId` and `DEFAULT_DEPLOY_TARGET_ID`. Pure data, no `chrome.*` and no state, so the list itself is asserted directly.
  - The Hub renders the strip from the registry and dispatches the active id through `renderDeployPane()`, a `switch` whose `default` assigns the narrowed id to `never`. A new platform is therefore one data entry plus one `case` — and forgetting the case is a build failure instead of a tab that opens onto nothing, because the entry widens the union on its own. The named side effect is resolved through an exhaustive `Record<DeploySelectEffect, () => void>`, so declaring an effect the component has not implemented also fails to build.
  - **Proved by perturbation, not asserted:** adding a single entry to the registry produced exactly one compile error — `src/views/DeployHubView.tsx(2518,15): error TS2322: Type '"probe-target"' is not assignable to type 'never'` — and was then reverted.
  - Scope is honest: this is a structural change, so the panes still close over the Hub's state and were not extracted into separate components. Each one is now a `case` rather than a conditional, which is what makes the next extraction a per-pane move with no dispatch to rewire.
  - 7 new tests: the pinned tab order and id uniqueness, distinct non-empty labels and tooltips, that every icon renders a real `<svg>` rather than an empty frame, that the default opens on a target that exists, that id lookup refuses junk instead of guessing, that the daemon effect sits on the target that needs it, and that no `{activeTab === '…'}` literal is left in the Hub markup while every registry id has a `case`.
- **Coverage Measures the Rules the Browser Can Express, Not an Upper Bound (`@blockingmachine/core`, `@blockingmachine/cli`)**:
  - `blockingmachine coverage` reported its hit rate as `matched / indexedBlockingRules`, and neither half was the set the name implied. The denominator "everything the domain evaluator indexed" silently **included** path-preserving rules — `||cdn.example.com^*/ads.js` is indexed under its host, with the path discarded — and silently **excluded** initiator-scoped rules, because the evaluator's modifier guard skips `$domain=`. So a path rule read as a whole-zone block counted as a hit it had not earned: the rate was an upper bound, and the README called 118,218 rules "hostname-decidable" when they were not.
  - New pure `blockingRuleScope()` / `countRuleScopes()` in `packages/core/src/coverage.ts` classify every blocking network line into the four scopes the DNR compiler actually emits: `hostname` (a blanket host block), `initiator` (`$domain=`, `$from=`, `$to=`, `$third-party` — compiled to `initiatorDomains`), `path` (a kept path, `$path=`, or a regex — compiled to a URL pattern), and `request` (a request type or a response rewrite — compiled to `resourceTypes`). The buckets are exclusive with precedence `initiator`, then `path`, then `request`. Anything that is not a blocking network rule — comments, cosmetic and scriptlet lines, exceptions, `$badfilter`, DNS-only directives — returns `null`, so a list can be counted without pre-filtering it.
  - `isNetworkRule` now also drops AdGuard CSS injection (`#$#`, `#@$#`) and the `$@$` redirect form. They already met the module's own definition of cosmetic ("enforced by content scripts against the DOM, not against a request"); they simply were not recognised, so 4,637 DOM-injection lines were being counted as network rules.
  - The command reports all four counts, states the hit rate over the hostname-decidable bucket alone, and in a replay keeps context-scoped winners out of *both* the numerator and the denominator, reporting them as their own per-scope tallies with the requests they accounted for. The trace block still reports every request that was blocked, so "how much was blocked" and "how much of the decididable list fired" are separate answers rather than one blurred one. In `--hits` mode, where the ledger is browser-reported, the same split is applied to it.
  - Re-measured on the repository's own compiled export (249,751 lines) and the checked-in 11-page browsing trace: **118,379 hostname-decidable · 4,224 initiator-scoped · 7,628 path-scoped · 1,855 request-scoped** among 132,086 blocking rules (plus 4,106 exceptions). The session fires **70 of 118,379** (0.059%) rather than 71 of 118,218 — the rule that moved is a path-scoped one that won on 5 requests with its path never checked — the 95%/99% heads move from 65/70 to **64/69**, and dead weight is **99.941%**. Those figures are a floor, not a universal constant: the head of the list grows as coverage grows, the trace is eleven content-heavy pages from one machine, and it records requests as *attempted* — the sandbox refused several third-party hosts, and a refused connection still counts, which is what a network blocker sees anyway. A longer session would fire more rules.
  - 6 new core tests pin the four scopes, the precedence between them, the `null` cases and the list count, and the CLI suite gained the same split on a fixture, on a ledger, and on the real compiled list — asserting that the rate's denominator *is* the hostname bucket rather than a separately computed number that can drift from it. Two CLI tests that had pinned the old behaviour were rewritten rather than loosened: one is now named for keeping scoped winners out of the rate, the other for why the path rule no longer counts.
- **The Element Classifier's Prior Is Chosen by Cross-Validation, Not One Inner Split (`@blockingmachine/core`)**:
  - `selectPriorStrength` chose the regularisation strength on a *single* inner split of the training cases, so each candidate was fitted on roughly half the data it would finally see and validated on about a quarter. That measures the fit's starvation rather than the prior's value: unregularised looked catastrophic there (inner logLoss **1.18 against 0.34**) and a positive strength therefore always won, while the same unregularised fit generalised *better* than any regularised one once trained on the whole split (held-out logLoss **0.0877 against 0.1796**). The option `innerHoldoutEvery` is replaced by `folds` (default **5**): the training cases are split into deterministic, class-stratified folds, each grid point is scored on the held-out fold of every split, and the winner is refit on all of the training cases.
  - The selection re-ran and moved the shipped strength from **5 to 1**. Mean fold logLoss across the grid: `0` 1.0929, `1` **0.5813**, `2` 0.6028, `5` 0.6535, `10` 0.6600, `20` 0.6828, `50` 0.7020 — unregularised is now clearly the worst candidate on the corrected scale too. Because the data term is a class-balanced mean that sums to exactly one example per case, strength 1 means the hand-tuned centre is worth one labelled element against 77: the corpus outweighs it about **77:1**.
  - The artifact was regenerated (`npm run fit:element-weights`): corpus 117 (77 train / 40 holdout), mean held-out cross-entropy **0.1796 → 0.1443**, held-out accuracy **0.90 → 0.95**, 2 cases won and 0 regressions, end-to-end 117/117 unchanged.
  - The residual gap the old rule hid is recorded rather than tuned away: unregularised still generalises better on the 40 held-out cases (0.0877 against 0.1443), which the ~62-case folds are too small to see. Selecting on the held-out set would make it a fitted quantity, so strength 1 ships and the named fix for the gap is more labelled cases, not a better hyperparameter.
  - One user-visible consequence, caught by the extension suite rather than the corpus: an element the user has personally marked *hide* is labelled `Ad` by the previous table and `Annoyance` by this one. The action and confidence are unchanged (`hide`, 78%) — only the class name moves, because a `user-choice` verdict weights Ad, Tracker and Annoyance identically and the residual evidence decides between them (a hero banner is not really any of the three). The extension test that pinned `Ad` now pins the property it exists for instead — that the tuned verdict travels through the *shared* classifier instance — which also makes it insensitive to future re-fits.
- **Action-Level Calibration Is Scored Only Where the Model Acts (`@blockingmachine/core`)**:
  - `elementActionCalibrationPair` read a `leave` verdict's confidence as `1 − p(acting is right)`, but that number is a class probability — "84% sure this is Content" is not a claim about a counterfactual action. It is why action-ECE floored around 0.10, and why adding six confident leave-only cases moved it from 0.1016 to 0.1228 **for the hand-tuned reference and the fitted head alike**: the scale tracked corpus composition, not the weights.
  - It now returns `null` for a `leave` verdict, so the metric averages over the cases where the model actually acts — **63 of 117** — and the report prints the coverage beside the number (`acting calibration : ECE 0.044   Brier 0.006   (63 of 117 cases acted)`). Restraint, the question the old form was accidentally asking, is measured directly instead: `missedHides`, `undersoldHides` and the action mix (`61 hide, 2 suggest, 54 leave`).
  - On the corrected scale the two heads are close: reference ECE **0.0424**, shipped **0.0444** (delta +0.0020), Brier **0.0059 → 0.0062**. The old `< 0.1` absolute bar could not distinguish "unchanged" from "changed" — the reference sat one ten-thousandth under its own gate and no re-fit could beat it — so the test continues to state the guarantee as a regression bound against the reference, now with a 0.005 margin that reflects the corrected scale and a 0.02 absolute backstop.
  - `weightSetActionCalibration` in the fitting module was updated to match: it filters the `null`s and reports `scored`, so the harness and the shipping evaluation still measure the same quantity with the same code. A candidate judged by a private copy of a metric can pass a bar the product does not actually meet.
- **A Permitted Suggest Is No Longer Scored as an Expected Action (`@blockingmachine/core`)**:
  - The previous correction stopped scoring `leave` verdicts but kept the expectation side of the pair as `entry.maxAction === 'leave' ? 0 : 1` — so ground truth was still "acting was permitted" rather than "acting was required". Over the real corpus the consequence was concrete: 9 of the 63 acting cases were scored `actual: 1` on cases whose `minAction` is unset — including `adblock-wall`, suggested at **95% confidence**, and `newsletter-signup-inline` at 58% — both counted as validated actions purely because the corpus allowed acting, while overconfidence on those same cases would have gone unmeasured entirely.
  - Ground truth about acting now exists only where the corpus states an expectation: **required** (`minAction` set — the label is whether the verdict sits inside the required band, so an undersold hide is a calibration error rather than a validated action) or **forbidden** (`maxAction: 'leave'` — any verdict the model takes labels 0, which is the only place overconfidence can show). The 27 permitted-only cases state nothing, so they are excluded: scoring them 1 would claim the corpus validated acting, scoring them 0 would claim it forbade acting, and either reading lets the metric be gamed by acting on exactly the cases nobody labelled. Re-measured: the metric averages **54 of 117** cases (the must-hide set the model acts on), reference ECE **0.0424 → 0.0346**, shipped **0.0444 → 0.0369**, Brier **0.0059/0.0062 → 0.0030/0.0033**. Safety and accuracy are unchanged — 117/117, zero destroyed, zero missed — because the metric is evaluation-only and moves no decision.
  - The fitted artifact was regenerated under the corrected scale (`npm run fit:element-weights`); only the recorded calibration provenance moved — same 2 wins / 0 regressions, held-out accuracy 0.95, cross-entropy 0.1443 — because the head's loss never depended on the action metric. The bounds in `element-ai-evaluation.test.ts` are relative to the reference, so they held without moving; the corpus-level rule (an acted case scores exactly when the corpus stated an expectation) is pinned by test, as is the scoring of an undersold hide as 0.

### Fixed
- **`--json` Promised a Document and the Logger Delivered a Log Line (`@blockingmachine/cli`)**:
  - The flag exists so a caller can pipe a command's result into `jq`, and the payloads that went through `logger.info` could not be parsed. The winston console transport prefixes a timestamp and a colourised level, and `colorize({ all: true })` wraps the entire message in ANSI escapes, so `JSON.parse` of the output failed on the *timestamp* rather than on the payload — `{` was never the first byte, and no consumer could work around it. Nothing caught it because the suite asserted on the returned `res.data` — the object the caller receives — and never on the bytes stdout actually carried, which is the only place a machine consumer reads them.
  - New `writeJson` in `lib/logger.ts` writes the payload to stdout untouched, deliberately leaving the human formatter and the file transports behind: `tier-plan --json` and `validate --verbose` now use it. The bootstrap had the same defect for the same reason — dotenv's loader banner goes to stdout before any command gets to decide whether the run is machine-readable — so `dotenv.config({ quiet: true })` silences it. Verified against the built CLI: `node dist/cli.js tier-plan --rules-dir packages/browser-extension/rules --json` exits 0 with a document whose first byte is `{`, no ANSI anywhere, and the same plan the caller receives; the other `--json` paths (coverage, ai-crawl, ai-scan) already wrote through `console.log` and were never affected.
  - The `tier-plan` json test now captures `process.stdout.write` and parses *that* rather than `res.data`, so a regression fails in the suite instead of in a consumer's script — which is the shape the test needed from the start (the suite stays at 64 tests; one was rewritten rather than added).
- **A Mistyped `--residual` Quietly Filed Unclassified Hosts in the Default-On Tier (`scripts`)**:
  - `--residual <tier>` chooses where every host whose name matches no vocabulary lands, and its default is deliberately an *opt-in* tier — `tier_ads` — because most of a merged blocklist matches nothing and `tier_core` ships enabled on a fresh install, so parking those hosts there would silently change what every user blocks. The parser then contradicted its own comment: an unrecognised name kept the default, and a later guard (`if (!TIER_IDS.includes(options.residual)) options.residual = 'tier_core'`) overwrote it, so `--residual tier_adz` filed thousands of unclassified hosts into exactly the tier the comment says they must not reach.
  - The flag now refuses the way `--hits`, `--attribution` and `--security` already do: `--residual is not a tier: "tier_adz". Name one of tier_core, tier_ads, tier_privacy, tier_annoyances, tier_security (the tier_ prefix is optional)`, one line, exit 1, and no file is written because the failure precedes the plan. A residual is a statement about where the classifier's leftovers go, and answering it with a tier nobody named is the one outcome that must not happen. `--residual core` still normalises to `tier_core` for the operator who *means* it, and the report names the tier it used on every run.
  - 3 new integration tests (extension 463, up from 460) run the compiler: the opt-in default is named in the report and the host lands there, the short `core` form places the host in `tier_core` as asked, and a typo plus a missing value both exit 1 with the curated baseline untouched.
  - CI now runs `npm run check:vocabulary` in the "Verify generated artifacts" step, so the derived vocabulary is re-derived from the shipped rulesets on every push instead of only on a machine where somebody remembered to run it.
- **The Extension Trusted Its Own Storage Where the Browser Was the One Holding the State (`@blockingmachine/browser-extension`)**:
  - An audit of everything the extension persists, against the browser state each one mirrors. The enabled static tiers were already reconciled — the browser is read on every worker start and repaired to the saved selection, with a suite that pins both directions — and so were the context menus, the per-tab telemetry, the hit ledger and the measurements that come from `getMatchedRules`. Two surfaces were not, and both failed silently in the direction that matters: the extension believed itself.
  - **The worst one is a blocking loss, and it needed no unusual conditions.** The compiled blocklist lived only in a module-level variable, and `applyCurrentRules` rebuilt the *entire* dynamic rule set from it on every local decision. A Manifest V3 service worker is torn down constantly, so the first site pause, domain allowance or custom rule after any restart ran with an empty list — and rebuilding from an empty list is not a no-op, it is `removeRuleIds: <everything>`. **Pausing one site therefore deleted every blocklist rule the extension had installed**, while the popup kept reporting the shield as *Active*, because the popup reads the decisions out of storage and they were still there. The same rebuild ran for the global-pause path, for the SSE remote-control path and for all three context-menu decisions.
  - **The fix is that the browser is never asked to forget something the extension cannot replace.** Dynamic rules are now split into two families by priority — the user's own decisions at `PRIORITY_USER_ALLOW` and above, everything a compiled list produced below it, which is the same boundary that makes an explicit decision outrank the list — and `updateDynamicRules` reads `getDynamicRules()` before it removes anything. A call that has no list to install now replaces only the user's decisions and leaves the installed list rules exactly as the browser holds them; the rules from the last successful compilation *are* the list as far as the browser is concerned, since the planner that produced them is the same pure function. The rules this call's own lines supersede are still retired (compared on the filter, so a stale scoped variant goes too) and new ids are allocated above the highest id kept, so a preserved rule cannot be overwritten. Kept rules are counted against the quota before the new plan is bounded, because preserving 400 rules and then planning a full budget of new ones is how a rule set grows past a watermark it thought it was inside.
  - **The startup reconcile that should have existed all along.** The tiers were reconciled on every worker start while the rules the user's decisions produce were only ever installed at the moment the decision was made — so a decision whose apply threw, or whose worker died, stayed in storage and never reached the browser. `reconcileDynamicRules` now reads the browser, keeps the count it reports (so the popup's fallback figure is a reading rather than this worker's memory) and installs only on a difference, comparing **both** directions: a decision with no rule behind it is a pause that is not pausing, and a rule with no decision behind it is blocking the user asked to allow. Both halves of what the user asked for are checked, because they arrive in different families — a rule saved by hand is compiled by the list planner and lands with the blocklist — and the hand-written half is matched on the filter the planner produced, deliberately: a stricter comparison could never be satisfied for a line the planner merges, and a check that can never be satisfied is a repair loop rather than a reconcile. An empty list family triggers a fetch rather than a remember (a multi-megabyte list duplicated into storage is the alternative), which is self-limiting because a successful sync leaves it non-empty; `null` rather than an empty pair is returned when the browser will not answer, since "no rules" and "no reading" lead to opposite decisions.
  - **The periodic work was scheduled once and never checked.** Both alarms were created inside `chrome.runtime.onInstalled` and never read back. Chrome's own documentation says alarms can be cleared by a browser restart or an update and that an extension should therefore re-assert its schedule, and there was no `onStartup` listener at all — so a profile that had ever lost them stopped syncing rules and stopped pushing telemetry, permanently and silently, since nothing in the pipeline distinguishes "quiet" from "never scheduled". New `src/background/alarmSchedule.ts` states the schedule once and reconciles it against `chrome.alarms.getAll()` on every worker start, on `onStartup` and on `onInstalled`, re-creating only the alarms that are absent, one-shot or on the wrong period and issuing no call at all when the schedule is already right. A missing or unreadable alarm list does nothing rather than clearing alarms that may be correct.
  - **One small honesty fix on the same card.** "Last rule sync" was also in memory, so it read *never* beside a rule count that was plainly not zero. It is now persisted under `bm_last_sync_at`: the rules themselves are read back from the browser rather than stored, because the browser already holds them, but a timestamp has no browser-side equivalent.
  - 37 new tests (extension 460, up from 423): 14 for the alarm schedule — what counts as missing (absent, one-shot, wrong period), that names the extension does not own are ignored in both directions, that junk in the browser's list is not trusted, and that confirming a correct schedule costs no call — and 23 for the dynamic rules, including the round trip through the browser's own shape, the family split, the preserving call that leaves the blocklist installed, the custom rule that would otherwise be installed twice, id allocation above the kept rules, the budget that counts them, the global pause that still clears both families, and the two absences that must *not* read as failures (a saved rule the user has allowed the domain of, and any rule at all while paused). The `chrome.alarms.getAll` and `chrome.runtime.onStartup` the module needs were added to the hand-written typings, which until now declared only `create` and `onAlarm`.
- **The Tier Block-Only Rule Was Asserted in the Suite but Not at the Release Gate (`@blockingmachine/browser-extension`)**:
  - `PRIORITY_STATIC_TIER` and `validateTierRuleset` have always required every tier rule to be a bottom-priority `block`, and a test walks the shipped tier files for both. The MV3 compliance script checked the priority but only that an action *existed*, never that it was a block — so the release gate and the suite were enforcing two different rules while the docs described one.
  - That gap was not cosmetic. Priorities are compared across every match, not just within a band, so a static `allow` at priority 2 beats a dynamic block at 1: a hand-edited or miscompiled tier could have shipped a ruleset that outranks the extension's own blocking, which is the exact failure the bottom band exists to prevent. The script now rejects any tier action that is not `"block"`, naming the reason, and its `STATIC_TIER_PRIORITY` comment spells out the cross-band comparison that makes block-only load-bearing rather than stylistic.
  - A new test pins the two implementations together, since neither imports the other: it asserts the compliance script still checks `action.type !== 'block'` and still declares `STATIC_TIER_PRIORITY` equal to `PRIORITY_STATIC_TIER`, so changing the module's band without changing the gate (or dropping the action check) fails instead of silently weakening a release.
- **Element Classifier Wrong on Five Live Pages: Five Defects, 33 Phantom Hides (`@blockingmachine/core`)**:
  - The element corpus is synthetic, so it only encodes the shapes someone thought of. The shipping scanner was run over **five real pages** (MDN, the Guardian front page, github.com, nytimes.com, Wikipedia) through the real collection path — the extension's own `isCandidateElement`/`snapshotElement`, compiled with the project's `tsc`, with only raw DOM facts read in the page and every decision made by the shipped `MiniAiElementClassifier`. Of 1,019 classifications, **109 were actionable and 52 were wrong**, 33 of them outright `hide` verdicts on things no one wants hidden. Full write-up in `docs/element-classifier-live-scan.md`.
  - **Destructive, and the single largest cause: a zero-area box was read as a 1×1 beacon.** `isPixel` was `width <= 4 && height <= 4` on *any* tag, and an unrendered `<script>`, a lazy photo that has not laid out and a collapsed ad slot all measure 0×0. That produced `Tracker/hide 98%` on MDN's own bundles, the Guardian's own asset host and its editorial photos, GitHub's bundles, NYT's `dd.nytimes.com` tags and **Wikipedia's own logo** — 33 verdicts across four of the five sites. Pixel geometry now requires a rendered box (`area > 0`) **and** a tag that can own one, and `details.pixelBox` carries the measured box so the reason string can no longer say "1×1" about a 0×0 element.
  - **A path-relative URL was parsed as a hostname.** `hostOfUrl` prefixed `https://` and stripped the leading slash, so `/static/client/runtime.js` resolved to the domain **`static`** and `/w/load.php` to **`w`**. Those invented hosts then read as "remote, not a CDN" and turned first-party assets into beacons. Relative references now yield no host; protocol-relative `//cdn.example/a.js` still resolves, because it does name one.
  - **A React-generated id named an ad.** GitHub's account menu carries `id="_R_ad_"`, whose fragments satisfied the compound-edge rule, so a working menu was **hidden at 98%**. Ids matching `GENERATED_ID_LIKE` (`_R_*`, `:r3:`, `radix-*`, `mui-*`, `headlessui-*`, `downshift-*`) now contribute no vocabulary. A first attempt also required both halves of a compound to be words, which broke the pinned `in-article-ad` case and was removed: the generated-id filter is the fix with direct evidence, and the extra net was an over-generalisation from one example.
  - **An analytics attribute was as strong as the word "tracker".** The prior rated `dataTrackerAttribute` at `+12` for Tracker (against `+13` for `trackerStrongToken`) and `−8` for Content, and modern pages put a `data-analytics-*`/`data-track-*` attribute on nearly every link — on github.com it was the entire difference between leaving a footer profile link alone and **suggesting it be blocked as a Tracker** (7 of the page's 14 verdicts). The attribute now counts only on an element that is already tracker-flavoured (tracking vocabulary, a measurement host, beacon or frame shape), and the prior rates it with `trackerWeakToken`. Corpus support was added and the weights refitted; a guard case keeps a real 1×1 beacon *with* an analytics attribute at `Tracker/hide`.
  - The fifth defect was a symptom, not a separate bug: the Guardian's `.top-fronts-banner-ad-container` was reported as **Tracker** on a rectangle whose own class says `ad`, because the bogus `pixel-shape` family is definitive and maps to Tracker, outranking the weak ad vocabulary actually present. It falls out of the first fix.
  - Six cases from these pages were added to the labelled corpus (GitHub footer link, `_R_ad_` menu, GitHub's own bundle, MDN's relative-src script, Wikipedia's logo, a lazy Guardian photo) plus the beacon guard, so the fixes are pinned by the corpus rather than by a reading of it.
